#2975. Philadelphia 76ers Make Tyrese Maxey Announcement Before Bucks Game

The Philadelphia 76ers have made an official announcement regarding Tyrese Maxey's availability for Saturday's game against the Milwaukee Bucks.

Maxey is listed as questionable with a right foot sprain on the NBA's official injury report released Friday, November 20, leaving his status uncertain for Philadelphia's 1 p.m. ET matchup against Milwaukee.

The designation comes after Maxey missed Wednesday's 121-116 victory over the Orlando Magic because of right foot soreness. While the latest report provides a more specific injury description, there was also encouraging news about his recovery Friday.

Maxey participated fully in the Sixers' lighter practice session, according to reporting from Philadelphia's local media. That gives the team reason for optimism, although his availability remains a game-time question.

Maxey has not been ruled out of Saturday's game, but the Sixers have yet to officially clear him to play.

His questionable designation follows an unusual development Wednesday, when the star guard experienced right foot soreness during pregame workouts ahead of Philadelphia's matchup with Orlando.

According to NBC Sports Philadelphia, Maxey was a late addition to the injury report before ultimately being sidelined.

Adam Aaronson of PhillyVoice reported that Maxey participated without restrictions in Friday's practice, although the session was described as relatively light.

The Athletic's Tony Jones also reported that Maxey was trending toward playing Saturday, according to an earlier Heavy on Sixers report.

Still, Philadelphia's decision to list him as questionable indicates that his return is not guaranteed. His final status will depend on how the injury responds ahead of Saturday's tipoff.

Maxey has been one of Philadelphia's most productive players this season, averaging 26.5 points, 5.8 assists and 3.3 rebounds across his first 15 appearances.

His return would be particularly valuable with another key Philadelphia scorer already ruled out against Milwaukee.

Maxey isn't the only significant name on Philadelphia's latest injury report.

Jaylen Brown has been ruled out with a left hamstring strain, meaning the Sixers will be without one of their leading scorers regardless of Maxey's availability.

Brown suffered the injury during Philadelphia's November 16 victory over the Brooklyn Nets. He participated in individual work Friday but has not been cleared to return.

The Sixers will also be without Dean Wade, who is dealing with left hamstring tightness and soreness, while Adem Bona remains sidelined with a cervical spine sprain. Ariel Hukporti is also out with a right Achilles tendon injury.

There is some encouraging news in the frontcourt, however.

Dominick Barlow is listed as probable with a low back stress reaction, putting him in position to make his season debut against Milwaukee.

Liberty Ballers reported that Barlow is trending toward returning after missing Philadelphia's first 16 games of the season.

The Sixers have managed to keep winning despite their recent injuries, including Wednesday's comeback victory in Orlando, when they overcame a 17-point deficit.

But Brown's absence makes Maxey's status especially significant. His presence would restore another established scoring option alongside Joel Embiid, LeBron James and V.J. Edgecombe.

Philadelphia will have another opportunity to evaluate Maxey's right foot before Saturday's early afternoon tipoff.

#2974. Some FHA borrowers pay mortgage insurance for a decade or more. There's an industry push to change the rules

Homeowners with conventional loans who pay mortgage insurance typically can shed that expense — meant to protect a lender against loss if the borrower defaults — once they've built sufficient equity in their home. But that's not how it works for those with a Federal Housing Administration loan.

A fresh push to change the rules governing mortgage insurance for FHA loans came this week, after the Department of Housing and Urban Development released a report to Congress on Monday showing that the FHA's mortgage insurance fund remains financially strong. Its capital ratio — a measure of its financial cushion against potential losses — was 10.29% as of Sept. 30, down from 11.47% a year earlier, but still more than five times the 2% minimum required by Congress.

"The program's financial strength provides an opportunity for targeted changes that improve affordability for borrowers while preserving the long-term stability" of FHA's insurance fund, said Bob Broeksmit, president and CEO of the Mortgage Bankers Association, in a statement released after the report was issued. The trade group was one of a handful of organizations that put out statements calling for changes.

The push, which revives a longstanding mortgage industry request, also comes several weeks after President Donald Trump issued an executive order that included calling for the launch of a federal pilot program that would cancel mortgage insurance on certain conventional loans once borrowers meet equity requirements.

The order would apply generally to future loans backed by Fannie Mae and Freddie Mac — two government-sponsored enterprises that purchase qualifying mortgages from lenders and resell them as bundled securities. It wouldn't apply to FHA loans.

FHA mortgages are issued by private lenders but insured by the federal government, which protects lenders against losses if a borrower defaults. The loans are designed to make homeownership more accessible, particularly for borrowers with limited savings for a down payment or with lower credit scores.

Affordability challenges persist in the housing market, with elevated home prices and mortgage rates putting homeownership out of reach for many prospective buyers. Existing homeowners are also facing rising costs alongside their mortgages, including property taxes and homeowners insurance amid overall high inflation.

About 83% of new FHA purchase loans went to first-time homebuyers in the most recent fiscal year, according to the FHA report to Congress. Altogether, the FHA insured more than 8.4 million mortgages as of Sept. 30.

The median age of first-time buyers is 40, according to a new report from the National Association of Realtors.

FHA loans also require borrowers to pay mortgage insurance regardless of the size of their down payment. Under current rules established in 2013, FHA borrowers with a down payment that's less than 10% of the loan must pay mortgage insurance premiums for the duration of the loan. Those who put 10% or more down pay that insurance for 11 years.

Borrowers generally pay both an upfront mortgage insurance premium and an annual premium, which is typically divided into monthly payments. The upfront premium is usually 1.75% of the base loan amount and can be financed into the mortgage. For many borrowers with 30-year FHA loans, the annual premium is 0.55% of the outstanding loan balance.

Conventional mortgages generally don't require mortgage insurance unless the loan is for more than 80% of the home's value. In 2022, the premium typically ranged from 0.58% to 1.86% annually, according to Fannie Mae.

If those borrowers have to pay mortgage insurance, they can typically request its removal once they've built 20% equity in the home, or it is automatically canceled when their loan drops to 78% of the original value.

The mortgage industry has floated two different proposals.

One would reduce the rate that applies to FHA mortgage insurance. For example, the Mortgage Bankers Association recently suggested a rate reduction of 20 basis points, which would lower the standard 0.55% rate to 0.35% for new borrowers.

For example: On a $350,000 mortgage, a 0.55% rate generally translates into $1,925 annually, or about $160 monthly, at the start of the loan. As the principal owed slowly goes down over time through a borrower's monthly payments, so does that mortgage insurance amount.

At 0.35%, the annual premium would be $1,225 at the start of the loan, or $102 monthly — a difference of $58 per month.

The FHA last cut that rate in 2023, when it was reduced from 0.85%. At the time, HUD estimated that affected borrowers would save an average of $800 annually. However, in the FHA's new report to Congress, the agency noted that the 2023 cut has reduced the fund's revenue, contributing to a decline in its capital ratio.

The other industry proposal would implement auto-cancellation of premiums once the loan balance fell to 78% of the home's original value at purchase — in other words, the same as for Fannie- and Freddie-backed mortgages.

"Cancellation prevents the squeeze for borrowers who have paid down their loans and shouldn't be paying for coverage they have outgrown," said Michael Farrell, president of the National Association of Mortgage Brokers, a trade group that generally has supported the cancellation proposal.

"NAMB believes that cancellation at 78% protects the [FHA insurance fund], because premiums can restart if the [capital] ratio ever falls to 2%," Farrell said.

FHA mortgages received that treatment from 2001 through 2013, but the agency eliminated it to shore up its insurance fund after financial reserves deteriorated over time following the housing market crash, which led to the 2008 financial crisis.

Whether the proposals will go anywhere this time is uncertain. HUD, which oversees the FHA, did not respond to a CNBC email seeking more information.

#2971. F1 Las Vegas GP: Kimi Antonelli quickest but Max Verstappen on pole, Ferraris out in Q2

Max Verstappen will start on pole at the Formula 1 Las Vegas Grand Prix despite Kimi Antonelli going fastest - while Charles Leclerc and Lewis Hamilton both failed to make Q3.

The 2026 world champion beat the Red Bull driver by 0.258s, but will take a five-place grid drop due to a new internal combustion engine on his Mercedes.

It came after Verstappen took provisional pole with a 1m34.151s while Antonelli was down in sixth, but the Dutchman failed to improve on his final lap, allowing others the chance to beat him.

Yet only Antonelli managed to topple Verstappen, setting a 1m33.893s at the chequered flag - the eighth time he has gone quickest in qualifying this year and the first since Austin.

But because of the grid penalty, Verstappen will be joined on the front row by Mercedes' George Russell, who hoped for a tow from his team-mate but traffic denied that opportunity.

Russell set a 1m34.224s, two-tenths quicker than his opening lap, and was just 0.020s ahead of McLaren's Lando Norris, who will therefore complete the top three on Saturday night's grid.

Verstappen's team-mate Isack Hadjar was fifth quickest in Q3, 0.699s off the pace, with sixth going to impressive rookie Arivd Lindblad a week after Racing Bulls confirmed him for 2027.

Oscar Piastri took seventh, but is one of five drivers set for a grid penalty, his being 10 places for an engine change, while eighth went to Lindblad's team-mate Liam Lawson.

Alpine duo Pierre Gasly and Franco Colapinto respectively completed the top 10, 1.212s and 1.421s off Antonelli, but the biggest talking point came in the second session as Ferrari suffered its first double Q2 exit since Imola last year.

The Italian outfit occupied row six after what was a messy Q2, highlighted by Leclerc aborting a lap midway through due to a Turn 7 lock-up.

The Monegasque subsequently failed to make the line to start a lap at the end, eventually dropping to 11th ahead of 12th-placed Hamilton, who failed to improve on his final tour.

"Unbelievable," reacted Leclerc, whose team-mate would have knocked out Antonelli had he improved at the chequered flag - the Italian surviving the drop by 0.111s.

Three-tenths behind Hamilton was Audi's Nico Hulkenberg in 13th, with 14th going to Williams man Carlos Sainz, who is set to serve a 10-place drop for a new turbocharger.

Hulkenberg's team-mate Gabriel Bortoleto will jump up from 15th, while 16th went to Alex Albon for Williams' first double Q2 appearance of the 2026 season.

Rafael Camara, who suffered a spin at the opening corner early on, looked set to profit from a significant tow by team-mate Oliver Bearman until Bortoleto dropped him into the Q1 elimination zone at the chequered flag.

The new Haas recruit missed out on Q2 by just 0.071s to take 17th, with a 1.163s gap to Cadillac's Sergio Perez in 18th and a 1.180s advantage over 19th-placed Fernando Alonso.

F1 2026 qualifying head-to-head: Las Vegas GP

Alonso's Aston Martin stablemate Lance Stroll took 20th, but is set to serve a 10-place penalty for a new energy store and new control electronics being fitted on his AMR26.

That will therefore bump up Valtteri Bottas from 21st, who was slowest in the Q1 representative times just two days after Cadillac announced the 10-time grand prix winner for 2027.

Bearman was therefore the only driver not to complete a proper lap, as his 35-place grid drop for a complete engine change meant his main role in Q1 was to assist Camara.

#2970. Junk bonds are 'flashing yellow.' Watch these warning signs

Cracks are forming in the junk bond market as investors demand higher payouts for owning the market's riskiest debt. It isn't time to ditch high-yield bonds, but investors should pay attention to the warning signs.

High-yield bonds now yield 8.1%, up from 7.22% a month ago. The increase reflects a jump in yields across the curve as investors bake in more inflation from high energy prices and other pressures, including concern about the deficit — hitting nearly $2 trillion in the fiscal year that ended Sept. 30.

The high-yield market is also showing stress on the credit side with spreads recently widening to levels not seen since April, according to the Federal Reserve Bank of St. Louis. Credit spreads are the difference in yield between the bonds and Treasurys of similar maturities. Wider spreads mean investors are demanding higher yields for holding corporate debt, viewing it as riskier.

Spreads are at 315 basis points in the overall high-yield market, higher than a year ago but still below levels in March when they reached 346 bps. One basis point equals one one-hundredth of a percent, or 0.01%.

The high-yield market consists of bonds rated BB+ by S&P and Fitch and Ba1 and under by Moody's. The lowest-rated cohort, CCC and below, has seen the most movement with spreads climbing dramatically over the past year to roughly 1,250 bps.

Right now, the high-yield market is "flashing yellow" but is "far from red," said Michael Arone, chief investment strategist at State Street Investment Management.

It makes sense that investors are demanding more compensation for taking on additional credit risk as borrowing costs rise, he said.

Yields are elevated across the board with the 10-year Treasury reaching its highest level since 2002 earlier in the week.

"The bigger question is whether this is simply a repricing of interest rate risk, or the beginning of a more fundamental reassessment of credit quality," Arone said.

He's in a wait-and-see mode since earnings are still growing, interest-coverage ratios remain good, and while default rates have ticked up some, he believes it is not concerning.

That said, the starting point in spreads is likely weighing on investors' psyche, since they are still low by historical standards.

"There's a small margin of error here, which I also think raises the anxiety level," Arone explained. "The compensation that investors are receiving for taking on this credit risk isn't overwhelming relative to history, and therefore subtle changes in credit spreads can be concerning."

While there may be concerns about part of the lower-rated market, the overall high-yield market is in good fundamental shape.

In fact, credit quality is at a record high, with BB bonds making up over 60% of the market compared with 38% prior to the global financial crisis, said Kelley Gerrity, a fixed income strategist at Morgan Stanley Investment Management.

"We've had higher-quality companies coming in, and with higher rates now, you also have more discipline from companies that are more indebted ... just because of the higher cost of capital, so that actually is creating a bit of a healthier picture as well," she said.

The lowest tier of high yield has always been more speculative, since the bonds have a higher default risk. Therefore, it's no surprise that they are the ones seeing the most widening in spreads, said Collin Martin, head of fixed income research and strategy at the Schwab Center for Financial Research.

The overall move in high-yield spreads have been "orderly," he said. "Are there cracks forming? I think the cracks are logical cracks with the lowest-rated bonds and it's too early to say that it's spreading to the broad credit market."

In addition, fluctuations within the CCC-rated and lower spreads have been idiosyncratic, Gerrity added. Morgan Stanley recently broke down the spreads in the lowest cohort into two buckets: performing assets and non-performing assets, which are defined as spreads above 1,000 bps.

The non-performing spread to worst is 2,818 bps, while the performing bucket — the biggest piece of the CCC market — is 461 bps, she said.

"It is isolated," she said. "I'm not necessarily sure that that will go away anytime soon, but it doesn't tell the story of overall concern and flashing caution for credit markets right now."

For now, investors should remain selective within higher yield, Gerrity advised.

"We're looking for the best relative value opportunities, and we think that exists within that single-B cohort, and so we're leaning into that," she said.

Investors should be concerned if there is a steep widening in the broader high-yield market.

But there's scant evidence of stress in the BB cohort. Spreads in that group are at 194 bps, up from 179 bps a year ago — although the move hasn't been in a straight line.

"We're going to be focusing our attention on what are considered some stronger businesses," Martin said. "If we start to see the markets demand higher spreads there also, that's what we'll be looking at to see if risks are really rising."

It's also important to understand that the Federal Reserve is raising rates in a strong economic environment, said R.J. Gallo, chief investment officer of global fixed income at Federated Hermes. The central bank is concerned about an inflation problem amid high fossil fuel prices due to the Iran war, while growth has been stronger than expected, he noted.

"With growth being good as the reason that the Fed is hiking, then you wouldn't expect high yield to blow out because growth means revenues stay up, means cash flow and profitability stays up," he explained.

High yield becomes a disaster when the economy is heading into a sharp economic downturn, Gallo said.

"That's when spreads really widen out," he said. "But a recession is not the odds-on bet. Now, talk to me in six months if the Fed keeps hiking over and over, if oil prices stay high for longer, well, then maybe we will start to wonder."

#2968. White House replaces cyber director Sean Cairncross amid widespread tech outages

White House National Cyber Director Sean Cairncross is leaving his post, with Pentagon CIO Kirsten Davies set to serve as acting director after a week of high-profile U.S. technology outages.

The role, which advises the president on cybersecurity and coordinates U.S. cyber policy across federal agencies, has been handed to Davies, the Pentagon's chief information officer, on an interim basis. A permanent national cyber director must first be nominated by the president and then confirmed by the Senate.

Axios first reported Cairncross' exit, citing two White House officials who said President Donald Trump's chief of staff, Stephen Miller, pushed for his departure.

The White House declined to comment. However, a source familiar with the White House's thinking told CNBC that the President fully supported firing Sean Cairncross from his position because he lacked the strong cyber credentials to perform the job. Davies has the credentials to excel in the position and has been Senate-confirmed, the person added.

The Office of the National Cyber Director did not immediately respond to a CNBC request for comment.

It comes after a series of technology outages, including a nationwide aviation ground stop, a worldwide Microsoft Outlook outage and a reported blackout of the National Security Agency's classified communications network.

The White House has insisted that there are no indications of a coordinated cyberattack, state-sponsored activity or malign actor behind recent tech outages.

Davies addressed her responsibilities in the new position and thanked Miller and Trump for their confidence late Thursday.

"Our nation stands in the crossroad of analog, digital, and quantum technologies, our American way of living increasingly interconnected. Our opportunity is to dominate the cyber landscape for generations to come … but only if we modernize and drive resilience into the foundations our great nation runs on," she wrote on X.

Secretary of Defense Pete Hegseth and Secretary of State Marco Rubio both congratulated Davies on her appointment.

#2967. Devon Dampier, No. 2 Utes on cusp of something historic

There's a lot on the line for the University of Utah in its trip to Fort Worth.

Not only would a win keep an undefeated season alive, but it would clinch No. 2 Utah's first-ever berth in the Big 12 championship game.

The Utes can punch their ticket to the last-ever Big 12 title game by beating TCU or West Virginia. A BYU loss at Kansas would also secure the Utes' place, regardless of Utah's Saturday outcome.

After getting through the Houston-Cincinnati-BYU-Arizona gauntlet unscathed, Utah has a real shot at finishing undefeated in the regular season for the first time since 2008.

Standing in its way are a pair of 5-5 teams — TCU (3-4 in Big 12 play) and West Virginia (2-5 in Big 12 play).

ESPN's matchup predictor gives the Utes a 73.5% chance of winning in Fort Worth, and Utah is favored by 8.5 points on the road.

It's Utah's first trip to Amon G. Carter Stadium since 2009, a visit most Ute fans would like to forget. Back then, No. 4 TCU kept its own undefeated season alive with a 55-28 win over the No. 16 Utes.

Now, 17 years later, the circumstances couldn't be more different.

Both teams are in the Power Four Big 12 Conference, but TCU is having a rare down season after going to the national championship game in 2022 and posting a pair of 9-4 seasons in 2024 and 2025.

The Sonny Dykes-coached Frogs have been on the wrong side of one-score games in all five of their losses this season, including a 17-10 loss vs. BYU and a 29-28 loss at Arizona that saw the Wildcats recover a pooch onside kick and kick a game-winning field goal.

"I mean, we're two recovered fumbles away from this game being for who's going to be in the championship game," Dykes said. "You know what I'm saying? I mean, we had a chance to recover a fumble against West Virginia and we had two chances to recover a fumble against Arizona.

"That even takes away the lack of productivity we've had on offense. Just those two plays right there, we're sitting here at, what, 7-3 and two losses in the league and this game is for an opportunity to get in the Big 12 championship."

As Dykes said, TCU's offense has been fairly unproductive. The Horned Frogs have what Dykes thinks is his best-ever defense, but the coach known for his offensive teams has been unable to find answers this season.

TCU is averaging just 23.4 points per game.

The Horned Frogs are averaging 372.9 yards per game, No. 74 in the nation and No. 15 in the 16-team Big 12, and have one of the nation's worst rushing offenses at just 106.5 yards per game.

This is a pretty one-dimensional offense that will try to beat teams through the air, and that's where the Frogs have found some success, throwing for 266.4 yards per game.

"They're throwing the heck out of it on the offensive side of the ball," Utah coach Morgan Scalley said.

Harvard transfer Jaden Craig, who is battling an ankle injury, did not practice all week, per 247Sports' Jeremy Clark. ESPN's Pete Thamel reported that sophomore Adam Schobel is expected to make his first-ever start on Saturday.

If it is indeed Schobel, it's a tough spot to be in for a debut.

The former four-star quarterback will have to have a great night against a Ute defense that has given up explosive plays but is still No. 10 in pass efficiency defense and is holding opposing quarterbacks to just a 53% completion rate.

Utah also has 14 interceptions on the year.

TCU quarterback Jaden Craig, center, looks to pass the ball as TCU offensive lineman Noah McKinney (58) tries to block BYU linebacker Isaiah Glasker (16) during game Saturday, Oct. 3, 2026, in Fort Worth, Texas. | LM Otero, Associated Press

The Utes will have another opportunity to get pressure on an opposing quarterback that doesn't have a top-tier offensive line protecting him. The Horned Frogs have allowed 28 sacks, one of the highest rates in the nation, and Craig has been pressured on nearly 33% of his dropbacks. For Utah to have success against a good TCU passing offense, it needs to get to the quarterback.

Craig or Schobel will have one of the top tight ends in the country in Kari Ashley to throw to. Ashley has 684 yards and four touchdowns on the season, and he's complemented by receivers Ed Small (633 yards, 2 TDs) and Jordan Dwyer (580 yards, 1 TD).

It's yet another test for Utah's secondary against a talented receiving corps.

"Wide receiver Dwyer I think is fantastic. Really good skill players," Scalley said.

Scalley and the Utes know they can't keep giving up explosive passing plays during the most important part of the season, especially against an offense like TCU's that has one of the better explosive play rates in the conference.

"We're giving up chunk yardage plays on the defensive side of the ball that we can't continue to give up. We've got to clean some stuff up on the back end," Scalley said. "Some of it's just plaster coverage. Sometimes when the quarterback escapes, guys trying to do too much, leaving their coverage.

"Some of it's pre-snap identification and communication. So there's enough there that we've got to get better at in the secondary for sure."

Devon Dampier has turned in Heisman moments in each of the past two weeks, and Saturday's game will provide another opportunity.

TCU's defense is one of the best the Utes will face all year.

"They run to the football. They're athletic, those defensive ends, the outside backers, and they put some pressure on you," Scalley said. "Anytime you're relentless and when the ball is thrown, you're turning and running and you've trained your body to do that, those are the defenses that usually end up making a bunch of plays."

TCU is No. 22 in the nation in total defense (304.4 yards per game), No. 23 in rushing defense (109 yards per game) and No. 41 in passing defense (95.4 yards per game).

The most points the Horned Frogs have given up this season is 28 against Noah Fifita and Arizona.

Simply put, it's going to take another phenomenal performance by Dampier.

The health of running back Wayshawn Parker will be key, though Daniel Bray has proven that he can step up in his absence. Parker is probable to play after getting just one carry last week with a heel injury.

If Utah can run on the Frogs' excellent rush defense, it will do a lot to open up the pass game for Dampier.

It's tough to bet against a Ute offense that has shown up in nearly every game and is averaging 37.9 points. If Utah can have offensive success against a tough TCU defense, it will be tough for the Horned Frogs to win in a shootout.

If TCU is going to hand the Utes their first loss, it will be because they slowed down Dampier and Utah's offense, something that a lot of teams have been unable to do.

Dampier is playing at a very high level against some tough defenses in the past two games, averaging 190.5 yards and two touchdowns through the air and 94.5 yards and two touchdowns on the ground.

Once again, Dampier's rushing ability will come up big to help break through a TCU defense that has been stingy against running backs.

In a primetime game on the road, the Utes have a chance to earn a trip back to Dallas in two weeks to compete for their first-ever Big 12 championship.

"Every game is a championship game. We don't have anything solidified. We got to go 1-0 this week. All of our attention is on TCU," Scalley said.

Utah Utes head coach Morgan Scalley walks the sideline during the first half of a football game against the BYU Cougars, Saturday, Nov. 7, 2026, at Rice-Eccles Stadium in Salt Lake City. | Erin Hooley, Deseret News

#2966. Ranking all the scary comments Steph Curry makes in the ESPN article

Three days ago, ESPN's Anthony Slater published a story that served to remind us all that time slows for no one and that Steph Curry's playing days are numbered. It was startling. The specifics of it were so definitive, so obviously instructive of how the music would suddenly stop, that many fans were (rightfully) shook. Every quote feels like a night night dagger; but, since this is a sports blog, let's rank them by order of "BANG!"-itude.

First: if you haven't read the piece, go and read it, because I'll be jumping around.

Is this actually a scary quote? Not totally. We must always remember that a professional athlete who has played at the highest level possible for a human being during this moment in time lives in the moment most of the time. But you know the end is on the horizon once the athlete begins to discuss the end. Most of our stars have been stuck in some version of that kid who loved the game they played and wound up making millions for doing and mere recognition of mortality is revolutionary in terms of self-reflection. But the scare factor is that Steph's aware the party's winding down. His body has been telling him, and now his conscious thought has joined the fray.

This is a heightening of that recognition. A recognition of context, but also "the ability to convince myself" is a heckuva self-reflection. His mind is asking his body to prove it, and — for now — it's satisfying his standards. Will he lower his standards to keep convincing himself? Or does he have a "If I can't do 'x' anymore, then that's it" in mind?

Slater gets quotes from Steve Kerr in this piece, too, and for this segment he relates:

"He's staying off his feet [more often]," Kerr said. "Relying on the fact that he's built up this incredible skill level that's not going to leave him. Then, he's found a way to stay really fit without pounding the knees, the ankles. He's on the bike, underwater treadmill. He's creative."

But I'm putting this 8th because I'm a little scared by how this aging curve seems to have surprised Curry. Slater begins the article by talking about earlier this year, and that being the first time he really seemed to have grappled with his aging body.

Every athlete — every human being! — is different, of course, so this isn't a criticism, it's just a note that Steph Curry is not one of those athletes who plans too far ahead. His greatness has been defined by putting the pedal to the metal at all times; but, everyone in their mid-thirties would be wise to reassess their body maintenance. You can see how the idea of leaving something on the table doesn't appeal to him. It's not a lack of wisdom or intelligence, it's just the way that he is wired. But as a fan reading this, the fear factor is in how he's just going to keep dancing until the music stops.

Well, okay, and then there's an acknowledgement that he needs to slow some aspect of it down. This line definitely amps up the bummer vibes of the piece. It's like he's counting how many sunrises he's got left.

Or counting how many more sandwiches he'll have left to eat in his life. But you can see why Dylan Thomas urged rage because contemplation or otherwise quiet reflection is just a bummer. It sucks to be reminded that this is all going to end — better to burn out than fade away, right? But that's the tension of being a professional athlete. That's certainly the trajectory Steph is on; will this slowdown prolong his career?

This is the first quote of the aptly titled article, "The clock is ticking on Steph Curry's final act with the Warriors." Slater starts with an anecdote about an off-day workout going awry and quickly pulls in the oblique abdominal strain, but the constant throughout the article is Steph's knee.

"a weird soreness and pain that didn't go away"

"It was basically holding. It wasn't going either direction."

There's even discussion of how much it held him back, where he was just going through the motions while on the court at times. This is as close to a medical assessment as we get in the piece, and it's devastating. Cartilage doesn't come back as we age thanks to how blood flow works in the area. That's where things like stem cell therapy come into play. But, at this point, it's pretty clear that after all the miles Steph Curry has logged over the years, we're talking about patching an engine that's getting near the end of its life. Consider this part one of a two-part idea. Part two:

As terrible a setback as the oblique abdominal strain/tear is, this quote plus the one before it really does point to his knee being the ticking time bomb, and that he's well aware that's the case.

The story is that Stephen Curry had the biggest setback of his professional career this calendar year and because his body has started to break down. It doesn't get any scarier than that. This year is almost over. What horror(s) will next year bring?

But I'll end this post … the least scary thing Curry says in the whole piece.

If circumstances demanded it, Curry said he would've been fine sliding into more of a complementary role late in his career, similar to most fading stars. Perhaps he eventually will. "It would not kill my ego," Curry said. "I want to win. Whatever that means, you make decisions based on what's in front of you."

You're not going to find any Stephen Curry followers who don't think the guy has a degree of humility about him. That's part of what makes him special. He might be the greatest. At times, even he might even acknowledge that he's "up there," in terms of the all-time greats. But he's also aware of how fleeting that feeling can be. It won't be long before he's an afterthought. He's certainly been in the league long enough to see opinions, rankings, or simply stature diminish or outright disappear overnight. And there's also the thing where, sometimes, we need to say something out loud just to convince ourselves it's true.

We don't know when Curry will return from his current injury, but there's still just a bit more time left for us to enjoy a bit more history created by his play. If this isn't the last ride, then it's the penultimate one.

#2965. Bitcoin sell-off: Crypto is failing the 'digital gold' test again as it sinks with stocks

Bitcoin's latest sell-off is reviving doubts that it can behave as the "digital gold" market hedge that crypto backers have long envisioned.

Coming into 2026, experts said that showing bitcoin could serve as a market hedge rather than a risk-on asset that moves up and down with growth stocks was an important test for the cryptocurrency.

Bitcoin was trading under $62,000 on Friday morning, down from an all-time high of more than $126,000 in early October last year. Its downward spiral in recent months and weeks has been precipitated by a confluence of factors. The heavy midterm elections losses for the Republicans, the Binance hack, and a world on edge about the security of all things technology — FAA ground stop, the outage of the National Security Agency's highly classified internal communications system, several online banking outages — have not helped the mood when it comes to putting trust in the digital world.

But the market itself may be the biggest factor. Investors are currently in sell everything mode, including tech stocks and gold — the Dow Jones Industrial Average and S&P 500 tumbled into correction territory on Friday, according to CNBC data. Bitcoin's recent price action has also tracked the move in the biggest tech IPOs, SpaceX and Anthropic. Taken together, the cryptocurrency is failing the test again when it comes to showing its worth as a market hedge during times of investor anxiety.

In the past month alone, bitcoin had dropped roughly $20,000, as of Friday morning, according to CNBC data.

Investors and financial management firms aren't viewing it as safe an investment as in the past, said Ryan Kirkley, co-founder and chief executive of Global Settlement Network, a Miami-based fintech that provides infrastructure for currency settlement, including crypto. "There's a bit more caution than there was a year ago."

What is behind bitcoin's fall, and what is in store for its future? Here is what experts say.

There have been many theories about how bitcoin would react based on different market and economic conditions. But historically, bitcoin has done best in low-rate environments and when lending opportunities are plentiful, said Juan Xavier Sanchez, head of wealth strategy at Activest Wealth Management in Aventura, Florida.

When rates are down and treasuries aren't paying good yields, people tend to be more risk-seeking for higher returns. "Bitcoin for many investors is in that bucket," Sanchez said.

Experts have noted that gold, which is now down over 35% from its early 2026 peak, is less attractive when cash accounts are paying 5%. Investments like gold and bitcoin offer no yield.

Bitcoin's rising correlation with the Nasdaq-100 since late September suggests the two are increasingly moving in tandem, according to data from Fairlead Strategies, a technical research firm. That's consistent with an environment of higher volatility in which correlations across risk assets tend to increase. However, the correlation remains below its early-2026 highs, the data show.

Bitcoin has certain characteristics of digital gold, in that it has a limited supply, no single owner and no CEO or board that can decide to issue more, said Anthony Georgiades, founder and general partner at Innovating Capital in New York, which invests in early-stage crypto, blockchain, digital infrastructure and deep tech companies.

But it's also been caught up in monetary policy since its inception. The last decade or so of monetary policy has been extremely rewarding to speculative risk-on assets, Georgiades said. However, in periods of volatility, those types of assets have experienced the most significant drawdowns and have been the most heavily punished, Georgiades said. That means that a shock on one side of the board that might be completely independent of bitcoin could trigger a large volatility event "given how intertwined digital assets have become in the overall financial system," he said.

Notably, bitcoin hasn't acted much like digital gold in more recent years, said Abe Sheikh, chief investment officer of Cordoba Advisory Partners in New York. In fact, it has fallen substantially during periods when the stock market has been falling. For example, from October 6 through November 18, the S&P 500 fell about 6.3%, and bitcoin fell around 27%. From January 21, 2025 through April 8, 2025, the S&P dropped around 17.4%, while bitcoin slid 27.8%, he said.

Democrats gaining control over both houses is viewed as a negative for crypto from a market perspective. The crypto industry spent more than $200 million to support crypto-friendly candidates, but its role had less of an impact on overall results than in the last election cycle. However, bitcoin's downward trend was already in the works, said Cyrus Amini, chief investment officer at Hyphen Wealth Management in Lafayette, California.

In September, the Clarity Act failed in the U.S. Senate, and while its outlook is now dim, according to many political and market experts, some form of crypto legislation is still considered a priority for many Democrats and Republicans, while the Trump-appointed heads of the Commodity Future Trading Commission and Securities and Exchange Commission continue to favor crypto in rulemaking.

This fall, investors also started to question the security of encryption technology, leading to more concerns over the security of their bitcoin holdings, said Kirkley of Global Settlement Network. The Binance hack in October muddied the waters further. And the recent stock market jitters and economic concerns piled on doubts, Kirkley said. The last few weeks have been especially rough with data security concerns from multiple fronts, he added.

Wall Street's embrace of bitcoin has been a big positive for the cryptocurrency. Kirkley noted that 20% of bitcoin's market price is coming from institutional investors, and that's a conservative estimate.

In addition, new research from digital assets company Galaxy shows that a record 40.5% of all 13F-reported spot bitcoin exchange-traded product holdings, and 11.2% of all the bitcoin in the funds, came from advisory and wealth planning firms, such as RIAs and brokerage platforms, as of the third quarter 13F filings with the SEC, which are required of firms with over $100 million in assets to report their holdings.

Much of this investment is through bitcoin spot ETFs like the iShares Bitcoin Trust ETF (IBIT) that track its price. These ETFs, first approved by the SEC in January 2024, were supposed to provide a reliable new source of institutional demand. But ETF ownership also makes bitcoin especially easy to sell.

Outflows from several of these funds have been substantial in recent months. IBIT, the largest of its peers, had six-month net outflows of $1.65 billion through Nov. 18 and one-month outflows of $1.37 billion, according to ETF Database. The second biggest, Fidelity Wise Origin Bitcoin Fund (FBTC) had one-month net outflows of $322.65 million and six-month net outflows of $771.86 million, according to ETF Database. Grayscale Bitcoin Mini Trust ETF (BTC) had one-month net outflows of $66.35 million, though it had inflows of $590.04 million over six months.

As of September 2026, public companies collectively hold close to 1.29 million bitcoins, which is more than 6% of the 21 million coins in existence, according to bitcoin.com.

Most public companies invest their corporate treasury portfolios in low-risk, highly liquid instruments such as money market funds, Treasury bonds and Treasury bills. That said, there are some well-known companies that have invested in bitcoin. SpaceX, for example, purchased 18,712 bitcoins for a total cost of $661 million, at an average price of $35,324 per coin. The company began accumulating bitcoin in early 2021 and held its position unchanged from mid-2024 until its IPO filing.

Digital Asset Treasury companies, known as DATs or DATCOs, have also played a role in bitcoin's volatility. DATs are publicly listed firms whose primary business strategy involves holding significant quantities of digital assets, such as bitcoin or ethereum, on their corporate balance sheets to drive shareholder value. Companies in this space include Strategy, Strive, and Twenty One Capital.

"DATs have exacerbated the problem," said Sheikh of Cordoba Advisory Partners. When bitcoin is rising, these companies borrow money in the debt market or raise capital to buy more bitcoin, which magnifies the upswings. They have also sold bitcoin to make their bond coupon payments. "It leads to enormous volatility on the upside and downside," Sheikh said.

As of mid-November, Strategy, the largest corporate holder of bitcoin, holds 850,849 BTC, according to a regulatory filing. That represents roughly 4% of bitcoin's total supply. The company acquired these holdings at a total cost of approximately $64.2 billion, with an average purchase price of roughly $75,452 per coin, according to the filing.

Bitcoin has been trending lower since the peak in October 2025. A trading range has taken hold and the bottom of the range is just below 60,000, said Katie Stockton, founder and managing partner of Fairlead Strategies. "It appears to be trying to find a footing," she said.

Oversold conditions on the heels of recent lows in June and July, in addition to long-term momentum, suggest bitcoin can support an upturn, she said. But that depends on whether the crypto can hold at a current range around $60,000, she said. "At the end of the day, bitcoin is exceptionally volatile. It's still an area that people might go to sell when they think that risk is higher in other areas."

By mid-morning Friday, the price of bitcoin had slipped below $60,000, its lowest mark since July. It was on pace for its seventh-straight weekly loss for the first time since May 2022, according to CNBC data.

#2964. Kayd Coffman Struggles in Brutal Michigan State Senior Night Loss to Oregon

Pat Fitzgerald and the Spartans picked up their eighth loss of the season, falling to the Ducks 45-7 in Michigan State's Senior Night game. Fitzgerald was forced to start Kayd Coffman at quarterback after Alessio Milivojevic was ruled out earlier this week, and Cam Fancher was ruled out after being a game-time decision. Coffman struggled to lead the offense, completing 10 of 19 passes for 106 yards, with one interception and zero touchdowns. Coffman was eventually taken out in the fourth quarter for Leo Hannan.

Running back Jaziun Patterson returned to the field for the first time since the Oct. 17 loss to Northwestern after an injury. Patterson entered the season as the projected backup running back, but an early-season injury to Cam Edwards made him the lead back for a few games before he also got hurt. He struggled in this game, finishing with 26 rushing yards on 16 attempts and averaging 1.6 yards per carry, but he did run in the Spartans' only touchdown.

The Spartans' defense couldn't do anything to stop Oregon's running game. The Ducks' running backs rushed for 196 yards on 31 attempts, averaging 6.3 yards per carry, and accounted for four touchdowns. Michigan State sacked quarterback Dylan Raiola three times, but it was credited with only four tackles for loss all night, so the front rarely got into the backfield against the run. The Spartans' run defense has been one of the season's better aspects, but on Friday night they couldn't find it.

Michigan State's biggest mistakes all came in the second quarter. The quarter started with a drive that ended with kicker Liam Boyd missing a 47-yard field goal attempt, which would have given Michigan State its first points of the game. Later in the quarter, the defense forced an Oregon punt, but receiver Chrishon McCray muffed it, and Oregon defensive back Trey McNutt recovered at the Michigan State 16-yard line. The defense then held the Ducks to a field goal. Oregon finished the second quarter with a fake field goal: placekicker Gage Hurych scored on a 15-yard run after special teams failed to stop the fake following a controversial no-holding call.

During the pregame Senior Night celebrations, four seniors who still have eligibility didn't walk: defensive end Michael Richard, tight end Brennan Parachek, and linebackers Cam Stodghill and Dion Crawford. Their decision not to walk might hint at a return to college football next season under the NCAA's new age-based eligibility rules. Pat Fitzgerald also spoke earlier about linebacker Jordan Hall, who could return as a Spartan, as his final season was filled with injuries. Hall still walked during the celebrations, but his options remain open.

Pat Fitzgerald and the Spartans will have one last chance to pick up a win this season as they travel to New Jersey to face Rutgers on Saturday, Nov. 28.

#2963. ANALYSIS: Day’Ron Sharpe is emerging as a bright spot despite the Nets’ ugly start

Things aren't pretty in Brooklyn right now, not even close.

The Nets are 3-11, and even though the record looks bad, the way they keep losing adds to the frustration. Brooklyn is coming off an embarrassing 137-107 loss to a Chicago Bulls team that entered the game with just two wins.

Beyond that, the team has now lost five games by at least 15 points this season, the most in the NBA. It doesn't stop there, either. The Nets have allowed 135+ points in back-to-back games for the first time since April 1969!

So, yeah, optimism is pretty hard to come by these days. That's to put it mildly, of course.

But for the fearless, ride-or-die fans out there anxiously searching for something, just anything, to feel good about, there is a bright spot worth discussing. That bright spot goes by the name Day'Ron Sharpe.

Sharpe, 25, is the longest-tenured player currently on Brooklyn's roster, in his sixth season with the organization. After the team dealt Nic Claxton to Chicago over the summer in a three-team deal with the Minnesota Timberwolves to bring in Julius Randle, this is Sharpe's first year as the Nets' full-time starting center.

Throughout his career, analytics have always loved Sharpe, particularly for his offensive rebounding. Entering this season, though, the question was more about whether that efficiency would translate to a larger sample size.

And so far, it has continued, and then some. According to Cleaning the Glass, the Nets have been 18.4 points per 100 possessions better with Sharpe on the floor than without him, placing him in the 93rd percentile among all forwards and bigs who have played a minute this season.

While rebounding has always been his big calling card, the biggest difference in Sharpe's on/off numbers seems to come on offense. The Nets have scored 13.0 more points per 100 possessions with the North Carolina product on the court, which ranks in the 95th percentile. It's also the seventh-biggest discrepancy among forwards and bigs.

For perspective, his overall on/off differential currently ranks ahead of notable names like Nikola Jokić (+15.5) and Alperen Şengün (+15.5). He's also just one percentile beyond Karl-Anthony Towns. All three of those players are averaging over 30 minutes per game, compared to Sharpe's 23.6, but that certainly doesn't make it something to scoff at.

Sharpe is no stranger to ranking high in efficiency categories, but the most encouraging part is that he's doing it with an expanded workload so far. Through 14 games, he is averaging over 23 minutes per night. His career high in a single season before this was last year, when he averaged 18.7 in 62 games. So, the big man has never averaged over 20 minutes per night before this season. He's also started all 14 games for Brooklyn this year, already shattering his previous career-high in starts per season, which was just eight in his rookie campaign.

Overall, Sharpe, who re-signed on a two-year, $20 million contract this offseason, has averaged 8.4 points, 7.1 rebounds (including 3.3 offensive boards), and 4.0 assists on 52.9% shooting.

Another encouraging number is the 4.0 assists per game. With Claxton out of the picture, Sharpe has taken on a much bigger role as the offensive facilitator. He's done a really solid job of embracing the responsibility that comes with being an offensive hub. It's a role Claxton anchored for years in Brooklyn.

That's especially important for a Nets team that still seems to be searching for an identity and consistency on offense. The passing isn't necessarily new, either. Sharpe had flashed that in smaller doses throughout his career. Now that he is getting the opportunites, it's becoming even more obvious.

Against the Atlanta Hawks on November 7, he dropped 13 assists and his first career triple-double. The 13 assists were the most in a single game by a Nets center in franchise history.

For me, the biggest concern regarding Brooklyn's squad entering the year was their lack of rim protection after dealing Claxton. That left a big void, and the Nets didn't do much over the summer to fix it. They added Mo Wagner, but as fans have seen, he is much more of an offensive player and that's being kind. That's not to downplay his impact; his offensive ability is certainly important, but he has limitations down low.

It's never been a strength of Sharpe's either. As a result, the team is currently allowing 48.7 paint points per game, the ninth-most in the NBA. For a team like Brooklyn that lacks elite-level firepower and just plain athleticism, it's difficult to win consistently when you're allowing nearly 50 points per game in the paint.

By no means does all of that responsibility fall on Sharpe, though. Sean Marks deserves criticism for structuring the roster that way, and his frontcourt teammates also haven't necessarily helped turn the tide in that facet.

Ultimately, his start to the year has been very promising. Digging deeper than the sheer numbers, the continued efficiency and success while playing at a career-high rate is exactly why fans should be excited moving forward.

There are still 68 games to play, but so far, Sharpe is making the most of his extended role.

#2962. Bitcoin's regulation bull case is played out. What could drive crypto gains moving forward

Crypto investors are hunting for the next positive catalyst after U.S. crypto regulation — a longtime market driver — fizzled in 2026.

Bitcoin traded around $61,500 on Friday, a more than 50% decline from its all-time high above $126,000 reached in October 2025. The broader digital asset market has struggled after the Clarity Act, a crypto market structure bill, failed to advance in the Senate in September.

Investors now see Wall Street's embrace of blockchain technology — particularly tokenization — as the next trend to give bitcoin a boost.

"Every single traditional firm is now looking at digital assets," BitGo CEO Mike Belshe told CNBC last week in an exclusive interview from the fintech-focused Cantor Convergence conference in Miami Beach. That includes BlackRock, the world's largest asset manager.

He pointed to statements from the asset management giant's CEO, Larry Fink, who said the next major evolution in global finance is the creation of digital representations of every stock, bond, fund and real-world asset onto a shared blockchain ledger.

"I think the biggest catalyst we're seeing right now is actually very much in motion and it's happening, which is capital markets are moving onchain," eToro CEO Yoni Assia told CNBC in an exclusive interview from the conference. He pointed to the SpaceX IPO as an example, which had derivatives trading on eToro before the IPO.

"First day of trading, we distributed it to our retail clients, and it started trading 24/7, and that's all because capital markets are now copying crypto markets and are sitting on the same rails," Assia said. "Crypto markets have always been 24/7, they never sleep, and now what we're seeing is that capital markets are copying crypto markets using the same technology so stocks are moving onchain, they're being tokenized."

Assia said that supportive regulation in the U.S. from agencies, including the Securities and Exchange Commission and the Commodity Futures Trading Commission, is also helping adoption of crypto.

He pointed to the CFTC earlier this month clearing the way for crypto-style perpetual futures tied to individual stocks to trade on regulated U.S. exchanges for the first time. Assia also highlighted the SEC's move to issue an order in September, creating a regulatory pathway for certain trading venues to trade tokenized representations of publicly traded U.S. stocks.

"Blockchain technology is now starting to power what's happening in capital markets," Assia told CNBC. "That is the one of the biggest changes we're going to see in capital markets for many, many years."

"We're talking about hundreds of trillions of dollars that are going to be tokenized and move onchain and at the same time, I do believe that will support prices of bitcoin and other blockchains as well," he continued.

The industry is also watching whether inflation will ease and geopolitical tensions will subside in the new year, which could lower broader macro risk currently hampering the industry.

Last month, Strategy's Michael Saylor said he thinks bitcoin's next bull market is already underway.

ARK Invest Chief Investment Officer Cathie Wood told CNBC the investment management firm's $1.5 million bull case price target for bitcoin by 2030 still stands. She also said that it wouldn't surprise her if bitcoin reached its all-time high again before the end of 2027.

"Our macro forecast has inflation breaking down considerably," Wood told CNBC in an exclusive interview at Cantor Convergence. "I think the elections have increased our confidence there because the war [in Iran], one way or the other, is going to end."

She added that "bitcoin has weathered so much bad news," including the $6.3 billion Binance hack and the "distraction" of AI which, "many people would say is sucking the oxygen away from bitcoin."

The maturation of the artificial intelligence trade could provide an opportunity for bitcoin to find new inflows.

Wood said that ARK Invest believes bitcoin has found its low and stressed that cybersecurity is "top of mind right now," which could provide yet another opportunity for crypto markets to rebound.

"I think if the Bitcoin and crypto world coalesces and makes investors feel that they've got their arms around security and actually understand that there might be more security issues in the traditional financial system, I think we're going to see a wave of support," Wood said.

#2961. Potential landing spots for Daniel Jones after $50 million Colts disaster

The Colts announced the release of quarterback Daniel Jones on Friday following another disastrous performance in a 26-3 loss to the Texans on Thursday night.

But he might not be waiting all that long to land his next gig.

The eight-year pro will hit waivers Friday afternoon with the opportunity to sign with any team if he's not claimed by Monday.

Will Anderson Jr. #51 of the Houston Texans sacks Daniel Jones #17 of the Indianapolis Colts for a safety in Houston, Texas, Thursday, Nov. 19, 2026. Getty Images

Jones, alongside All-Pro running back Jonathan Taylor, was one of two key figures who were the subject of trade deadline speculation as the Colts begin their latest rebuilding process.

According to ESPN's Adam Schefter, the Ravens made an offer to acquire the signalcaller around the deadline before turning to Jacoby Brissett. Brissett threw for 340 yards and one interception without a passing touchdown in a 23-20 loss to the Chargers, and it remains to be seen if Baltimore would want to bring in another veteran passer.

The Vikings also expressed interest in Jones before bringing back Kirk Cousins.

Teams whose quarterback rooms need help include the Cardinals and Patriots.

If Jones wants to be a starter in the NFL this year, Arizona may be his best bet. The Cardinals are in dire need of quarterback help, with Carson Beck questionable for this week's matchup and the team preparing to start 40-year-old Josh Johnson against the Chiefs.

The Patriots may also be looking for a new starter under center. The team announced Friday that Drake Maye is out indefinitely with a fractured fibula, leaving former Giant Tommy DeVito as the likely starter going forward.

Indianapolis Colts quarterback Daniel Jones talks to the media following an NFL football game against the Houston Texans in Houston, Nov. 19, 2026. AP Photo/David J. Phillip

The release comes at a premium as Jones signed a two-year, $88 million deal heading into the season. The historic deal also included nearly $50 million guaranteed and a maximum value of $100 million.

In his final game with Indianapolis, Jones completed 12 of 27 passes for 144 yards and an interception as the Colts (3-8) have now lost six of their last seven games.

Through 11 appearances this season, Jones has thrown for 1,974 yards, seven touchdowns and 13 interceptions, an NFL high. He also posted a 65.4 passer rating while completing 58.4 percent of his passes, both career lows.

#2960. Duncanville shows it's rounding into playoff form with come-from-behind win over Rockwall

FORT WORTH — For its second consecutive playoff victory, Duncanville had to overcome a halftime deficit.

It's a trend Brayden Arnold knows must be corrected, but it didn't keep him and his teammates from celebrating a 49-32 victory over Rockwall in a Class 6A Division I Region II area playoff Friday night at Crowley ISD Multi-Purpose Stadium.

Rockwall safety Blake Baird (4) consols Rockwall offensive lineman Antonio Cruz after a 49-32 loss to Duncanville during a Class 6A Division I area-round playoff game at Crowley ISD Multi-Purpose Stadium, Friday, Nov. 20, 2026 in Fort Worth. (Chris Swann/The Dallas Morning News)

"We've had a couple of games when we've had to make a comeback,'' said Arnold, a junior who rushed for a game-high 92 yards and two touchdowns. "It seems like we have a lot of young guys and it takes us a while to settle down. We've got to start fast going forward.'

Duncanville (8-4), a state semifinalist a year ago, is unbeaten in its last 10 area round games dating to 2016. Next is a Region II semifinal against Aldine Davis, a 21-14 winner over Katy Cypress Lakes.

Under head coach Reginald Samples, Duncanville is in the playoffs for the 11th consecutive season, compiling a postseason record of 47-8 with six appearances in state championship games and a pair of state titles in 2022 and 2023.

That pedigree paid off with a clutch fourth-quarter drive when Duncanville drove 75 yards in 14 plays and chewed up seven minutes of clock to increase its lead to 42-32.

Finishing the drive with a 16-yard run was Trenton Yancey, who later added an insurance touchdown on another 16-yarder with three minutes remaining.

Rockwall (9-3), coming back strong after a streak of 12 consecutive playoff appearances was broken last season, has had its season end in the area round for the ninth time in its last 13 postseason trips. Duncanville also ended Rockwall's playoff hopes in the second round in 2019 and 2024, with the identical final score of 56-14.

Rockwall coach Trey Brooks said he will look back at some missed opportunities early in the game but had no qualms with his team's effort throughout the season.

Duncanville went ahead for the first time 21-18 on the opening play on the second half, a 75-yard pass from Yancey to Jairus Ingram.

The first turnover of the game, a Rockwall fumble at midfield, set up another quick Duncanville scoring play, a 3-yard Wildcat run by Arnold.

That set off a flurry of touchdowns. Rockwall went 66 yards in seven plays, ending on a 2-yard run by Kaydon Castro, who rushed for 73 yards on 20 carries. Duncanville countered with a 51-yard jet-sweep pass from Maximus Denson to Isaiah Beal as the Duncanville lead shifted back and forth from three to 10 points.

Rockwall marched 92 yards with Castro scoring on a 13-yard run to again cut the Duncanville lead to three, 35-32, in the opening minute of the fourth quarter.

Rockwall dominated the opening minutes of the game, and its early 15-0 lead could easily have been larger but for a couple of missed chances.

On its third possession, Rockwall senior quarterback Collins Jones connected with Ryan Wiley on a 25-yard scoring pass and Jones, who threw for 208 yards, added the conversion run for an 8-0 lead.

Alijah Deloache's 11-yard scoring run early in the second quarter finished off a 39-yard drive. Rockwall's advantage in special teams created favorable field position throughout the first half.

Duncanville did little on offense until Arnold on third-and-1 bolted 66 yards for a touchdown. In the final seconds of the half, Duncanville found its passing game on a 75-yard drive that ended on Denson's 12-yard scramble to cut the Rockwall lead to 18-14.

#2959. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Uncertainty in the Middle East, elevated Treasury yields and fears about an AI bubble continue to keep global markets on edge. Given this volatile backdrop, investors seeking stable income can consider enhancing their portfolios by adding dividend-paying stocks.

However, the vast universe of dividend stocks can make selection challenging. Tracking the ratings of top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend stocks, as their recommendations are backed by in-depth research.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Chord Energy is an independent exploration and production company with long-lived assets primarily in the Williston Basin. With a quarterly base dividend of $1.30 per share, CHRD offers a dividend yield of 3.67%.

In a research note on third-quarter expectations, RBC Capital analyst Scott Hanold reiterated a buy rating on CHRD stock with a price target of $175. "We model production near the top of guidance and continue to see strong performance from new wells and base production," said Hanold.

Specifically, the five-star analyst expects Q3 2026 production at 283 thousand barrels of oil equivalent per day, or Mboe/d, in line with the Street's consensus and near the high end of the 278 to 284 Mboe/d guidance. Hanold said his Q3 2026 earnings per share estimate increased to $5.29 due to lower non-cash depreciation, depletion and amortization.

Meanwhile, cash flow per share estimate moved lower to $13.59 to reflect Hanold's update based on the current backdrop and commodity mark-to-market. He expects Chord Energy to report capital spending of $375 million for Q3 2026, slightly above the Street's estimate of $369 million.

Overall, Hanold remains bullish on CHRD, given its best-in-class balance sheet. He expects a free cash flow payout of 80% in the third quarter, with the possibility of an upside. Hanold expects investors to focus on management commentary on 2027 capital plans, Williston M&A opportunities, and long-term performance of 4-mile wells.

Hanold ranks No. 310 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 14.9%. See Chord Energy KPIs on TipRanks.

Williams, an energy infrastructure company that delivers natural gas, is the next dividend pick. The company recently completed the $5.5 billion acquisition of Momentum Midstream, which will expand its natural gas infrastructure in the Haynesville to meet growing Gulf Coast LNG, power and industrial demand. At a quarterly dividend of $0.5250 per share, Williams offers a yield of 2.89%.

In her latest research note, RBC Capital analyst Elvira Scotto reiterated a buy rating on Williams stock with a price target of $87. Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, the analyst said that she expects WMB to announce strong quarterly performance.

Scotto raised her estimates to reflect insights from the quarterly catch-up call, the completion of the Momentum Midstream acquisition, and mark-to-market quarter-to-date commodity prices. She expects WMB to report Q3 2026 adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization of $2,036 million, which is modestly above the Street's consensus estimate.

Furthermore, Scotto expects Williams' Transmission, Power & Gulf segment to increase quarter-over-quarter, driven by the startup of the Socrates power project and favorable natural gas liquids, or NGL, margins.

Scotto noted that WMB stock has underperformed due to backlash related to data centers and investor anticipation of another Power Innovation project. She expects the company to announce at least one more project before the end of this year, potentially with a new customer and a contract duration of 15 to 20 years, which would be a positive catalyst for the stock.

Scotto ranks No. 175 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been profitable 67% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.2%. See Williams Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

EOG Resources, a crude oil and natural gas exploration and production company, will hold its third-quarter earnings call on Nov. 6. At a quarterly dividend of $1.02 per share, EOG offers a dividend yield of 2.75%.

Heading into Q3 earnings, Jefferies analyst Lloyd Byrne reiterated a buy rating on EOG Resources stock and increased his price target to $185 from $175. The analyst expects the company's Q3 cash flow per share to exceed the Street's consensus by 13%, driven by better-than-expected production.

Byrne expects crude oil production, excluding the UAE, to reach 551 Mboe/d, which is at the high end of guidance (546-551 Mboe/d) and above the Street's projection of 548 Mboe/d, driven by continued growth in the Utica.

Furthermore, Byrne expects cash operating expenses to come in at $10.27 per barrel of oil equivalent, below the midpoint of guidance of $10.05 to $11.35, helped by lower lease operating expenses. Overall, the analyst expects EOG to deliver free cash flow of $2.65 billion, supporting another quarter of buybacks near the minimum 70% shareholder return threshold and implying about $1.5 billion in share repurchases.

"As a first indication of the '27 plan, we expect EOG will message another year of mid-single-digit (~3.5%) growth on a flat capital budget (~$6.55bn), which is above the Street modeling 1% oil growth at $6.6bn," said Byrne.

Byrne ranks No. 303 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 58% of the time, delivering an average return of 18.5%. See EOG Resources Statistics on TipRanks.

#2958. More Than A Minor League: How The G League Builds NBA Champions

The NBA G League is full of talent, littered with former collegiate stars and former lottery picks. But it's not just a league full of flops who were never good enough for the NBA. It's a launching point for the careers of future NBA champions and a place where struggling NBA players can find their feet and become contributors on an NBA roster once more.

No G League arena knows that story better than the Mississauga Sports and Entertainment Centre. Champions and Raptors of old such as Pascal Siakam, Fred VanVleet, Norman Powell and Chris Boucher once plied their trade on the hardwood at the venue formerly known as the Hershey Centre, before a name change and a changing of the guard saw JaKobe Walter, Gradey Dick, AJ Lawson come through the doors during the era of the Paramount Fine Foods Centre in recent years.

The Raptors have always been at the forefront of using the G League to find contributors and develop their own draft picks, and that should mean something to fans in Mississauga. A lot of folks in other places may scoff at the G League but it is a chapter in the stories of some of Raptors fans' favourite players and, at the bare minimum, a footnote in the 2019 championship.

While fans may know those stories, there are G League alumni littered around the NBA, many of them NBA champions who either got their start in the G or earned their way back to the NBA.

The 2019 Raptors championship team featured two players whose stories are integral to the tale that is 2010s NBA basketball, and both of them got their start in the G and were even teammates at one point in Reno, Nevada, for the Bighorns in the 2010-11 season.

The three-time NBA champion with the Spurs, Raptors and bubble-era Lakers was drafted by Cleveland 46th overall in 2009 after an NCAA championship at UNC, but after 20 games in a rookie year in which he struggled, the Cavs cut him loose, allowing him to move on to San Antonio, where they also waived him. He landed in Nevada with the Reno Bighorns, the former G League affiliate of the Sacramento Kings.

Green balled out in 16 games for Reno, leading the team in scoring with 20 points a night, as well as 7.5 rebounds per game. Arguably most importantly, he shot 43.4 per cent from deep, which was the skill that got him to the NBA and made him valuable there. He just needed a platform to show it.

Jeremy Lin was Green's teammate in Reno, albeit not for long. Lin began his NBA career in Golden State after going undrafted, a tough place to come by playing time as he sat third on the point guard depth chart behind Monta Ellis and some guy named Steph Curry.

Lin spent a lot of time down in the G League with Reno, who had an affiliation with Golden State at the time. He played well and earned himself an opportunity in China during the NBA lockout before returning to North America with the Knicks the following season, and everyone knows how that went for him.

San Antonio Spurs guard Danny Green tries to pull the ball away from Charlotte Hornets guard Jeremy Lin during Monday night's game at Time Warner Cable Arena on March 21, 2016 in Charlotte, N.C. The Charlotte Hornets defeated the San Antonio Spurs 91-88. (Robert Lahser/Charlotte Observer/Tribune News Service via Getty Images)

That Reno Bighorns team was a launching point for Lin and Green, but it also had a roster any basketball sicko would love. Steve Novak and Hassan Whiteside suited up for the Bighorns, as did Patrick Ewing Jr, son of the Hall of Fame Knicks big man.

Another multi-time NBA champion, with the Lakers and Thunder, actually began his career with the Oklahoma City Blue, the G League affiliate of the OKC Thunder. His lone season with Oklahoma's G League club led him to a Summer League invite with the Lakers and a Summer League championship alongside Lonzo Ball, Josh Hart and Kyle Kuzma.

Caruso earned himself a two-way contract, which made him the first two-way signee in Lakers history. While on a two-way, he continued to play in the G League with the South Bay Lakers until his talent became so undeniable that he eventually earned himself a regular rotation spot. He's gone on to win two championships and sign $123 million worth of NBA contracts.

While for Lin, Green and Caruso, the G League was where they got their first really meaningful opportunities in professional basketball, for others it has been a platform to resurrect a career that once looked dead in the water. Certain players take longer to find their role and carve out their basketball niche, and the G League can provide an opportunity away from the bright lights of the NBA.

Gary Payton II, son of the Hall of Fame SuperSonics legend, had actually played for the Lakers, Bucks and Wizards before finding his way to the Raptors 905 in January 2021.

With the 905, Gary carved out a role for himself that should be familiar to anyone familiar with the Payton family name: defensive stopper. He won the league's Defensive Player of the Year Award, joining Chris Boucher as the only other player to win the award as a member of the 905. Finding his role in professional basketball during his lone season with the 905 opened an opportunity for him to join the Golden State Warriors, where he won a championship in 2022 and has been an NBA player ever since.

Raptors 905 centre Jamarion Sharp won the 2026 Defensive Player of the Year Award in the G League as a member of the Texas Legends. Who's to say he can't follow in the footsteps of Payton II and carve out a defensive role for himself in the NBA?

While many fans write off the G League as a land of misfit toys and failed prospects, it is anything but that. You are highly unlikely to find future NBA All-Stars in the G League, with the exception of draft picks getting sent down for extra reps, but fans of G League teams ought to be on the lookout for the next elite-level role player like Caruso or Green, because they just might be playing in a G League gym this season.

#2957. SpaceX’s subsidiary Cursor tells lawmakers it's considering phasing out Chinese AI models

SpaceX's artificial-intelligence coding platform, Cursor, is considering moving away from using of Chinese AI models due to "geopolitical risk," CNBC has learned.

Cursor, which SpaceX acquired at an implied $60 billion equity value in August, had built its coding model Composer 2 on top of Chinese lab Moonshot AI's Kimi K2.5.

It's among the U.S. firms that have adopted Chinese models, whose usage has spiked across the globe this year, worrying lawmakers in Washington. The battle for AI supremacy between the U.S. and China has reached new heights in 2026 as Chinese models have improved.

But now Cursor plans to "explore phasing out PRC [People's Republic of China]-developed open-weight models because of geopolitical risk," according to a report produced by the House Committee on Homeland Security and the House Select Committee on China, shared exclusively with CNBC ahead of its publication on Friday.

The report, which follows a months-long investigation into the adoption of Chinese AI models into the American tech stack, said Cursor "informed the Committees" of those plans. Airbnb and DoorDash also engaged with the investigation about their use of Chinese AI models.

SpaceX announced it entered into a formal agreement to buy Cursor in June; the deal was completed in August. SpaceX is developing the Grok family of models, which Cursor has been involved in training.

Cursor, SpaceX and DoorDash did not respond to a CNBC request for comment. Airbnb declined to comment. The U.S. Commerce Department's Bureau of Industry and Security did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

The report found that cost, customization and near-frontier performance of Chinese AI models were driving adoption, with the U.S. lacking a competitive open-weight alternative at "price and performance the market demands."

"Beijing is now seeking to turn those technological gains into global market share and long-term dependence," the report says. China's embassy in the U.K. did not respond to a request for comment. China's Foreign Ministry did not immediately respond.

It also sets out recommendations to address the issues it has identified.

Those include launching a "national American open-weight initiative" that combines public computing power, shared evaluation infrastructure and "lawful access" to American frontier models for training.

The report also says an order, made public in October, that banned DeepSeek from federal civilian agency systems, should be built on, by prohibiting the use of "PRC-developed AI models" in federal information systems and in performance of federal contracts.

Separately, Washington is also concerned about Chinese companies accessing Nvidia chips remotely, via overseas data centers, and gaining ground by using "distillation," where new models use older, more established ones to help train them.

The U.S. Commerce Department's Bureau of Industry and Security, or BIS, announced on Monday an interim final rule designed to prevent Chinese companies' remote access to AI chips.
