#2954. U.S.-China trade, Taiwan and tech tensions remain unresolved as Trump returns from Asia

SHENZHEN, China — Even as Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation leaders gathered for high-profile photos at the summit's close, key issues from Nvidia to Taiwan remained unaddressed.

"The intrigue is around not what was said, but what remains unresolved," said Gary Dvorchak, managing director at Blueshirt.

"Taiwan remains a major source of strategic uncertainty. North Korea was notably absent from the trilateral nuclear declaration," he said. "Southeast Asia, which is increasingly central to global manufacturing and investment, was overshadowed by major-power diplomacy."

President Donald Trump did not meet with North Korea's leader Kim Jong Un, despite speculation.

Taiwan, one of the 21 member economies of APEC, sent Acer Group founder and honorary chairman Stan Shih to Shenzhen. But the self-governed island, which Beijing claims is part of its territory, did not feature in any significant way during the event. Taiwan is set to hold key elections for Taipei mayor and other posts on Nov. 28.

Instead, the highlight of this week's APEC summit in Shenzhen was a trilateral meeting Thursday between Trump, Chinese President Xi Jinping and Russian President Vladimir Putin. The three leaders, meeting together for the first time, reaffirmed interests in reducing nuclear risks and plans to increase oil production.

"The optics are impressive, but there's not much happening," Ian Bremmer, president and founder of Eurasia Group, said Friday of the trilateral on CNBC's "Squawk Box Asia."

Resolving the years-long Ukraine war would require involvement from President Volodymyr Zelenskyy and European allies, Bremmer said.

Ahead of the trilateral, Trump and Xi also held their third bilateral summit of the year. Xi greeted Trump at the airport late Wednesday, reciprocating the hospitality shown in September when the Chinese leader visited Washington.

But little was shared publicly on whether the two countries agreed to reduce tariffs further, allow rare earths to keep flowing beyond a January deadline, or address tech competition.

Recent reports tying Jensen Huang to an alleged Chinese spy led to the executive's resignation as Nvidia CEO. It also raised questions in Congress about the effectiveness of U.S. efforts to restrict China's access to advanced chips used to train artificial intelligence.

Trump indicated to Fox News that Nvidia came up briefly in his talks with Xi. U.S. officials last week said Trump did not plan to raise the matter, while noting Tesla and SpaceX CEO Elon Musk would accompany the U.S. president.

No other executive was named. Other American business leaders, including Visa CEO Ryan McInerney, who also chairs the U.S.-China Business Council, joined the APEC CEO Summit and was seen at a welcome banquet Wednesday evening.

However, Musk did not appear with Trump at major APEC-related events. CNBC was present for the opening portions of a banquet Wednesday and the Economic Leaders' Meeting Thursday and did not see Musk.

In fact, Musk flew to Shanghai Thursday morning to visit Tesla's factory there, a company executive said on Chinese social media. Musk also showed up in a social media video with Ren Hongbin, chairman of the China Council for the Promotion of International Trade.

Musk addressed Wednesday's DealBook Summit in New York via video conference, having previously said on X that he would be joining from China.

Tesla and the White House did not immediately respond to CNBC's requests for comment.

"We still don't have a durable resolution of the U.S.-China trade dispute," Dvorchak said, adding: "Business needs certainty, not just ceremony."

Trump visited Shenzhen after briefly traveling to the Philippines on Tuesday for a meeting with the leaders of Australia, India and Japan as part of the Quad grouping happening on the sidelines of the ASEAN Summit. Trump also met the prime minister of Thailand, Anutin Charnvirakul.

Topics of discussion included U.S. cooperation with the region, trade and critical minerals, U.S. Ambassador to ASEAN Kevin Kim told reporters Wednesday. He declined to comment on whether China's influence in the region was addressed.

Kim emphasized it was the second year in a row that Trump visited Southeast Asia, and noted the region "will be at the heart of SI [artificial intelligence] going forward," making it important that American technology is "getting into the right hands" through a regulatory framework the president is building.

Speaking to reporters a day later in Shenzhen, Japanese Prime Minister Sanae Takaichi also highlighted the ASEAN and Quad events over APEC. "The major outcome of this diplomatic week was the Quad Summit," she said, noting via official English translation how the four leaders expressed "strong opposition to the unilateral approach to change the status quo by force or coercion ... [as well as] arbitrary export control."

Takaichi, the first Japanese leader in seven years to visit China, received a cool reception upon her arrival in Shenzhen for the APEC meeting.

Tensions between Japan and China have escalated since Takaichi drew Beijing's ire in Nov. 2025 when she said that a Chinese attack on Taiwan could draw a Japanese military response.

Takaichi said Thursday that Japan's position on Taiwan remained unchanged as stated in the 1972 Japan-China Communiqué.

The communique said Japan "understands and respects" the People's Republic of China's position on Taiwan, but does not explicitly acknowledge Taiwan as part of the PRC.

— CNBC's Anniek Bao and Lim Hui Jie contributed to this report.

Who: Phoenix Suns (7-7) @ Utah Jazz (3-13)

Where: Delta Center — Salt Lake City, Utah

Game three of this five-game road trip comes as the Suns head up to Utah. This is their first meeting with this squad this season. Mark Williams — OUT (Left Shoulder Surgery)

Harrison Ingram — OUT (G League – Two-Way)

Jaren Jackson, Jr. — OUT (Right Knee; MCL Tear)

Lauri Markkanen — OUT (Right Hand Fracture)

Jusuf Nurkic — OUT (R Foot; 2nd Plantar Plate Tear)

The Suns, in their last two games, have run the same starting lineup but have staggered certain players on the bench to make it easier. That has brought some success to this team and is something to watch for in this game. Dillon Brooks has taken advantage of this switch, as it allows him to be aggressive on offense as the second unit's lead scorer. Not only that, but it has helped on defense as well, with him more locked in. We saw this trend in the Kings game with Brooks, and with Devin Booker as well. He had a stint in the first quarter where he was left with just the bench, which let him hunt his shots and let the team take a lead to end the first.

Having key guys like Jordan Goodwin, Ryan Dunn, and Collin Gillespie, who will do anything to find success and win on the court, makes it easier for guys in Brooks and Booker to be the lead offensive guys. Jordan Ott can maintain success by mixing both units, which would help the Suns find another win.

Two things the Suns need to win against the Utah Jazz are the three-point and rebounding game. With no Jaren Jackson Jr., Jusuf Nurkic, or Lauri Markkanen, one of those should be easier than the other. The Suns cannot lose the rebounding game to Mo Bamba, who is trying to revitalize his career, or Jaxon Hayes, who has bounced from team to team. This is a weak frontcourt, and for the Suns, this is the true test for centers Oso Ighodaro and Khaman Malauch. If they are the real deal, they should excel in these matchups and prove they can handle some of the weaker frontcourts.

On the three-point end, they have Keyonte George, who shoots almost 35% from three, with rookie Darryn Peterson also shooting 36%. Mix that with rotation guys like Svi Mykhailiuk and Brice Sensabough, and this Jazz team can erupt at any time from behind the arc. Key shooters Gillespie and Kennard will have to be at their best to match some of the threes this smaller Jazz team will fire off. Brooks and Booker will have to be at their best as well, since they are the two main three-point shooters in the starting five.

The Suns are on the road, so handle this one like the previous one, too. Even if the Jazz court looks funky due to the NBA Cup, their team is going to look even funkier trying to score on a defense that will be bought in. Phoenix extends their longest win streak of the season while also getting their first win in Group A, taking down the Utah Jazz.

#2951. Bitcoin suffered identical sell-off pattern ahead of Anthropic, SpaceX IPOs — what happens next?

Bitcoin has suffered nearly identical sell-offs ahead of two of the biggest public offerings this year, leaving investors wondering if competing demand for investor capital could be weighing on the cryptocurrency and perhaps delaying its recovery.

The price of the bitcoin dropped 22.7% in the 30 days before Anthropic's IPO and fell 22.3% in the month leading up to SpaceX's June 12 listing. Ether declined 25.3% in the 30 days preceding Anthropic's debut, and 28% in the same window ahead of SpaceX.

"Liquidity events can elevate correlations across asset classes quickly," Matthew Sheffield, chief investment officer at the ether treasury firm Sharplink, told CNBC. "The first 15 min of [Thursday's] Anthropic IPO put that on full display. Tech stocks, value stocks, oil, gold, crypto all sold off as traders sold assets to buy shares in the secondary market."

"Stores of value function as such over long horizons, but in the short term are still liquidity driven," Sheffield said. "We saw this in early Covid when the market fled to cash. Stock, bonds, commodities ... it didn't matter, you were being sold for cash."

While investors appear to be rotating capital out of crypto to make room for history-making IPO investments, that can't be said with precise certainty. Crypto has also been under pressure from ETF outflows, leverage, macro uncertainty and broader risk-off sentiment.

Still, the post-IPO price action may offer a data point in understanding where bitcoin's price goes next. With Anthropic now public, investors are watching to see whether bitcoin and ether find a floor, and if institutional demand returns.

"[After] the SpaceX IPO, bitcoin didn't truly find its final bottom until after the IPO was completed, and it didn't actually reverse the downtrend until 70 days later," said Matt Cole, CEO and chairman of Strive Asset Management, a bitcoin treasury firm. "My view is that [for] bitcoin, the most likely scenario is that it chops around here."

The expiration of IPO allocation restrictions can influence the timing of capital flows into digital assets, Sheffield said.

"Post SpaceX we saw the wealth effect in full force," Sheffield said. "Many brokers make traders wait 15-30 days before they are allowed to flip shares they received as allocations. … If you went long ETH versus SpaceX on the day of the IPO, on day 31 was when you would have become profitable."

Bitcoin's next critical test is between $60,000 and $62,000, according to Nicolai Sondergaard, a research analyst at Nansen. On Friday, it was sitting at about $62,000.

As of Friday, bitcoin was hovering at the upper end of a critical $60,000 to $62,000 support range. Its next move could determine whether it can stabilize or if it will face another leg lower and revisit its July low of $57,737.35.

Throughout November, bitcoin has been struggling from a combination of dollar strength, institutional selling, and ongoing leveraged bitcoin liquidations. The declines began in late October, as concerns over artificial intelligence valuations and spending fueled a broad tech sell-off. They were compounded by the $6.3 billion Binance hack and weakening institutional ETF demand.

Bitcoin is in the early stages of forming a bottom, according to Cole. Retail fear is elevated amid recent outages, quantum concerns and other short-term uncertainties, but bitcoin should continue to benefit from macro forces like rising debt and weakening fiat currencies, which should drive price higher, he said.

"Capital markets are open, … retail fears are high, and when you combine those things it's much more likely to be a dip-buying opportunity across the board, including bitcoin, than the start of a great financial crisis," said Cole. "It'll likely age as a volatile time to consider allocating to risk, not the opposite."

"The winter for bitcoin is basically at the end, but after winter does not come summer, it comes early spring," he added. "Anytime anyone's been around early spring, what you notice is it gets really cold still, it still rains – it's not necessarily great weather, and I think that's the environment we're in for bitcoin."

#2950. LSU Is ‘Most Dangerous Team in the Country,’ Tim Tebow Declares

OXFORD, MISSISSIPPI - SEPTEMBER 19: Head coach Lane Kiffin of the Louisiana State Tigers takes the field before the game against the Mississippi Rebels at Vaught-Hemingway Stadium on September 19, 2026 in Oxford, Mississippi. (Photo by Justin Ford/Getty Images) Getty

The LSU Tigers have given their fans plenty to be frustrated about this season. Tim Tebow, however, sees a team that could make somebody else's season more complicated. Before LSU's matchup with No. 9 Tennessee, the former Florida quarterback delivered an assessment that should get attention in both fan bases.

Speaking on SportsCenter, Tebow described the unranked Tigers as more than a difficult opponent. He believes their talent makes them a threat that goes well beyond where they sit in the rankings. That is a strong statement about a team trying to turn its ability into consistent results.

"I think they're the most dangerous team in the country. They're unranked and maybe one of the top three or four most talented teams in the country," Tebow said.

For LSU fans, that is encouraging and frustrating at the same time. Hearing that the roster has that much ability is a reminder of what this team could accomplish. It also raises the question of why the season has not produced more to celebrate.

GettyOXFORD, MISSISSIPPI – SEPTEMBER 19: Head coach Lane Kiffin of the Louisiana State Tigers takes the field before the game against the Mississippi Rebels at Vaught-Hemingway Stadium on September 19, 2026 in Oxford, Mississippi. (Photo by Justin Ford/Getty Images)

Tebow's assessment is an opinion about LSU's potential, rather than a declaration that the Tigers have played like a top team. The distinction matters heading into Tennessee. Having enough talent to beat a ranked opponent and putting together the performance required to do it are two different challenges.

For Lane Kiffin, the opportunity is straightforward. Beating Tennessee would give LSU something to show for the confidence Tebow expressed. Another disappointing result would leave fans having the same conversation about ability without enough wins.

The Tigers cannot rely on being dangerous in theory. Protecting possessions, finishing scoring opportunities and avoiding mistakes that make an opponent's job easier are how that talent has to show up. Against a ranked team, a few wasted chances can outweigh stretches of good football.

There is also a reason Tennessee should take the assessment seriously without getting caught up in the wording. An opponent's ranking does not decide how difficult matchups will be. LSU can demand attention even without a number beside its name.

Tebow has supplied a compliment that LSU fans can enjoy before kickoff. Now the Tigers have an opportunity to make it hold up on the field. Tennessee will provide a test of whether this team can deliver on that potential.

#2948. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on the growth potential of these 3 stocks

Fears around the artificial intelligence trade and higher pressure on oil prices are weighing on global markets, but a few stocks offer investors solid growth prospects even in choppy times.

Investors with a long-term horizon can look beyond the ongoing volatility and track the recommendations of top Wall Street analysts to pick attractive stocks with strong long-term growth potential.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Health insurer UnitedHealth delivered better-than-expected results for the third quarter of 2026. The company also raised its full-year earnings outlook, reflecting continued progress on its turnaround plan.

Following the Q3 print, Morgan Stanley analyst Erin Wright reiterated a buy rating on UNH stock with a price target of $529. "UNH's beat & raise reinforces turnaround momentum, with improving MA [Medicare Advantage]/Optum Health supporting a higher '26 EPS baseline & upside to '27 consensus," said Wright.

The five-star analyst added that better-than-expected performance in the UnitedHealthcare and Optum Health businesses supported another upgrade to the company's 2026 adjusted earnings per share outlook to at least $20.50. Wright noted that UNH raised the earnings guidance despite incremental investments planned for the fourth quarter of 2026.

Furthermore, Wright highlighted management's view that the consensus 2027 EPS of $22.63 was too conservative. Reaching even the low end of the company's 13% to 16% long-term EPS growth target would imply an EPS of $23.17.

Wright raised her 2026 EPS estimate to $20.65 from $19.82 and 2027 EPS to $23.24 from $22.54, reflecting the latest upside and an improving 2026 medical loss ratio to 87.4% from 88.1%. Overall, she believes that UNH's operational performance continues to improve, increasing her confidence in its UHC strategy and Optum Health turnaround execution.

Wright ranks No. 264 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been successful 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 15%. See UnitedHealth Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Social media giant Meta Platforms has been making headlines since the launch of its Muse AI agent, which is seeing solid traction. Investors look forward to updates on the company's AI strategy when it announces its third-quarter results on Oct. 21.

Heading into Q3 earnings, Guggenheim analyst Michael Morris maintained a buy rating on Meta stock and increased his price target to $850 from $700, reflecting higher estimates for 2027. He expects Muse usage momentum and engagement levels to shape investor sentiment into the Q3 earnings call.

Specifically, Morris highlighted that, according to Apptopia, Muse reached 1.3 million daily active users in five weeks versus 300,000 in the first week. Given that Muse's current adoption represents less than 0.05% of Meta's 3.6 billion daily active user base, the analyst expects management to prioritize boosting usage over near-term monetization.

"Over time, we believe Muse monetizes primarily through businesses paying to reach consumers through their agents, consistent with the economics of Meta's core advertising business, rather than consumer wallets," said the five-star analyst. He added that the checkout integrations with Shopify and PayPal reflect this framework.

While there are concerns that a rival could overtake Muse or the recently launched Muse Wonder and push Meta into a prolonged cycle of overinvestment, Morris remains bullish due to the company's strong record in scaling WhatsApp and competing strongly with TikTok via Reels as well as free distribution to an extensive user base.

Morris ranks No. 629 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.2%. See Meta Platforms KPIs on TipRanks.

Next is semiconductor company Marvell Technology. Earlier this month, Marvell held its Investor Day, where it raised its total addressable market estimate to $400 billion by 2030, reflecting solid AI-led demand tailwinds.

After hosting a non-deal roadshow with Marvell's senior management following the Investor Day, Stifel analyst Tore Svanberg reiterated a buy rating on the stock with a price target of $370.

The five-star analyst said that a major focus of the roadshow was the XPU and XPU Attach mix, as Marvell's Custom business expands toward its FY31 revenue target of $30 billion. In this regard, management reiterated that it expects a balanced mix of XPU and XPU Attach revenue in FY31, though it noted that either segment could grow rapidly enough to shift the mix toward 60/40 or 40/60.

While XPU drove most of the nearly $1.5 billion Custom revenue in FY26, Svanberg expects Marvell's XPU Attach business to grow substantially through the end of the decade, leading to a more balanced mix in this business. At the Investor Day event, the company raised its FY29 Custom target from more than $10 billion to over $12 billion, mainly driven by strength in the XPU Attach business.

Svanberg said that another key topic in the roadshow was whether Marvell can secure enough capacity to meet its raised targets. Management said that it met with suppliers ahead of Investor Day to share its five-year goals and received commitments to support these targets. The analyst believes that Marvell's supply chain commitments give it a competitive edge over rivals as customers seek reliable partners for large-scale infrastructure buildouts.

Svanberg ranks No. 91 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 58% of the time, delivering an average return of 31.8%. See Marvell Technology Statistics on TipRanks.

#2946. Saints at Bears Q&A: Dennis Allen Revenge Game Part 2

The Bears are back from their bye week and ready to welcome the New Orleans Saints to Soldier Field. The Bears are sitting at 7-2 and in prime position to make the playoffs. The Saints sit at 3-6, but fortunately for them, they are only a game and a half out of first in the weak NFC South.

To help get you set for this matchup, we sat down with Nic Jennings of our sister site, Canal Street Chronicles (SB Nation's New Orleans Saints site), to get you set for their opponent.

1. The Saints are sitting at 3-6, but from my outsider view, I feel like this team is close to clicking, potentially even the back half of this season and start winning some football games. What's the vibe around the Saints for the fan base?

The vibe around the fanbase isn't great right now. I don't think fans have given up on the players, but rather they've given up on Kellen Moore, Mickey Loomis, and Gayle Benson. The Saints are somehow only one game out of first place in the division, so there's still hope that they can turn things around and make a push. But it is frustrating to watch a roster with this much talent continue to fail to put everything together. At this point, I think changes need to be made within the regime, and until that happens, I expect the vibes around the fanbase to remain largely the same, win or lose.

2. I'll be honest, when Kellen Moore was hired, I didn't think much of him as a head coaching candidate, but I've come around on him a little. What did Saints fans think of the hire last year, ar and where are they at with him midway through 2026?

The initial reaction to Moore's hiring was that the Saints finally had another offensive-minded head coach in the building who could bring a refreshing change to the way things had been done. However, that optimism has long since faded. Moore is running out of excuses, and quite frankly, I don't think there is any way he can win the fanbase back at this point. Through his first 26 games, the Saints have trailed in every single one of those games, while failing to score in the first quarter 18 times. Two consistent issues have plagued Moore's teams across both seasons: slow starts that force the Saints to claw their way back into games and an offense that continues to look dysfunctional, with a putrid run game forcing Tyler Shough to carry the team on his back. At this point, the frustration has gone beyond results, and fans are rightfully questioning whether Moore is the guy to lead this team moving forward.

3. I've never been a fan of how Mickey Loomis has handled the cap and the rebuild/reload (whatever you want to call it) of this roster. Are Saints fans still behind Loomis or do they want to see a change?

Yes, the fanbase still wants a change in the front office, but it's not necessarily because of the financial side of things. The Saints are expected to finally be out of "cap hell" next season and back to operating with more financial flexibility. However, Mickey Loomis' draft-day decisions and head coaching hires since Sean Payton's departure have taken their toll on the fanbase. For a team that has been in a rebuilding phase, it's difficult to get out of that cycle without consistently drafting impactful players, and that's been one of the biggest issues. The tricky part is that Loomis is the NFL's longest-tenured general manager at 25 years and has won a Super Bowl with the franchise. Given his history and relationship with the organization, I don't see Gayle Benson moving on from him unless he decides to step down himself. That's what makes the situation so complicated.

4. Tyler Shough certainly looks like he's taking strides this year, but he's also 27 years old. Do you think Shough is a legit NFL QB that can lead this Saints offense for the next 5 to 7 years?

Absolutely. Shough is the real deal, and his teammates rally behind him. He's been one of the few consistent bright spots for the Saints this season, along with Chris Olave, Chase Young, and Carl Granderson.

Shough showed a lot of promise in his limited starts as a rookie, then attacked the offseason and blew expectations out of the water. He's carried that same momentum into this season. His ball placement, ability to stretch the field with his arm, poise in the pocket, and ability to extend plays with his legs give the Saints a dimension offensively that they haven't really had. And most importantly, he has that clutch gene. He's shown he can make plays when the Saints need them most.

I do think there are still things he can improve on, and he's had a bit of a rough stretch over the last two weeks. Like I previously mentioned, the offense has developed a habit of starting slowly, and while I'd put more of that on Kellen Moore, Shough still plays a role in getting the offense into a rhythm. Though,gh to Shough's credit, the Saints have a nonexistent rushing attack, which hasn't eased the pressure on Shough and the passing game one bit.

But overall, I've seen more than enough to believe he can be the long-term answer in New Orleans.

As for his age, to put things into perspective, Drew Brees joined the Saints at 27 years old, and it felt like he was in New Orleans for a lifetime. Even as a 26-year-old rookie, Shough was visibly more prepared than his fellow rookies. I view his age as a benefit rather than a drawback and believe he has a long career ahead of him.

5. What is one player on the Saints offense and one player on the Saints defense that may not be a headliner that you think can make an impact on Sunday? Do you have a prediction for the game?

After the Saints invested a fourth-round pick in him last year to pry him away from Denver, Vele has come in and become the contested-catch slot receiver the Saints have been searching for since Michael Thomas.

He's quickly become one of Tyler Shough's favorite targets and has established himself as the go-to guy in third-and-long situations.

With Jordyn Tyson back in the mix alongside Chris Olave, Vele's production has dwindled a bit over the past two games. That said, I believe his opportunities will come, and he's proven he can take advantage of them.

For a six-foot-five receiver to move the way he does and possess that type of body control is truly remarkable.

On defense, keep an eye on second-year defensive back, Jonas Sanker. He consistently finds his way to the football and has been impressive in his first year starting in the slot. He returned a fumble for a touchdown against the Vikings and had another return for a touchdown in Week 1 against the Lions that was called back after Jared Goff apparently "intended" to throw the football forward.

With Justin Reid now out of the picture in New Orleans, perhaps we see Sanker move back to his natural position at safety, where he spent his rookie season.

#2944. Jensen Huang's — and Nvidia's — legal exposure balloons

The still-unraveling scandal of former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's extramarital affairs and the tech giant's potential role in its advanced semiconductor chips ending up in China present a legal minefield for Huang and Nvidia.

Federal and state prosecutors and Nvidia employees may target Huang and the company in court, where some shareholders have already taken action.

That's on top of a rapidly growing pile of probes by Republicans who control Congress now and Democrats who will have majorities in both the House and Senate come January.

"It's not going to get any better for Jensen Huang; it's going to get far worse," said Salvatore Graziano, a New York securities litigation attorney at Bernstein, Litowitz, Berger & Grossmann. His firm is looking at shareholder lawsuit opportunities against Nvidia and Huang.

[Huang] "faces the Department of Justice, the Securities and Exchange Commission and private litigation," Graziano said. "The criminal exposure is very serious. The SEC could bar him from being an officer. And the potential liability [from shareholder] lawsuits can be very large."

Huang's and Nvidia's legal exposure stems from three issues that led up to his shock resignation Nov. 12.

There's his alleged affair with suspected Chinese spy Alice Huang, who worked for Megaspeed, a Singapore-based company under investigation for diverting advanced Nvidia semiconductor chips to China in violation of U.S. export rules. That, in turn, has raised questions about whether Huang and Nvidia were aware of any diversions.

There are Huang's two other alleged affairs with Nvidia employees.

And there is the sharp drop in the company's stock price in the wake of the scandal. Nvidia's share price has fallen 19% since Nov. 8, when Huang sent a company-wide apology acknowledging extramarital affairs.

Huang last week retained the law firm Paul, Weiss and its attorneys Melinda Haag and Walter Brown to represent him. Haag is a former U.S. attorney in San Francisco, and Brown is also a former federal prosecutor.

Huang's spokesman at Trident, a crisis communications firm, declined to comment when asked if Haag and Brown are representing him in any potential criminal case and other questions about his legal exposure.

Nvidia did not respond to a request for comment. Last week, Nvidia retained the law firm Wachtell, Lipton, Rosen & Katz to conduct an independent investigation into Huang allegedly being targeted by a spy, as U.S. intelligence reportedly has concluded.

The civil liability Huang and Nvidia could face is underscored by a hearing Graziano, the securities attorney, attended Tuesday in U.S. District Court in San Francisco. A judge at the hearing approved a $175 million settlement from Seagate Technology Holdings to resolve a shareholder lawsuit alleging it concealed sales of hard-disk drives to Huawei in China in violation of export bans.

The Seagate settlement came three years after the Commerce Department's Bureau of Industry and Security — which is investigating Megaspeed's business with Nvidia — imposed a $300 million penalty on Seagate for its sales to Huawei.

Graziano said his law firm is "actively looking at securities law liability for Nvidia, Jensen Huang and other senior executives in the company given all the recent developments regarding their [potential] knowledge and facilitation of illegal chip sales to Chinese entities."

There is already a pending class-action shareholder lawsuit in San Francisco federal court against Nvidia and Huang by the Rosen Law Firm, for allegedly misleading and false statements related to Megaspeed and Alice Huang.

"Several hundreds of investors have contacted us and expressed an interest to join the case — investors of all walks of life, from mom and pops to investors with very large losses," Rosen attorney Phil Kim told CNBC in an email.

Katie Sweeten, a former federal prosecutor in the DOJ's National Security Division, said the question of "who knew what and when, and what was [Huang's] role" is potentially the biggest risk for Nvidia and him.

"Now DOJ can leverage their criminal authorities," Sweeten, a partner at Scale LLP, told CNBC's Kelly Evans on "The Exchange."

"They can bring grand jury subpoenas, search warrants for emails, bank accounts, personal devices, computers, things like that," Sweeten said.

While it is "not illegal to, you know, sleep with a spy ... it is illegal to pass certain types of information to a counterintelligence officer," she said.

Sweeten also pointed to potential export control violations, which "could be very serious."

She noted the DOJ can indict a company as well as its officials if there is evidence of criminality. Export control violations carry a maximum possible criminal sentence of 20 years in prison and "really large fines," she said.

"You can't send the company to prison, but they can face fines, civil penalties, debarment — where you can't export items down the line," she said.

One example of that came in July 2025, when the DOJ obtained a guilty plea and an agreement to pay more than $140 million from Cadence Design Systems for violating the Export Control Reform Act by unlawfully exporting semiconductor design tools to China's National University of Defense Technology, a school led by China's Central Military Commission.

Another potential legal risk for Huang comes from his planned appearances before Congress.

Huang is scheduled to give a closed-door interview Dec. 1 to three House committees. Senate banking committee Chair Tim Scott, R-S.C., wants Huang's testimony before that panel to be public, and told CNBC Tuesday he hopes to have that happen "before Christmas."

Sweeten said Huang "has to be very careful" about what he says there because of the risk of being criminally charged with lying to Congress, and of saying something "that absolutely can become part of a criminal or civil investigation."

"I expect Huang to take the Fifth on most questions" from Congress, Jonathan Kanter, a former assistant attorney general in the DOJ's Antitrust Division, told CNBC, meaning Huang will likely use his constitutional right against giving testimony that could be used against him in a criminal case.

"With a Justice Department criminal investigation into Megaspeed already underway and more investigations possible, anything he tells Congress could be used against him," Kanter said. "His lawyers are likely to advise him against taking that risk."

Ben Heineman Jr., a former senior vice president and general counsel of General Electric, and a senior fellow at Harvard University, told CNBC that Nvidia's board will be scrutinized over its actions before it pushed Huang to resign.

Heineman cited the board's apparent lack of succession planning and what looks like weak oversight of Huang, including relating to personal conduct such as the two alleged affairs with Nvidia employees and with an executive of an Nvidia customer.

"When there is a sort of reasonable suspicion, they need to investigate it, and they don't appear to have done that," Heineman said.

Nvidia and Huang could face investigation from state attorneys general, including in California, where the company is headquartered.

On Tuesday, California Attorney General Rob Bonta told Bloomberg Television that the situation at Nvidia with Huang is "something that we're interested in." Bonta's office later told CNBC, "We are monitoring the situation."

Huang's alleged affairs with Nvidia employees also open the possibility of workplace litigation.

Ari Wilkenfeld, a Washington-area employment lawyer, said a consensual relationship between a chief executive and a subordinate "is not in and of itself illegal."

"The liability never comes from the existence of the relationship alone. It comes from whether it was genuinely welcome, what job benefits flowed from it, and what it did to everyone else in the building," Wilkenfeld said. "California law recognizes that so-called sexual favoritism can create a hostile environment for an entire workforce.

"The affair is Huang's problem," Wilkenfeld said. "What, if anything, Nvidia did once it knew is the company's problem."

#2943. Colts officially place Daniel Jones on waivers

Daniel Jones is officially on the waiver wire.

The NFL transaction report has gone out this afternoon, and Jones appears as a waiver from the Colts. After the trade deadline, every player who gets released by his team has to go on waivers before he becomes a free agent.

That means any team that wants Jones on his current contract can claim him, but that is extraordinarily unlikely to happen. As the waiver transaction notes, Jones is a vested veteran with a fully guaranteed salary for the rest of this season. No team is going to want to claim that salary.

Once he clears waivers, however, he'll be an unrestricted free agent, free to sign with any team, and it won't be surprising if some team wants to add him for veteran depth. Jones has been terrible this season, but he's been a competent starter at times in his career, and a team that wants an experienced backup may be interested in signing him for a minimal salary.

Jones was also waived almost exactly two years ago, on November 22, 2024. He cleared waivers and signed with the Vikings' practice squad before signing with the Colts after that season.

#2941. A wave of 'pent-up quits' could boost turnover in the job market, expert says: 'The labor market is a bit like a coiled spring'

Frustrated workers could trigger a wave of turnover, creating a shift in the sluggish job market, according to experts at the job boards Indeed and Glassdoor.

That sluggishness, or what labor economists call a low-hire, low-fire economy, means that fewer people are getting hired, and fewer workers are leaving their jobs — even as a growing share of job holders report frustration with their career opportunities. In 2026, 21% of workers report feeling dissatisfied with their career prospects, according to Glassdoor reviewer data. Dissatisfied workers rated their jobs at a one or two out of five stars.

A stagnant job market can discourage even the unhappiest of workers from quitting, but hiring could soon ramp up. Indeed's Job Posting Index, or JPI, which tracks the level of job postings on Indeed relative to a pre-pandemic baseline, saw year-over-year growth for the first time in September 2026 since 2022. The upward trend suggests that the job market is "heating up," Glassdoor chief economist Daniel Zhao tells CNBC Make It.

As more opportunities open up, job holders might regain the confidence to exit, turning into "a wave of quits and turnover," Zhao says. Turnover could also help those who are out of a job secure employment as roles that were once filled become vacant.

"The labor market is a bit like a coiled spring right now," Zhao said at a press event Tuesday in New York City. "There's a lot of pent-up potential energy, and it's unclear exactly which way that that spring is going to release that energy."

In a healthy job market, employee dissatisfaction can forecast turnover rates. When employee dissatisfaction reached 17% in April 2022, per Glassdoor reviews, quit rates also reached their highest levels at 3%, seasonally adjusted, according to data from Bureau of Labor Statistics Job Openings and Labor Turnover Survey data published by the St. Louis Federal Reserve.

However, when workers feel anxious about their future job prospects, turnover can stall. Though workers feel dissatisfied with their jobs at higher rates in September 2026 than they did in April 2022, only 2.1% of workers quit their job in the month, according to the BLS JOLTS.

"There aren't those opportunities to climb the career ranks internally or externally," Zhao says of the job market. "There's no outlet for the frustration or dissatisfaction that [workers] might be feeling."

Employee turnover was one focus of Indeed and Glassdoor's 2027 Hiring and Worklife Trends Report, published Wednesday. Their research, which assumed a consistent relationship between worker dissatisfaction and resignations based on historical data, found that the quit rate is 0.22% lower than what employee sentiment would typically suggest. While the figure seems small, it is equivalent to an additional 349,000 quits per month.

"Workers are very hesitant to quit, especially without something lined up, because hiring is very sluggish, and long-term unemployment is looming over them," Zhao tells CNBC Make It.

While unemployment levels are stable, people are remaining jobless for longer, according to data from the BLS. Those who face long-term unemployment, job seekers who have been out of work for 27 weeks or more, accounted for 27.6% of all unemployed people in October 2026.

Even if more jobs open, turnover across sectors could vary. Industries that are highly exposed to artificial intelligence like software development, engineering and banking previously dragged Indeed's JPI down, meaning employers in these sectors posted fewer jobs. Indeed's data shows that postings in these sectors have since seen growth, and could result in what experts call "pent-up quits," which occur when dissatisfied workers leave their jobs once market conditions improve. Job posts on Indeed for employers in industries with less AI exposure like healthcare have softened, according to experts.

However, business and professional services, which includes banking, shed 27,000 jobs in October 2026, according to data from ADP Research.

"As we look ahead to 2027, that increase in demand might not necessarily translate into more hires, and it also might not be enough hires to give workers the confidence that they can actually quit their jobs and move on to something better," Zhao says.

Even as the job market appears to be trending in a positive direction, further economic pressure could continue to place a strain on employers and their hiring plans.

"Nobody was really expecting 2026 to come with the geopolitical challenges that have happened and the the impact that has had on energy prices and inflation," Zhao says. "And so there's always the outside chance that something happens in 2027 that derails the job market from the path that it's on."

#2939. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Global stock markets remain volatile amid persistent geopolitical tensions and concerns about the sustainability of AI trade. Given this backdrop, investors seeking steady income can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios.

Tracking recommendations from top Wall Street analysts can help investors pick attractive stocks, as these experts assess a company's ability to pay dividends consistently.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Chevron (CVX) is a dividend king. The company has increased its dividend for 39 consecutive years. At a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share (annualized dividend of $7.12 per share), CVX offers a yield of 3.14%. Chevron will announce its third-quarter results on October 30.

In an earnings preview note on U.S. oil majors, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on Chevron with a price target of $241. The analyst is focused on the company's international growth prospects across Latin America, the Middle East, West Africa, and the Eastern Mediterranean.

Specifically, Mehta highlighted management's commentary on solid execution in Venezuela, with gross production expected to more than double to nearly 600 kbd (kilo barrels per day) by 2031 via three joint ventures. He also noted a recent oil and gas condensate discovery in offshore Angola and is awaiting commercial updates on the West Qurna 2 and Nasiriyah projects in Iraq.

Furthermore, Mehta expects CVX to report improved productivity and capital efficiency across domestic shale assets. He expects the improvement mainly in the Permian Basin, where Chevron projects advanced chemical treatments to drive about a 10% recovery uplift in new wells and bring down capital spend per barrel by about 25% year-over-year. Overall, Mehta expects Chevron to report Q3 2026 EPS of $4.95, above the consensus of $4.89.

"While unfavorable timing effects and volatile commodity prices may present quarterly earnings headwinds, we highlight healthy U.S. refining margins and strong asset utilization as partial offsets," said Mehta.

Mehta ranks No. 381 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Chevron KPIs on TipRanks.

Kinetik Holdings (KNTK) is a fully integrated, pure-play, midstream company operating in the Delaware Basin. Earlier this month, the company declared a dividend of $0.81 per share, payable on October 30, 2026. With an annualized dividend of $3.24 per share, KNTK offers a yield of 5.82%. Kinetik will announce its Q3 earnings on November 4, with a conference call scheduled the next day.

Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, Goldman Sachs analyst John Mackay reiterated a buy rating on Kinetik stock and slightly raised his price target to $58 from $57. He expects the company to report Q3 2026 EBITDA of $279 million, higher than the Street's expectation of $270 million but lower than the second quarter's EBITDA of $281 million.

The 5-star analyst expects Kinetik's Q3 performance to gain from higher gas volumes after notable curtailments in the second quarter. Mackay also expects the quarter to benefit from higher production and steady commodity margins, even though some lower-margin contracts and weaker Waha spread marketing gains could weigh on results.

Overall, Mackay increased his Q3 2026 estimates to reflect higher volumes, driven by rapid Permian egress pipeline commissioning, stronger throughput at Kings Landing I, and higher M2M commodity prices, offset by increased costs.

"As natural gas takeaway constraints ease across the Permian, investor focus centers on KNTK's YE26 volume trajectory and durability of its multi-year growth," said Mackay.

Mackay ranks No. 569 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.9%. See Kinetik's Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Moving to Crescent Energy (CRGY), an exploration and production company with activities focused in the Eagle Ford, Permian, and Uinta Basins. With a quarterly dividend of $0.12 per share (annualized dividend of $0.48 per share), CRGY's dividend yield stands at 3.56%. Earlier this month, Crescent announced an agreement to acquire Eagle Ford assets from Devon Energy (DVN).

Heading into Q3 earnings on Nov. 2, Evercore analyst Stephen Richardson reiterated a buy rating on Crescent stock and slightly lowered the price target to $17 from $18, citing dilution from new shares.

"CRGY's recent acquisition of DVN's Eagle Ford assets marks a strategic, growth-oriented move, potentially enhancing cash flow performance," said Richardson.

The 5-star analyst noted that the acquisition of the Eagle Ford assets at a net purchase price of $3.85 billion will add about 68 MBoe/d of net production and over 600 net Tier 1 locations. Richardson added that a solid track record of synergies from past deals in the Eagle Ford region increases confidence about Crescent achieving efficiencies with this deal.

Additionally, Richardson noted that the financial implications of the acquisition seem impressive, with a projected increase in cash flow per share of about 9% for 2027 to 2029.

The analyst believes that Crescent is not just expanding its business through acquisitions but positioning itself as a more durable company, regardless of market conditions.

Richardson ranks No. 478 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.2%. See Crescent Energy Statistics on TipRanks.

#2938. Forget Anthropic: In Europe, the IPO hype is about a blue lagoon

While investors are fixated on Anthropic's blockbuster stock market debut, a very different IPO is making waves in Europe: Iceland's Blue Lagoon, the operator of one of the world's most famous geothermal spas.

Blue Lagoon Corp. raised roughly 14.3 billion Icelandic kronur ($110 million) through the offering, valuing the Icelandic geothermal spa operator at about $787 million, according to the company's investor presentation.

The Blue Lagoon traces its origins to 1992, when Blue Lagoon Ltd. was founded to study the water in the Blue Lagoon, an open-air pool of warm, milky-blue water set in a black lava field in southwestern Iceland. It grew into a business spanning a spa, hotels and skincare made with ingredients from the lagoon.

Shares will trade under the ticker "BLUE" on one of Europe's smallest stock exchanges, Nasdaq Iceland Main Market, part of the Nasdaq Nordic group, which also operates exchanges in Sweden, Denmark and Finland.

"Wellness is the new luxury, and we know that the wellness economy is growing faster than the economy as a whole, so we have tailwind from that," CEO Sigríður Margrét Oddsdóttir told CNBC on Thursday.

"Now is the right time for us to list," she added. "We are a company that thinks long-term, we have a clear vision for the next decade, and being publicly listed gives investors the opportunity to participate in our journey."

The company's operations are centered on Iceland's Reykjanes Peninsula, where, as well as the Blue Lagoon geothermal spa, it runs the Silica Hotel, the Retreat luxury hotel and spa, and the Michelin-starred Moss restaurant.

It also operates the Kerlingarfjöll Highland Base and Hrauneyjar properties in Iceland's highlands, and sells Blue Lagoon-branded skincare products internationally.

The 34-year-old company has averaged 11% annual revenue growth over the past decade, and next year is expected to be a record, with revenue growing by more than half, Oddsdóttir said.

The company plans to use the raised capital to fund expanding its existing properties, acquiring businesses, and developing new destinations.

In total, nearly 4,000 subscriptions were received in the initial public offering, worth about 15.3 billion kronur, about 7% more than the total size of the offering, the company said, citing strong demand from smaller investors, including retail investors.

Iceland's relatively small stock market had just 27 companies on its main exchange at the end of 2025, according to Nasdaq. The market is dominated by banks, seafood companies and other domestically focused firms.

The global wellness industry reached $7.5 trillion in 2025, up 8.9% from the previous year, according to the Global Wellness Institute. Spending on thermal and mineral springs grew 10.4% over the same period, with the institute forecasting annual growth of nearly 10% for the sector through 2030.

Tourism accounted for nearly 9% of Iceland's GDP in 2025, and is growing, according to Statistics Iceland. The country welcomed around 2.3 million international visitors that year, with the U.S. as its largest source market.

#2936. Tirico caps fourth-quarter comeback as Seneca earned state title berth

TABERNACLE — One more time, Seneca's field leader connected with the team's vocal leader.

Just 27 seconds remained in Friday night's state semifinal with Burlington Township when quarterback Anthony Tirico lofted a perfect strike to Chase Horner in the right corner of the end zone. Horner made the grab in tight coverage to catapult the Golden Eagles into the lead.

An interception by Shane Richardson as time expired wrapped up a heart-pounding 30-27 Seneca victory, as the Golden Eagles head to their first-ever state final.

"It's just trust," Tirico said. "(Chase and I) are brothers on and off the field. So, in a situation like that, with the game on the line, there's no one else I want making that catch, and that's why I gave him a chance."

Seneca will take on Old Tappan (12-0) next Saturday at 4 p.m. in the NJSIAA Group 3 State Final at MetLife Stadium.

A tremendous season for Burlington Township (7-6), a season that included the team's first sectional title since 1977, came to a close.

The game saw four lead changes in the first half alone and six total. Seneca trailed 27-17 going into the fourth quarter.

"That was the best win in my eight years," head coach Anthony Diorio said. "That was unbelievable. The boys just kept fighting."

Trailing 27-24, with a 1st-and-10 on the Falcons' 18, Tirico went to Horner. Diorio was not surprised.

"He's the vocal leader of our team. He just goes until there's nothing left in the tank," Diorio said of Horner. "He just went up and made a play, and it was awesome."

Tirico (9-of-21, 142 yds) threw three touchdown passes in the contest, including two to Horner (6-for-56 yards receiving; 10-for-51 rushing).

"I looked at the play call and I knew I was the only option — I had to go get it," Horner said. "I just had full trust in my quarterback to throw me a good ball and give me a fighting chance. When adversity strikes, we always prevail."

The senior also gathered in a 20-yard swing pass down the left sideline for a score early in the second quarter, which provided a 7-3 Seneca lead. The two touchdowns bookended one heck of a contest.

"Chase is a baller," Tirico said. "(On the winning score), I knew the line would give me the time I needed and Chase would make a play, and that's a play I knew he was gonna make."

The versatile Tirico also rushed for 138 yards including a four-yard score and hit Nate Sauerwald for a 67-yard touchdown that provided a 17-14 advantage in the second quarter. Even three uncharacteristic interceptions did not sink Seneca's poised senior quarterback.

"He had a couple tough plays in the third and fourth quarters," Diorio said. "So for him to bounce back and keep fighting and make that kind of a throw (on the final TD), that's impressive."

What set the winning drive in motion was a big play by the Seneca defense. Hit as he threw, Falcons quarterback Bryce Carter was intercepted by Peyton Preston, who came up with the game's most pivotal takeaway. Ten plays later, Seneca was in the end zone.

Nate Sauerwald made a huge impact on his three receptions. They went for a total of 86 yards, including a touchdown.

Aiden Binns pounded away for 152 yards on his 22 carries for Burlington Township. His four-yard touchdown run in the second quarter gave the Falcons a 14-10 lead before Seneca pushed back in front before the half.

Seneca got a shot in the arm when Brayden Van Horn pounced on a muffed punt by Burlington Township just as the fourth quarter got underway. Tirico dashed in from five yards out moments later to slice the deficit to 27-24.

Along with Binns' output, Burlington Township got a nice performance from Carter, who threw for 151 yards and a pair of scores. He found a wide open Mekhi Curry for a 33-yard TD in the second quarter and later hooked up with Gabriel Atz for a 63-yard touchdown that gave the visitors their 27-17 lead before Seneca made its final push.

Gerald Sackie had Burlington Township's other touchdown, a four-yard rush.

Hunter Donovan booted a 23-yard field goal for Seneca to open the scoring.

"You come out here, and it's a small community, you know?," Tirico said. "And yet these stands are full of people who love and care for this program. I couldn't ask for a better feeling."

#2935. Brandon Ingram expected to make season debut for Clippers on Monday vs. Rockets

Brandon Ingram is expected to make his 2026-27 season debut for the Los Angeles Clippers on Monday against the Houston Rockets, according to ESPN's Shams Charania.

Before opening training camp, the Clippers announced that Ingram had been diagnosed with a partially torn Achilles tendon in his right leg. The partial tear was discovered while the two-time All-Star underwent surgery to remove a bone spur from his right heel in May.

That heel injury caused Ingram to miss Games 6 and 7 of the Toronto Raptors' first-round playoff defeat to the Cleveland Cavaliers.

Ingram was traded by Toronto to the Clippers as part of a package including Gradey Dick, unprotected 2031 and 2033 first-round selections, a 2027 first-round swap and second-round picks in 2030 and 2033 in exchange for Kawhi Leonard.

The deal was originally agreed upon in late June and expected to become official on July 6 when the NBA's moratorium on transactions ended. However, the trade was put on hold amid the NBA's investigation into the Clippers allegedly evading the league's salary cap while signing Leonard to a three-year, $103 million free-agent contract in July 2019.

Both the Clippers and Raptors agreed that the trade wouldn't be completed until the league's probe was completed. The deal finally went through in mid-September, approximately two weeks before NBA teams opened their training camps.

The Clippers knew about Ingram's Achilles tear — repaired in the same procedure that removed his bone spur — before agreeing to the trade, according to interim president of basketball operations Trent Redden. Redden told reporters that Ingram would require about six fewer months of rehab with a partial tear and expected he would play for the Clippers this season.

Ingram averaged 21.5 points, 5.6 rebounds and 3.7 assists for the Raptors last season after being acquired from the New Orleans Pelicans in early 2025. Shortly after joining Toronto, he signed a three-year, $120 million contract extension.

During his 10 NBA seasons, Ingram has averaged 19.8 points, 5.2 rebounds and 4.2 assists while shooting 37% on 3-pointers. He was the Los Angeles Lakers' 2016 first-round pick (No. 2 overall) out of Duke. Ingram played three seasons for the Lakers before being traded to New Orleans as part of a three-team deal that sent Anthony Davis to L.A.

Without Ingram, the Clippers are off to a 4-10 start that currently has them as the third-worst team in the Western Conference. The team has also been without Bradley Beal (knee) and Jordan Miller (shoulder) during the opening weeks of the season.

#2933. Cathedral tops Bloomington South football in semi-state battle for ages

INDIANAPOLIS — There was no doubt in their minds, they were going for the win.

It was how the whole game had been played on both sides. Bloomington South and Cathedral football were not bowing to the will of the other.

So after Jonathan Winstead made one last spectacular catch at the 2-yard line and Duncan Combs spiked the ball with 3 seconds left, Combs got under center, but Cathedral called timeout. Head coach Gabe Johnson and his seniors had a decision to make down three — go for the tie, or go the glory of a long-awaited final four win.

The offense went back out. The ball was snapped to Combs, who pitched to senior running back Hayden Clark on his right, but Cathedral clogged all the escape routes and began celebrating after a hard-fought 24-21 win in their IHSAA Class 5A Semi-state game at Arsenal Tech on Friday, Nov. 20.

More: Scott Fischer steps down as Edgewood football coach

"He asked us, 'Do we got it?' I believe in our team, I said, 'Yes, let's go for it.'" Combs said. "To come up short by a yard sucks, but I wouldn't have had it any other way.

"Either go out trying your best to win it all, or lose hard with it. It's tough."

The heartache and tears were real. South (10-3) was making a fourth straight semi-state appearance, and none were as close as this one. South could taste it when they broke the sideline huddle and lined up at the 2.

"(The field goal) was for a moment," Johnson said. "But when you have 11 guys who say, 'Let's go for it. Let's go win it.' Then you've got to do it.

"I thought we were going to score. We had momentum at the at point."

"We just wanted an opportunity to play for a state title," Johnson said. "It was the right call to go for it and we didn't get it. There were a lot of calls I think I made that maybe didn't contribute to us being in a better position tonight. That's on me. But our kids fought hard and we gave ourselves to play for a state championship."

Cathedral will now get a chance to add to its haul of 14 as the ghosts of 1998 live on for South.

"They're hurting right now," Johnson said. "There nothing I can tell them to make it feel better. It just has to work its way through your body and leave. It's going to take some time because I know they wanted it badly. And they gave everything they had against a great football team."

The game was everything to be expected from the quality of teams on the field.

"They're a really great team and I think they would say the same about us," Combs said. "When two great teams come together, that's what happens, you get great football. I'm sure they'll do well at state."

Easton Bellini's third-down sack forced Cathedral's punt and set up the dramatic finish.

South got the ball back on its own 15 and needed a fourth-and-1 conversion by Clark to start the drive. It was everything a three-year senior starting QB dreams of and Combs delivered, going 7-of-8 for 77 yards.

First, 5 yards to Connor Parker, 17 to Winstead, 13 to Mayson Lewis, 3 to Clark then 17 to Parker, who just drug his left to the 18-yard line with 13 seconds left. Then 16 to Winstead at the 2.

"Our defense did a great job of holding them there," Combs said. "And when we got the ball, we only had one thing to do and that was move the ball down the field.

"I put faith in all my guys, my O-linemen, my receivers and we did a great job marching down there. I appreciate the coaches for putting faith in us for going for it on the 1-yard line."

Cathedral's starting offensive line goes 275, 285, 290, 310, 330 pounds and leaned on it well, rushing for 155 yards.

But the Irish also freely use junior QB Luke Finn (7-10, 102 yards passing) and sophomore QB Emmett Knowles (13-17, 138) interchangeably throughout the game. Knowles' live arm gave some pep to the passing attack, though both tossed for TDs critical to the win.

The first came after South punted with 1:21 left in the first half. Starting at his own 28, Finn hit a big 48-yard completion and connected on two more passes before finding Ollie Lewis open for a 5-yard score that made it 14-7 at halftime.

"That was not a great series for us," Johnson said.

Cathedral responded in quick fashion again in the third, after Mayson Lewis flew 99 yards with the second half kickoff to tie it.

All night, both teams took chances, going for it on fourth down in their own territory but this time, it backfired spectacularly for the Panthers, who gave the ball back on their own 34.

Next snap, Lewis, a thorn in South's side all night with 11 catches for 119 yards, was wide open and just like that, it was 21-14 Irish.

"I did not like my call to go for it on fourth down there," Johnson said. "I felt good that we could get a yard or two and we didn't."

Cathedral had its own fourth-down letdown when Finn was stopped at the Irish 44. But South couldn't take advantage. The Irish mixed run and pass well in a 14-play drive that started at their own 20 and ended with a 25-yard field goal and a 24-14 edge.

South stormed back, with Clark sneaking out of the back field for two big completions, the last of 30 yards, to score with 6:22 left.

"It was a great group of seniors," Johnson said. "It's as good a four-year span as there's been at Bloomington South minus the state championships.

So, it says a lot for them. It's completely on them. They put in the work."

It was getting tougher for Johnson to hold back tears. South went 29-10 over the past three years thanks in part to this year's seniors.

"We knew we had a great chance coming into this week," Combs said. "And leave it all out there on the field. We've been brothers for years now, going back to middle school, elementary, I've known these dudes my whole life. And I wouldn't want to do it with any other class."

#2932. Jimmy Garoppolo’s reunion may not be with the Rams

If the Los Angeles Rams lost Matthew Stafford for any amount of time this season, would you trust the current backup quarterback options?

Would you feel any better if an experienced veteran, like Jimmy Garoppolo, could step in?

There's nothing to indicate Stafford is at any increased risk of an injury or missing snaps for any reason. But as the NFL playoffs approach and the margin for error shrinks closer to zero, it's hard not to think about protecting yourself against calamity. That's especially true for a Rams team that has dealt with tons of injuries this year, including another serious one to a starting offensive lineman just this week.

This is relevant now because Garoppolo, who spent the 2024 and 2025 seasons in Los Angeles, is suddenly being linked to other playoff contenders, like another one of his former teams, the New England Patriots.

After initial reporting on the matter by Jordan Schultz, ESPN's Jeremy Fowler discussed the situation this week and threw some cold water on the topic but stopped short of completely killing the idea. Specifically, he mentioned that there are questions about whether or not the 12-year veteran has any actual desire to play again.

He also mentioned, however, that Garoppolo enjoyed playing on the west coast. That could work in the Rams' favor, obviously.

Don't get it twisted, this is not an earth-shattering kind of hypothetical personnel scenario. This is a quarterback who had good moments in several years as a starter, but isn't exactly a Hall of Fame candidate. He would purely be an insurance policy if Los Angeles wanted to pursue that route. And realistically, if Stafford missed more than a game or a couple games at the absolute most, things would get dire in a hurry for the Rams.

Still, the alternatives currently are Stetson Bennett, who's taken a whopping 10 offensive snaps over four seasons (all of which have come this year) and Ty Simpson, the first-round pick who still hasn't seen the field and who is a known project player.

This idea isn't completely out of left field, either, as it was discussed over the offseason as the backup job was still in flux in the wake of the Simpson pick in the NFL draft. There isn't much that's controversial about re-signing a generally inoffensive backup quarterback.

The Rams have enough personnel concerns that they likely don't have to go full bore after another reserve quarterback right now. But if push came to shove and they had to sign one out of desperation, Garoppolo still makes sense. That's the situation the Patriots are quite literally in right now with Drake Maye injured.

It would be a terrible scenario regardless, but a slightly less devastating one with a more reliable backup in the fray.

#2930. Anthropic raises $93.5 billion total as underwriters scoop up ‘greenshoe’ overallotment

Anthropic underwriters have officially exercised their overallotment of shares in the historic initial public offering, bringing the total raised to $93.5 billion, according to a release Friday.

The artificial intelligence company raised $81.3 billion on Wednesday and netted an additional $12.2 billion through the overallotment, surpassing SpaceX, which held a record IPO in June.

Anthropic said it sold an aggregate of 1,127,000,000 shares of its Class A common stock at $83 per share in the offering. It sold 980 million shares on Wednesday and its underwriters had the option to purchase up to an additional 147 million shares by Friday as part of the overallotment, which is commonly referred to as the "greenshoe."

Shares of the Claude maker opened trading at $111.50 on the Nasdaq on Thursday, but slid throughout the day and closed at $97.17. The stock sank 12% in premarket trading on Friday during a broad tech sell-off.

In addition to the sale of its Class A common stock, Anthropic closed a concurrent sale of its Class N common stock after a group of tech companies, including Advanced Micro Devices, Google, Microsoft, Nvidia and others, agreed to participate in a $26 billion private placement at the IPO price. Anthropic said Friday that aggregate gross proceeds from its IPO, including the private placement, totaled $119.5 billion, before deducting offering expenses.

Anthropic dodged an unforgiving series of obstacles on its road to the Nasdaq on Thursday, as investors brooded over Jensen Huang's shocking ouster at Nvidia, voters' stinging rebuke of the tech during the midterms and whether rising interest rates would stymie industry plans for a multitrillion-dollar AI infrastructure buildout.

CEO Dario Amodei co-founded the company in 2021 after he and a handful of other researchers, including his sister Daniela, quit their jobs at OpenAI, now Anthropic's chief rival. Anthropic is best known for developing a family of AI models called Claude, which have captivated the corporate world and fueled the company's historic rise.

The siblings celebrated the IPO as a "milestone" in an interview with CNBC on Thursday, but said there's more work ahead for Anthropic.

"For us, it's like one stop on the journey," Daniela Amodei said. "We think about this as, 'Wow, this is an incredible moment for Anthropic for our mission, but we're going to wake up tomorrow and we're going to be the same company.'"

#2929. Robinhood shares fall as crypto slump weighs on profits, offsetting gains in new businesses

Robinhood shares were down around 8% in pre-market trading on Wednesday as investors digested weaker-than-expected third-quarter earnings despite growth in options trading and prediction markets.

Here's how the brokerage performed in its quarter ended Sept. 30, compared with Wall Street estimates, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Robinhood's net income fell to $468 million, or 51 cents per share, from $556 million, or 61 cents per share, a year ago. Revenue represented a 12% increase from $1.27 billion in the same quarter last year.

Despite that gain, transaction-based revenue of $867 million came in below Wall Street estimates from StreetAccount of $879 million, putting focus on whether Robinhood can sustain momentum across its trading businesses and reduce its reliance on volatile market activity.

Robinhood has been diversifying its business as it tries to expand its brokerage app into a broader financial services platform. Options trading and event contracts have become increasingly important sources of transaction revenue, while the company has continued to invest in wealth management, subscriptions and other offerings.

Transaction-based revenue grew 19% from the same period last year to $867 million, driven primarily by a more than 500% increase in event contracts revenue. Additionally, options revenue grew 31% to $399 million and equities revenue rose 41% to $121 million.

Those gains were partially offset by cryptocurrency revenue, which fell 55% to $120 million.

Chief Financial Officer Shiv Verma told CNBC event contracts on crypto movements did well in the quarter, offering investors another way to express a view on crypto despite the slump in prices and spot trading.

Net deposits on the Robinhood platform totaled $15.7 billion for the quarter, equal to a 17% annualized growth rate based on total platform assets at the end of Q2 2026. Over the past year, net deposits totaled $71 billion, representing 21% growth compared with total platform assets at the end of Q3 2025.

—With reporting from CNBC's Robert Hum and Kate Rooney

#2927. Trump says he discussed Jensen Huang with Xi; Bessent tells CNBC Nvidia chip export policy is unchanged

President Donald Trump on Friday said he and Chinese leader Xi Jinping discussed their shared affinity for former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang during their talks in China this week. Earlier in the day, Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent said the ousted leader was not mentioned in separate talks he had with Chinese officials.

"I did discuss that with President Xi, and he said, 'Oh, brilliant, brilliant! He's a brilliant man.' He is," Trump said at a White House press conference.

Asked earlier Friday on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" if Huang, a close ally of Trump's, had come up in "any of your discussions with the Chinese," Bessent said "no," without elaborating.

A senior Trump administration official later said that both Trump's and Bessent's remarks were correct, and that both were addressing their own conversations. The official spoke on condition of anonymity to discuss those conversations.

Trump also reiterated that he would like to see Huang — who was replaced by interim CEO Colette Kress after resigning amid allegations of an extramarital affair with a Chinese spy — be restored as Nvidia's CEO.

"I would love to see Jensen be given a second chance," Trump said. "You know, we're a country of second chances, and I'd love to be able to see him. That's a very important company to us, and he put us in a position where we are literally 10 years advanced over other companies."

Nvidia's board is not considering bringing Huang back, a person familiar with the matter told CNBC earlier this week.

The president's comments came amid growing scrutiny of advanced U.S. chips, including those made by Nvidia, ending up in China either legally or by being diverted via third countries.

Both Trump's and Bessent's remarks are potentially an indication that the Trump administration may not focus on cracking down on China's efforts to acquire advanced chips.

Bessent suggested that U.S. policy allowing the sale of Nvidia's powerful H200 AI chips to China remains unchanged following the talks in Shenzhen this week.

"We've already agreed that the ... H200s are the threshold level of chips that could be sold to the Chinese, and, you know, that continues as planned," Bessent said.

"I wouldn't anticipate any change in the policy," he added after CNBC's Sara Eisen noted that Nvidia said it had stopped selling H200s to China.

The administration ignoring the subject of Huang's affair with a possible Chinese spy — whose Singapore-based company Megaspeed has been accused of acting as a cutout for Beijing — contrasts with the importance Trump has placed on beating China in the race to artificial intelligence dominance. It could suggest that despite his support for the U.S. AI industry, Trump is less concerned with addressing China's ongoing efforts to acquire U.S. semiconductors.

Trump has also defended Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick, who has come under fire from multiple directions for reportedly slow-walking export restrictions on Chinese chip sales. "I understand both sides of it," Trump said on Monday when he also said he's like to see Huang back as CEO.

Bessent's appearance on CNBC came after Trump's return from a tour through Asia that saw him meet with Xi and Russian President Vladimir Putin in Shenzhen, China.

The meeting was historic as the first time the leaders of the three countries had met without other nations' counterparts. But the trilateral summit appeared to yield few deliverables on any number of the most pressing global issues related to the countries, including the Ukraine and Iran wars or the ongoing AI race.

Bessent told CNBC on Friday that there was a "robust discussion" between Trump and Xi on trade policy and AI.

They "agreed that humans should always be involved in any military decisions that SI makes, and the SI should not be allowed to be autonomous for major military actions," he said, abbreviating the term "super intelligence," Trump's attempted rebrand of AI.

In a CNBC appearance last week, Bessent lamented the "leakage" of U.S.-made AI chips to China and blamed the Commerce Department, led by Lutnick, for slow-walking export controls on suspected Chinese cutouts like Megaspeed.

Multiple Trump administration leaders have long sought to impose export restrictions on Megaspeed, but Commerce "has the pen" and "you will have to ask them about their internal processes," Bessent said in that interview.

On Friday, however, Bessent was much more tight-lipped.

Asked if there is a dispute between him and Lutnick over how aggressively the U.S. should be pursuing Chinese export controls, Bessent said, "I think we're all committed to following President Trump's instructions."

"I think we are going to see that the ... proper chip restrictions are in place, and the remote access has been curtailed," he added. Commerce recently took steps to close one of China's paths to accessing AI processors.

Correction: Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent spoke to CNBC's "Squawk on the Street." A previous version of this story misstated the name of the show.

Editor's note: This story and headline have been updated following additional information provided by the Trump administration about the nature of Bessent's and Trump's meetings in China.
