#2925. Dominick Barlow probable, Jaylen Brown out for Sixers vs. Bucks

Reinforcements are finally on their way for the Sixers. Dominick Barlow appears ready to make his season debut, as he's listed as probable for Saturday's game against the Milwaukee Bucks.

Tony Jones of The Athletic reported earlier Friday that Barlow was trending in that direction, and Barlow foreshadowed it on Instagram on Thursday as well. Still, for a Sixers team that's been woefully short on frontcourt depth as of late, his return could not be coming at a better time.

Even with Barlow back, the Sixers won't be at full strength against Milwaukee. Adem Bona (cervical spine strain), Jaylen Brown (hamstring) and Dean Wade (hamstring) are all out, while Tyrese Maxey is listed as questionable with a right foot sprain. For the Bucks, Ousmane Dieng (left finger fracture) is their only notable player who's out.

Luckily for the Sixers, the Bucks only loosely resemble a professional basketball team this year as they begin to chart a course in the post-Giannis Antetokoumpo era. They're an NBA-worst 1-15, and this will be their third game in four days. The 1 p.m. ET start likely won't do their tired legs any favors.

Since this will be Barlow's first game back this season, the Sixers figure to ease him in slowly. It'd be surprising if he played more than 15-20 minutes at most. Still, it'll bear watching how the Sixers deploy him. Will he stick strictly to the 4, or will he cut into Tacko Time and steal some minutes at backup center whenever Joel Embiid is off the floor?

Barlow was a revelation for the Sixers last year, averaging 7.7 points and 4.8 rebounds in 23.8 minutes per game while shooting 53.9 percent from the field. They initially signed him to a two-way contact last offseason, but he played so well for them—he even started 59 games!—that they wound up spending a hefty chunk of their taxpayer mid-level exception to convert him to a standard contract after the trade deadline.

Barlow's three-point shooting might be the swing skill that determines his place in the Sixers' rotation this year. He knocked down only 25.6 percent of his long-range attempts on low volume last season, which won't cut it moving forward. Jabari Walker's improvement from deep gives him the inside track to backup frontcourt minutes, but it's worth seeing how a Walker-Barlow pairing fares defensively, particularly with regard to rim protection.

The Sixers proved Wednesday that they're capable of winning even with Brown, Wade and Maxey out. Regardless of whether Maxey returns, they should be able to take care of business against an overmatched Bucks team. But Barlow's return adds some extra juice to an otherwise sleepy 1 p.m. ET start against a lottery-bound squad.

#2924. Warsh wants Fed officials to talk less. Instead, they’re talking more

In his first six months as Federal Reserve chairman, Kevin Warsh has diligently avoided signaling where he sees monetary policy and interest rates headed. His colleagues have been only too happy to fill the void.

Fed officials have been rushing to the microphones lately, voicing sometimes conflicting opinions on how the central bank should be handling an inflation bug that the economy can't seem to shake.

Most agree that rates should be raised — and soon. Some are willing to hike at a measured pace as conditions play out, while others, such as Governor Christopher Waller and St. Louis Fed President Alberto Musalem, entertain the thought of more aggressive hikes in a shorter period of time.

Largely on the sidelines in the debate is Warsh, who has expressed disdain for that sort of forward guidance and argued that it handcuffs policymakers long before they actually have to make a decision. But that hasn't stopped most of the other 18 officials who sit in on Federal Open Market Committee meetings.

In fact, according to a Bank of America analysis, there has been more so-called Fedspeak in the past two weeks than in any similar period since the start of 2025.

"Warsh's refusal to guide markets might be encouraging the rest of the FOMC to speak more, not less," Bank of America economist Aditya Bhave said in a note Thursday.

"There is always a range of views across the 19 FOMC participants. Typically, the Chair would weigh in, indicating to markets whether the hawks or doves are in ascendence," he added. "Absent this leadership, however, both sides might feel incentivized to speak more often and more forcefully."

The increasingly vocal debate comes at a critical time for the Fed and has myriad policy implications.

Bhave noted "a deepening divide" on the FOMC. The Fed's decision not to increase its benchmark rate at its October meeting "increasingly looks like a mistake," and as a result "the hawks have been very vocal of late," he wrote. Leading the charge is Waller, who has raised the possibility of hiking by half a percentage point when the committee next meets in December.

Minutes from the October meeting hint at the tension, particularly when it comes to Warsh's refusal to provide guidance.

The summary related that some officials who were looking for a hike in October "could have supported no change in the federal funds rate provided that it was clearly understood by market participants that further tightening was likely" — a statement that points toward the forward guidance that Warsh has insisted the Fed avoid.

The minutes "revealed that some officials favoring a hike would have accepted a hold if the Fed had clearly communicated further tightening," market veteran Ed Yardeni wrote Thursday. "Warsh's communication philosophy may thus be limiting the Fed's flexibility precisely when it needs it most."

Cleveland Fed President Beth Hammack, who voted against the October pause in favor of a quarter-point hike, tiptoed around the forward guidance issue during a recent public appearance.

Prefacing some observations she made about where she sees policy headed, Hammack noted that, "Those are not promises. Those are not commitments. They're not forward guidance, which is a hot-button topic. But what they are are a conditional set of expectations of where I think the economy can go, based on what I'm seeing."

Hammack's comments illustrate a needle that Fed officials have been trying to thread: continuing to be transparent about their thinking without appearing locked into a future policy that changing economic conditions could render obsolete.

Speaking Thursday, Chicago Fed President Austan Goolsbee framed the argument around credibility.

"I am sympathetic to the view that [Warsh] has expressed that let's not be so, in in our language, forward guidance-oriented, saying here is what I think rates are going to be at the next meeting," Goolsbee said, calling such pronouncements "falsely precise."

However, he quickly separated forward guidance from transparency.

"I think central banks should be careful about doing anything that undermines credibility," Goolsbee said. "That said, I also think that it's pretty important [that] each person who sits around that table explain how they see the world. What do you see in business conditions? You don't want to surprise everybody."

Taking a slightly different tack, Philadelphia Fed President Anna Paulson, in a speech Thursday on policy communication, insisted that markets can figure out where policymakers are going without specific guidance so long as they understand how economic developments will influence decisions.

Paulson also defended the variety of voices coming from the Fed.

"But volume is not the same as clarity," she said. "The measure is not how much we say. It is whether we are understood." The mixture of voices "guards against any single communication becoming the only one that matters."

Financial markets have taken the plethora of Fed communications and interpreted it in a fairly hawkish way.

Traders are implying a 100% chance of at least a quarter percentage point hike in December, with a very small probability of a half-point move, according to the CME Group's FedWatch. From there, odds are high for a follow-up increase in January, then perhaps one more before the end of 2027.

While current inflation indicators are running above 3%, compared to the Fed's 2% target, market measures of longer-term inflation have declined.

Five-year forward expectations, as indicated through the Treasury market, are at their lowest since April. That suggests investors could be less concerned about inflation over the longer term, even as policymakers warn that current price pressures remain elevated.

Still, a sclerotic communications strategy from the Fed amid volatile economic and geopolitical currents could prove dangerous ahead.

"The result of this back-and-forth is undue rates volatility in already turbulent times," BofA's Bhave said.

For his part, Warsh has been advocating for a "family fight" atmosphere inside the Fed, arguing that competing viewpoints deserve a full airing as the committee works towards decisions.

"There are benefits to shaping the market's future expectations. Perhaps this is part of the 'family fight' that Warsh keeps referencing in his pressers," said Tom Graff, chief investment officer at advisory firm Facet. "That family fight seems to be happening out in public."

#2923. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on these 3 stocks for the long haul

The ongoing earnings season, Middle East tensions and buzz ahead of the U.S. midterm elections have been keeping investors busy. Further, worries about elevated artificial intelligence spending are weighing on investor sentiment.

Investors looking beyond the short-term noise and focusing on long-term returns can track top Wall Street analysts to pick the right stocks, backed by strong research.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

This week's first stock pick is Datadog, an AI-powered observability and security platform. The company will announce its results for the third quarter on Nov. 4. Datadog is seeing strong demand for its offerings, thanks to cloud migration and rapid AI adoption. TipRanks' KPIs tool for DDOG shows the growth in its customer base over the past few years amid increasing market penetration and growing adoption of its products.

Heading into Q3 FY26 earnings, Bank of America analyst Koji Ikeda reiterated a buy rating on Datadog stock and raised his price target to $335 from $305. The analyst noted that industry checks and data indicate that infrastructure software demand is strengthening, with AI increasingly driving usage and growth.

Ikeda called Datadog and Atlassian his top picks, saying that companies that can show that AI is a tailwind to growth could see their valuation rerate, even at premium multiples. He added that the total addressable market for observability is growing faster than anticipated, driven by accelerated growth of enterprise AI and the rapid ramp of consumer AI agents.

The five-star analyst believes that focus will be on revenue growth, which he estimates to be 28.7% for Q3 FY26 versus the Street's consensus of 29.1%. Ikeda thinks that the market is underestimating the growth prospects of DDOG's security products. Meanwhile, he cautioned investors about concentration risk related to large customers.

"We think results will be supportive of our long-term thesis that Datadog is a strong share gainer in the rapidly expanding observability opportunity," said Ikeda.

Ikeda ranks No. 673 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 55% of the time, delivering an average return of 10.9%. See Datadog Statistics on TipRanks.

The next pick, semiconductor giant Nvidia, is also benefiting from AI-driven demand for its products. After attending Nvidia's GTC Berlin conference, Morgan Stanley analyst Joseph Moore reaffirmed a buy rating on NVDA stock with a price target of $300, calling it a top pick in the semiconductor space.

The five-star analyst said that the conference supported his thesis that Nvidia continues to effectively navigate bottlenecks in the supply chain, land/power/shell, and funding. In fact, Moore is "convinced that all 3 aspects are opportunities for NVDA."

While Nvidia's GTC Berlin offered no major updates, Moore noted the conference reflected that the company continues to add to its significant advantages heading into 2027. The analyst said that Vera Rubin is gaining momentum, though details on next year's Rubin Ultra were lighter than expected.

With demand outpacing supply, Moore said that focus is on closing the gap. In this regard, he highlighted the supply chain deals on substrate, packaging and testing. While the analyst continues to view TSMC 3 nm wafer supply as the biggest challenge, he expects it to ease over time.

Moore ranks No. 142 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 59% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.4%. See Nvidia Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Aerospace company SpaceX recently announced solid third-quarter results, with its AI revenue surging to $7.56 billion from $813 million in the prior-year quarter.

Impressed by the results, Needham analyst Ryan Koontz reiterated a buy rating on SPCX stock with a price target of $250. Koontz highlighted that SpaceX's Q3 results significantly exceeded expectations, proving that the company's vertically integrated business model is driving massive scale.

Furthermore, Koontz emphasized the 195.2% sequential growth in SpaceX's AI revenue, fueled by wholesale spot compute deals. He noted management's increasing focus on its AI strategy, given that it is optimistic about adding 10 gigawatts of incremental capacity during 2027.

"With increasing confidence on Starship re-use, we meaningfully increase our estimates," said Koontz.

Koontz ranks No. 357 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 42% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.7%. See SpaceX Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

#2921. Where the 49ers need to improve defensively vs. Vikings

Last weekend was not a good day for the San Francisco 49ers defense, as they gave up 40 points on the Dallas Cowboys, yet still managed to come away with the 46-40 victory.

San Francisco's defense struggled on all fronts. The secondary especially had its issues, as Dallas's top three receivers all had quality games. George Pickens had 11 catches for 145 yards and three touchdowns, and that was with him dropping three passes. CeeDee Lamb had seven catches for 72 yards and a touchdown, while Ryan Flournoy had six catches for 71 yards and a touchdown.

Cornerbacks Deommodore Lenoir, Renardo Green, and Upton Stout entered the game banged up, and it showed, as both had their fair share of struggles against Dallas's elite offense. Of course, the matchup wasn't easy facing a high-caliber quarterback with two Pro Bowl receivers, but it was a rough day for a defense that had been quite good over the first half of the season.

That wasn't the only issue. The 49ers pass rush was also ineffective, failing to sack Dak Prescott, and Nick Bosa came out this week and took accountability for his play this season. Bosa has yet to record a sack, although his other numbers still indicate a productive season, especially with the double teams and chips he's facing.

Now, this week, the health is looking better. Stout remained limited in practice with his ongoing shoulder injury and is questionable, but Lenoir wasn't on the injury report and Renardo Green practiced in full on Thursday and is off the injury report for Sunday's game.

But the 49ers still have a tough challenge against the Minnesota Vikings, who put up 30 points on the Green Bay Packers in Kirk Cousins's return. Minnesota's offense suddenly looks formidable, and this battle should be a tough one on both sides.

After their struggles against Dallas, where do the 49ers need to improve defensively against Minnesota? Ahead of Sunday night, defensive coordinator Raheem Morris broke down the main issues he saw from the Cowboys game and what to take from it.

"Extending drives early with some of the penalties to get those guys to go down there, particularly on the first drive [was a problem]. We gave up three, I believe, on that first one," Morris said. "We got the big penalty to get those guys down there. They got a big-time field goal kicker. A couple of big plays in the first half. The two big plays were the ones that killed us there early. Got a pretty good grip on it after that and kind of held it down. It was at some point it was like 38-19. Then that finishing moments, we didn't do a very good job of getting the finishes off. Usually, the finishes happen when you're able to generate pressure with sacks or you're able to get interceptions.

"The Cowboys did a nice job, to give them credit, of getting the ball out of their hand. Did a nice job of getting the ball into their good players' hand, keeping drives going, extending drives. They had about three or four drives there where it was almost like overtime-like play for them, where it was going to be fourth down territory regardless and they were able to capitalize and go down and score on us. You would like to be able to stop some of those plays and be able to get those guys going and get off the grass at those moments and then that score wouldn't have got to where it was, but those things didn't happen.

"We didn't execute that way. And it's a collective group. I know [Nick] Bosa stood up in front of you guys and took all the credit and took all the accountability, but I really think it's just all of us. Some of it could be better play calls for them. Some of it could be better for us, what we can do for those guys. Some of it can be better plays made by us. Some of it, we just ran out a little bit of gas, got a little bit tired. Let's not kid ourselves. Human nature as well. So, fortunate to get out of there with a win. Definitely want to learn those lessons when you get a chance to do those things in those moments, when you get wins. Something that we can learn from and grow from and something that we definitely will."

One thing Morris did see from his team was accountability after the game, with the leaders on the team stepping up.

"They're such an accountable group," Morris added. "Most guys, you come in and you're just upset after a game. These guys, they hold themselves to such a high standard. They come in, they're able to speak very clearly right off the bat. So it's not going to be more vocal the next week because they were vocal the first week. Not necessarily Bosa. Bosa's kind of a to-himself guy, but for a guy like Fred [Warner], he's always going to speak up and do the things that happened. It wasn't like there was mistakes. They did some better job executing. There was a few mistakes. There's always going to be mistakes and there's always correcting mistakes. I'm not saying there's not mistakes, but there's always going to be correcting mistakes, but you correct those mistakes, you move on.

"There's some other things that, just you talking to as an individual player. The best thing about our guys, they always point at themselves first. Whether it's Fred, whether it's Bosa, whether it's DMo (Deommodore Lenoir), Renardo [Green], anybody on our football team. That's the cool part about being here with these guys is they point at themselves first. Then a lot of the times I'm able to say, 'Hey, I can help you here' or 'we can do these things better. We can make these calls. We can help you out, do a little bit better in these situations.' They are all so upfront and forthgoing with those things. That makes it really cool to talk to those guys that way."

Health seemed to be a factor last weekend, but the 49ers do need to be more disciplined and avoid the killer penalties (although some looked to be questionable calls).

But there is a lot at stake this weekend for the 49ers, as their No. 1 seed odds shift dramatically with a win or a loss. San Francisco's offense has always been up to the task, but they'll get their toughest matchup of the year with Brian Flores's defense. So, the 49ers defense will need to show up as well against this new-look Vikings offense with Kirk Cousins and execute on all three levels.

#2920. Brandon Ingram expected to make season debut soon with Clippers

Brandon Ingram is set to make his season debut with the Los Angeles Clippers on Monday, Nov. 23, according to ESPN's Shams Charania.

The Clippers will play the Houston Rockets at the Toyota Center in Houston on Monday.

Ingram had been working his way back from right heel surgery he had in May. The two-time All-Star also had a partially torn Achilles tendon.

Ingram was initially supposed to have a bone removed from his heel, but the doctors found the partially torn Achilles during that procedure.

Earlier this month, Ingram told reporters that he was "pretty close" to returning and had been on the court a bit more.

He also stated that he had not played any 5-on-5 during the summer but has since been "gaining that confidence."

The Clippers acquired Ingram as one of the primary trade assets in the package sent over by the Toronto Raptors for Kawhi Leonard during the offseason.

Ingram has remain close with the team since his arrival.

He took some time to praise his teammates despite the Clippers' slow start to the season. L.A. has a 4-10 record through the first 14 games.

"I actually want to commend those guys because everybody is in new positions and asked to do more," Ingram told reporters earlier this month. "So the guys are trying. They're playing really, really good defense."

The Clippers fell short in a 124-112 loss to the San Antonio Spurs, in what was a competitive game considering both teams are on opposite sides of the standings.

Ingram return would be welcomed, especially on the offensive end of the floor. The All-Star led Toronto with 21.5 points and was third in rebounds (5.6) during the 2025-26 season. He started 77 games for the Raptors.

"They're trying to figure it out on the offensive end," Ingram told reporters. "When I come in, I want to play make, make it a little bit easier for guys to catch and shoot, and not have to make so many decisions. And be able to, when it gets down to it, be able to score."

Ingram already had a level of familiarity with head coach Tyronn Lue and assistant coach Jeff Van Gundy from playing for Team USA during the 2023 FIBA World Cup.

The Clippers had several players injured to start the month, including Ingram, center Yanic Konan Niederhäuser and Max Strus.

The trio was recalled on Nov. 2 and participated in a weekend assignment with the team's G-League affiliate.

Strus returned to the team after recovering from a partial tear to his right plantar fascia. Konan Niederhäuser made his season debut on Nov. 19 against the Clippers, after recovering from right Lisfranc surgery.

Bradley Beal (right knee injury management) and Jordan Miller (left rotator cuff surgery) will remain the only Clippers without a timetable to return.

#2919. Data center construction costs soar 13% in 2026

A version of this article first appeared in the CNBC Property Play newsletter with Diana Olick. Property Play covers new and evolving opportunities for the real estate investor, from individuals to venture capitalists, private equity funds, family offices, institutional investors and large public companies. Sign up to receive future editions, straight to your inbox.

The demand for large-scale data centers in the U.S. this year, driven by the need for computing power for artificial intelligence, grew well beyond the supply of powered land and the labor to build these structures. It also pushed timelines and risk for contractors.

All that resulted in costs rising 13% year-over-year to more than twice the global rate, according to a new report from Turner & Townsend, a global project management company. The gain this year was about three times the last ten-year average.

The report looked at over 500 live or recently completed projects in 62 global markets. It also included a survey of 400 experts in the global data center industry.

While there was some stabilization in cost inflation this year, it was not enough to offset the huge growth in not just demand but speed of construction and delivery.

"More aggressive schedules tie to contractors in particular and subcontractors baking in more risk allowances in their pricing," said Paul Barry, North America data center lead for Turner & Townsend. "So whether that's acceleration allowances, whether that's more overtime and labor attraction allowances, even just higher fees for taking on the risk of delivering at a faster pace than what they would traditionally take on."

All of those things, combined with the spike in demand and the strain on the supply chain, translate into higher costs, he added.

Capital expenditure on global data centers is projected to rise from about $800 billion this year to $1.8 trillion per year in 2050, according to a recent report from PwC. The U.S. is expected to capture almost half of all global investment in AI infrastructure, which includes both data centers and chips.

Power is, of course, key to all data centers, and 56% of those surveyed by Turner & Townsend globally said it was a major obstacle to delivery. Just over half said utility connections typically take between one and two years, and almost a third reported timeframes of over two years. Costs for power vary regionally — from $15.01 per watt in Silicon Valley to $10.51 per watt in Dallas, based on a typical 60-megawatt design.As with every type of construction in the U.S., labor continues to be a significant headwind. Just over one quarter of respondents said their contractor capacity, that is, the ability to manage construction backlogs, was sufficient.

Going forward, Barry said he expects costs to moderate somewhat, with cost increases in the 6% to 10% range in the next year. A lot of the major projects have already started or are starting now, so cost risk has been baked into those contracts.

"If this demand continues, then there'll be more continued flow of project starts and so on, but I think we've hit a big surge in 2026, especially of new big major projects that have been procured," said Barry.

#2917. Pat Freiermuth says Steelers still believe in DK Metcalf

DK Metcalf hasn't quite lived up to expectations during his time in Pittsburgh, but that hasn't shaken the confidence of his teammates. Pat Freiermuth opened up to the media today about the embattled wideout's time in the Steel City.

"Obviously he's had some struggles but if you look at his effort on the run game or downfield blocking, he doesn't let his situation impact his ability to get other guys open," said Freiermuth on Thursday afternoon.

"I think that's something that you can appreciate as a teammate and as a guy you can tell a lot about his character. You know, we're with him until the end. He's going to get this figured out. We've got a lot of trust in DK and a lot of belief in him. We paid him for a reason, and the Steelers have backed him for a reason."

Metcalf's struggles have been a combination of costly drops, difficulty creating separation against physical coverage and inconsistency in the Steelers' passing game. Opposing defenses have increasingly focused on limiting his opportunities, while Pittsburgh has leaned heavily on its running game.

The Steelers acquired Metcalf to be their No. 1 receiving threat, and his physical tools and big-play ability remain undeniable. He previously established himself as one of the NFL's more productive receivers in Seattle, recording 6,324 receiving yards and 48 touchdowns from 2019-2024.

While the production hasn't consistently followed him to Pittsburgh, Freiermuth's confidence suggests the locker room still believes Metcalf can turn things around. The challenge now is translating his talent into more consistent results on the field.

#2916. Things look bleak for Warriors, but they can survive this

The Warriors are in free fall, losing five games in a row, including their last four by 20 or more points.

Golden State Warriors guard Stephen Curry, right, confers with head coach Steve Kerr during an NBA game in Denver, Monday, Oct. 26, 2026. AP Photo/David Zalubowski

Of the 15 Western Conference teams, only 11 are going to have a real shot at securing the 10 playoff spots unless something drastically changes.

In other words, the West cellar is so weak that even if the Warriors can't arrest their precipitous slide without Steph Curry, they'll have more than enough time to recover when he returns.

So, while what's happening to the Warriors is unfortunate, it's far from catastrophic.

Curry is expected to be sidelined close to a month after suffering a right oblique abdominal tear on Nov. 11.

The 38-year-old had carried the Warriors to eight straight wins while playing some of the best basketball of his career, averaging 27.8 points (50% shooting from the field, 45.7% from beyond the arc), 3.7 rebounds and 3.9 assists per game.

Before Curry sustained the injury, he and Yaxel Lendeborg were looking like one of the NBA's best duos. Lendeborg, whom the Warriors drafted with the No. 11 overall pick in June, was having a Rookie of the Year campaign, averaging 21.6 points on 52.2% shooting.

But with defenses collapsing on him without Curry, the rookie has wilted, averaging 13.7 points on 26.8% shooting over the last four games.

Not only is Curry going to be back in the lineup soon, but Jimmy Butler (torn right ACL) is expected to return midseason.

The Warriors could still be really good. They could still have a deep playoff run. They could still be a contender.

This has been a roller coaster for Warriors fans.

Entering the season, it was questionable whether the Warriors would make the playoffs after they pretty much stood pat during the offseason. And after dropping their first three games, it seemed all but guaranteed that this was going to be a wasted season for Curry, a two-time MVP who carried the Warriors to four championships in eight years from 2015-2022.

Lendeborg emerged as his sidekick. He sprinted past his expectations. He became a force.

Yaxel Lendeborg, left, and Golden State Warriors head coach Steve Kerr smile during a game against the Houston Rockets on Wednesday, Nov. 11, 2026, in Houston. NBAE via Getty Images

All of a sudden, Curry and Lendeborg were stomping on their competition, beating contenders such as the Lakers, Timberwolves and Nuggets over a stunning stretch that had pundits questioning whether the Warriors were championship contenders.

But the short-handed Warriors were always one Curry injury away from disaster.

And when their nightmare was realized, the Warriors went from being one of the league's most fun teams to one of its biggest duds.

Golden State Warriors head coach Steve Kerr watches play during an NBA game against the San Antonio Spurs on Friday, Nov. 13, 2026, in San Antonio. AP Photo/Darren Abate

Curry and Butler will return, and the Warriors will have more than enough runway to correct course.

So the excitement the team was generating a little over a week ago?

#2915. Jensen Huang to testify publicly before Senate banking panel, Chairman Scott says

Former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang will testify publicly when he appears before the Senate banking panel, a spokesperson for Committee Chair Tim Scott said Friday.

The South Carolina Republican confirmed earlier this week that Huang had agreed to appear, but the details of that appearance remained unclear. Huang, who resigned from the company he co-founded earlier this month over a series of extramarital affairs, including one with a suspected Chinese spy, also reached an agreement with three House committees to appear for a closed-door, transcribed interview on Dec. 1.

The date of Huang's public testimony in the Senate has not yet been announced, though Scott initially requested he appear on Dec. 3.

His office did not respond when asked when Huang would appear. Scott told CNBC on Wednesday he hopes to hear testimony from Huang and Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick "before Christmas." The new Congress, when Sen. Elizabeth Warren, D-Mass., is expected to take over as Senate banking chair, convenes Jan. 3.

#2913. Trump says he still has confidence in Lutnick

President Donald Trump on Friday said he has not lost confidence in Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick, pushing back on growing pressure from bipartisan lawmakers and even members of his own administration.

Trump, at a wide-ranging White House press conference, was asked about Sen. Tom Cotton, R-Ark., telling The Wall Street Journal that, if Lutnick is to be ousted, it should happen soon enough for the Republican-led Senate to confirm his replacement before Democrats take over the chamber.

"Well, Tom's right," Trump replied, saying he would agree with taking swift action if he had indeed lost confidence in Lutnick. "But I have not lost confidence," Trump said in response to a question from CNBC's Eamon Javers.

"He's a different kind of a guy, very good businessman. He's not traditional," Trump said, praising Lutnick's involvement in the U.S. taking an equity stake in Intel.

#2911. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 dividend stocks for consistent income

Democrats' midterm election win has put the artificial intelligence trade in question, as voters clearly expressed opposition to the rapid buildout of data centers. The stock market could remain volatile amid AI trade concerns, macro uncertainties, and geopolitical tensions.

In this scenario, investors can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios to earn steady income. However, the extensive universe of dividend stocks can make selection challenging. Top Wall Street analysts' recommendations can help investors shortlist attractive dividend stocks, as these experts assign ratings after in-depth financial analysis.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Viper Energy (VNOM) is a subsidiary of Diamondback and owns and acquires mineral and royalty interests, primarily in the Permian Basin. The company reported upbeat third-quarter earnings, supported by recent acquisitions and continued development activity from Diamondback and third-party operators. Viper announced a base cash dividend of 50 cents per share for Q3 2026, payable on Nov. 19. Based on the base and variable dividends announced over the past 12 months, VNOM's dividend yield stands at 5.7%.

Following the Q3 results, TD Cowen analyst Aaron Bilkoski reiterated a buy rating on Viper Energy stock with a price target of $61. The analyst noted that the company delivered solid Q3 results, with production, including oil and natural gas liquids, exceeding both TD Cowen and consensus estimates.

The 5-star analyst highlighted that the key update was the announcement of two royalty acquisitions for $809 million in cash, including deferred consideration, and $82 million of newly issued shares.

Given the recently announced base dividend hike, Bilkoski expects Viper to direct incremental free cash flow toward buybacks. While the acquisitions increase leverage, the analyst believes that Viper has enough capacity for opportunistic buybacks.

Bilkoski noted that Viper has historically delivered one of the highest production per share growth profiles in his royalty coverage, but still trades at a notable discount to the royalty peer average and the company's historical FCF yield range. "In our view, its above-average growth warrants a higher valuation," said the analyst.

Bilkoski ranks No. 525 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 12.80%. See Viper Energy Stock Buybacks on TipRanks.

Exxon Mobil (XOM) reported robust Q3 2026 profits, as its U.S. refineries boosted production to capitalize on the high diesel fuel prices sparked by the conflict in the Middle East. The oil and gas giant announced a quarterly dividend of $1.07 per share, payable on Dec. 10, reflecting a 3.9% increase. This marks Exxon's 44th consecutive year of dividend increases. At an annualized dividend of $4.28 per share, XOM offers a yield of 2.53%.

Impressed by the Q3 print, TD Cowen analyst Jason Gabelman reiterated a buy rating on Exxon stock and raised his price target to $193 from $180 to reflect higher downstream earnings, with the expectation of increased mid-cycle margins.

Gabelman noted that the strength in Exxon's refining business continues to offset Middle East-related headwinds. He added that the company increased its ownership in LNG projects, which should reduce its Middle East concentration over time from 75% to 50% and lower the likelihood of diversification-focused mergers and acquisitions. Beyond LNG, Gabelman expects Exxon's growth in the 2030s to come from Guyana, Kashagan, Permian, and possibly Venezuela.

Gabelman noted that Exxon announced a dividend hike of nearly 4% and plans to maintain buybacks at $20 billion for 2027. "The decision to maintain reduces the pro-cyclicality nature of the buyback," said the analyst.

Gabelman ranks No. 347 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 66% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.90%. See Exxon Mobil Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Williams (WMB), which owns and operates energy infrastructure to deliver natural gas, reported strong third-quarter results. The company announced a quarterly dividend of $0.525 per share, or $2.10 per share annualized, payable on Dec. 28. WMB offers a dividend yield of 3.09%.

In reaction to the Q3 2026 results, CIBC analyst Robert Catellier reaffirmed a buy rating on Williams stock with a price target of $82. The analyst noted that Q3 results were essentially in line with expectations and reflected continued strength in execution across WMB's infrastructure portfolio.

Catellier said that Williams' Q3 performance was driven by higher transmission, storage, and power generation revenues, as well as contributions from the Momentum Midstream acquisition.

He noted that Williams maintained its full-year EBITDA guidance of $8.3 billion to $8.5 billion. In fact, results are tracking toward the higher end of the guidance range, backed by contributions from recently completed projects and the Momentum Midstream deal. WMB continues to target long-term EBITDA compound annual growth rate of at least 11% through 2030.

Catellier expects WMB's long-term targets to be supported by incremental transmission and storage opportunities, including Transco's Southeast Supply Enhancement II project and an 84 MMcf/d expansion of the Garden Connector project.

Catellier ranks No. 667 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 66% of the time, delivering an average return of 9.60%. See Williams Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

#2910. Steelers-Eagles preview: What to expect from Philadelphia’s offense, defense Week 11

The Steelers will travel to Philadelphia — a city where they haven't won since 1965 — on Sunday in a game that many fans have had circled as an Eagles victory since before the season.

However, the 2026 Eagles aren't the same team that made the playoffs every year from 2021-25. They enter Week 11 with a dismal 2-7 record, with all those losses coming in a row against the Bears, Rams, Jaguars, Panthers, Cowboys, Commanders and Giants. Philadelphia hasn't been in the win column since a Sept. 20 victory over the Titans.

Is this Eagles team finally bad enough for the Steelers to break such a lengthy losing streak in The City of Brotherly Love? Or will Philadelphia, fresh off a Week 10 bye, be able to hold off the Steelers at home for yet another season? We'll find out in Week 11.

RB: It's been a rough year for the Eagles offense, now on its third quarterback of the season ahead of Week 11's matchup versus the Steelers. The numbers above speak for themselves: Philadelphia has struggled on both the ground and in the air in 2026, dealing with injuries, the sudden retirement of right tackle Lane Johnson, and poor execution from players on the field. The Eagles have only scored over 20 points four times this season, and have yet to break 30 once.

Before a Week 10 bye, the Eagles faced the New York Giants in Week 9, falling 19-11 in an ugly game that's been all too familiar for the Philly faithful. But one thing did change, with the Eagles trying to spark their Jalen Hurts-less offense by replacing backup Tanner McKee with Joe "Bazooka" Milton at quarterback. He's expected to start again against Pittsburgh on Sunday.

The results were not great against the Giants, with Milton finishing with 169 passing yards and 18/34 completion in Week 9. He didn't throw a single touchdown or interception, and was sacked a whopping seven times. However, the guy with the "Bazooka" nickname does indeed have a powerful arm, with Milton logging an 11.8% deep throw percentage along with eight air yards per attempt (both team highs for the Eagles this season). He's also a much better runner than McKee, leading the Eagles with 64 yards on the ground and a 7.1 average against New York.

Frankly, Milton plays like a still-developing quarterback. He holds onto the ball too long and doesn't seem to go through his reads at NFL speed. However, his arm talent does allow him to rifle balls into open zones when he sees them, and there's always a little bit of worry when playing a passer with his physical abilities — even if everything only clicks a few times a game, he can still make defenses pay. It's worth noting that Pittsburgh hasn't played many highly-mobile quarterbacks this year, and has had some issues with deep coverage this season.

With Milton under center, the Eagles have mixed up their playbook from McKee a bit with a few more rollouts and designed quarterback runs. What hasn't changed, however, is that Philadelphia remains an 11-personnel heavy team that likes short throws outside the numbers to move the ball in the passing game. Making matters worse is that despite the short passing game, Philadelphia ranks dead-last in the league in yards after catch in 2026, with just 738 on the year.

First-round receiver Makai Lemon is a name to watch as a twitchy underneath target, and Milton will get to throw to the now-healthy Devonta Smith for the first time this season after the latter missed the Giants game with injury. That's a big boost to what was a decimated Philly receiving core in Week 9. However, Dontayvion Wicks will still be out for the Eagles against Pittsburgh.

Capping the Eagles' offensive ceiling has been a rough year from the line, which lost All-World right tackle Lane Johnson to retirement in October and has struggled with some regression. Jordan Mailata remains a very good left tackle and Philadelphia now has Landon Dickerson back from injured reserve, but the Eagles still rank seventh-highest in sack rate allowed (9%), third-highest in pressure rate allowed (41.9%), and second-highest in runs stuffed percentage (23.5%) despite facing the seventh-most light boxes in the league.

Having inexperienced signal-callers since Hurts landed on injured reserve certainly hasn't helped the Eagles in pass protection. Philadelphia struggled mightily with interior pressure against the Giants, and gave up a number of unblocked rushers on stunts and twists.

One name to watch is rookie right tackle Markel Bell, a massive human being who has struggled some in pass protection this year, but is a promising run blocker.

Still, on the ground, the Eagles' inside zone-heavy attack is tied for 28th in the NFL in yards per rush (3.7), with just 28 non-Milton yards on the ground against New York. However, star running back Saquon Barkley, who played through injury against the Giants, should be rested up after the Eagles' bye and looking a bit more explosive. In two of Barkley's first three games this season, he averaged 5.5 yards per carry — when healthy, the 29-year-old is still a legitimate offensive weapon.

After a nightmare game against the Bengals last week, the Pittsburgh defense matches up much better against Philadelphia. The key will be stifling the Eagles' run game and pressuring Milton, which the Steelers' front seven is certainly capable of, especially with Derrick Harmon back in play on the interior. Milton averaged 3.12 seconds to throw last week — that should give TJ Watt more time to get home against a rookie right tackle.

Still, with Devonta Smith back in the lineup for the Eagles, along with solid ancillary weapons in Lemon and tight end Dallas Goedert available, a Pittsburgh secondary that got torched last week will still need to bring a better performance to the table. That said, there should be a lot more optimism heading into this week than the last for the Steelers' defense.

RP: Much like the Eagles' offense this year, the defensive side of the ball still possesses a lot of talent, but has performed well below expectations. A unit that was the driving force behind a Super Bowl victory just two seasons ago, the Eagles seemed poised to field a young and imposing defense that would keep them contenders for the rest of the 2020s. Instead, they were sellers at the deadline, shipping away defensive tackle Jordan Davis, whom they'd signed to a three-year, $78 million extension this March.

I spoke with Brandon Gowton of Bleeding Green Nation this week, to try to get a sense of why the defense has underperformed, and he said it's been one of the more baffling developments this season. He cited a lingering injury to superstar defensive tackle Jalen Carter and a general lack of pass rush as the biggest factor. Whether or not Carter is living up to the extension he signed this past summer (four years, $152 million) is seemingly a hot topic among Eagles fans and media, but a Steelers offensive line that just lost Mason McCormick should still be plenty concerned about his presence. Even in a "down" year, Carter's five sacks are only two off the NFL lead for defensive tackles.

As a defense, the Eagles' 16 sacks on the season rank 28th, and their 109 pressures rank 26th. According to Next Gen Stats, the Eagles are below league average in blitz rate (26% vs 32%), and in generating pressure, whether that's with the blitz (41% vs 43%) or without (30% vs 33%).

Carter's 20 pressures this season are tied for second on the Eagles behind only Moro Ojomo (22), an upcoming defensive tackle who allowed the Eagles the flexibility to trade away Davis. Jalyx Hunt, a rotational pass rusher who hasn't played more than 43% of the defensive snaps in a game since Week 4, is tied with Carter. Nolan Smith (17 pressures, 5 sacks), Jonathan Greenard (11 pressures, 1 sack), and Zack Baun (9 pressures, 2 sacks) round out the Eagles' underperforming pass rush.

Baun, the Eagles' top off-ball linebacker, is out for the game, the team announced Friday.

With the lack of pressure, the Eagles' secondary has predictably struggled. Third-year nickel corner Cooper DeJean has taken a step back, allowing four touchdowns this season after allowing just one during his first two seasons, and he has nearly as many missed tackles this season (13) as he had in his career (19) before this season. Meanwhile, Riq Woolen remains a volatile corner who has looked like a shutdown player some weeks and like a weak link in others, including surrendering four touchdowns against zero interceptions so far this season.

If there is one bright spot in Philadelphia's secondary, it's cornerback Quinyon Mitchell, who is still performing at a high level. He's allowed just a 55.6% catch rate and a QB passer rating of 76.5 when targeted. Philadelphia has typically let Mitchell stick to one side of the field, but in their most recent game, the Eagles finally tasked Mitchell with shadowing an opponent's number one receiver. Against the Giants, Mitchell followed Malik Nabers and shut down New York's most explosive playmaker.

As for what coverages we can expect from Philadelphia, the Eagles are a team that has lived in both nickel (68% of defensive snaps) packages and single-high safety looks (54% of snaps) for multiple seasons now. That has most typically shown up as Cover-3 looks in zone coverages (36.5%) and Cover-1 in man looks (15.3%).

On the ground, the Eagles aren't quite as bad as the numbers might indicate. While the Eagles' raw run defense totals look poor, they're mostly in the middle of the pack in most metrics, and are surrendering a high yardage per game total mostly because they have been in negative game scripts for much of the season. Philadelphia allows the seventh-fewest average yards before contact (0.81); however, they also allow the eighth-highest average yards after contact (3.33). As a unit, they've missed 43 tackles on running plays, per PFF, but only DeJean (8) and safety Andrew Mukuba (6) are particularly heinous in this regard. No other defender has more than three missed tackles against the run. Philadelphia ranks 15th in stuffed run percentage (17%), but are tied with the Vikings and Patriots for the 13th-most runs of 10+ yards allowed (27).

In theory, the Steelers' strength — running the ball — should fare respectably against this Eagles front. However, the loss of Mason McCormick cannot be overstated. Spencer Anderson takes his spot and has shown a troubling deficiency in play strength and power thus far in his career. He's excelled on the edges as a sixth offensive lineman, where he can take on lighter and less powerful defenders. Having him line up against Carter, though, gives me pause. Meanwhile, the Eagles' secondary should be gettable, but Rodgers has not played well this season. The Eagles haven't gotten pressure consistently, but Rodgers has often found his way into pressure even when the line is holding well. Now, without McCormick, there is potentially an easier path for the Philadelphia defensive line to get into the face of Pittsburgh's mobility-challenged passer.

This could be another frustrating day for the Black and Gold offense.

#2909. Clemson commit drills 58-yard field goal, walk-off winner in Florida playoff stunner

Brandin Gallaway has the leg to change a football game. Friday night, the Clemson commit did it twice.

The Palmetto senior kicker drilled a 58-yard field goal to tie the game before halftime, then came through with a 34-yard kick as time expired to give the Tigers a dramatic 12-10 upset of Tampa Bay Tech in the Class 5A-Region 3 semifinals.

Gallaway accounted for all 12 of Palmetto's points, converting all four of his field-goal attempts to send the Tigers (11-1) into the regional final.

His biggest kick of the first half came with 35 seconds remaining. Facing a long attempt, Gallaway connected from 58 yards to tie the game at 6-6 and give Palmetto momentum heading into the break.

Tampa Bay Tech had taken the lead late in the first quarter on a 15-yard touchdown pass from Martavius Conway Jr., but missed the extra point.

Gallaway put Palmetto ahead with a 42-yard field goal with 3:41 remaining in the third quarter. Tampa Bay Tech answered with a 29-yard field goal with four minutes left in the game, setting up a dramatic finish.

The Tigers' ensuing possession stalled near midfield, but their defense gave Gallaway another opportunity.

Palmetto's defense flushed Conway out of the pocket, and Jordan Allen stepped in front of his pass at the Tampa Bay Tech 42-yard line, returning the interception to the 34.

Palmetto moved the ball to the 17-yard line before sending Gallaway onto the field with three seconds remaining.

The senior, like he had all night, delivered once more for safe measure.

His 34-yard attempt sailed through the uprights as time expired, sending Palmetto into the regional final and capping a four-field-goal performance that will be difficult to forget.

Palmetto will travel to face top-seeded Armwood next Friday. Armwood advanced with a 37-12 victory over Sumner.

#2907. Trump vows to stop U.S. companies from building data centers abroad, threatens 'tremendous tax'

President Donald Trump on Friday said U.S. companies will be banned from building data centers in other countries and will face a "tremendous tax" penalty if they do so.

"We're going to fight very hard for the data centers," Trump said at a White House press conference before announcing the new policy — which apparently came as news to his own staff.

"I don't know if that's a breaking story," Trump said. "I haven't even said that to my people. But I'm telling them now they're here."

Trump's latest push for more data-center construction in the U.S. came despite Americans repeatedly stating — and showing with their votes — that they dislike the structures.

CNBC's All-America Economic Survey found that 64% of registered voters opposed an AI data center within 10 miles of their home, while just 13% of respondents said they would support one.

If Trump follows through on his threat to clamp down on overseas data center construction, he will have to contend with the fact that numerous U.S. tech giants — including Alphabet, Amazon, Microsoft, Meta and Oracle — have already built them in other countries.

International expansion represents a growth opportunity for the top cloud infrastructure providers, which want to serve clients from data centers that are nearby, sometimes to meet regulatory requirements.

Trump said Friday, however, that if companies want to continue building the centers outside the U.S., "they can, but we're going to put a tremendous tax on them."

"We have a lot of places that want them badly, and they're great places. They have energy, and they have everything. They're tremendous assets," Trump said.

Representatives at cloud providers Amazon, Google, Microsoft and Oracle did not immediately respond to requests for comment.

Trump's remarks came a day after Trump met with Chinese President Xi Jinping and Russian President Vladimir Putin in China for a trilateral summit that appeared to produce few concrete deliverables on an array of pressing global issues.

Xi "congratulated me on how well we're doing" in artificial intelligence, Trump said, using his preferred term "super intelligence," which he said the Chinese president also likes better.

— CNBC's Jordan Novet and Annie Palmer contributed reporting.

#2906. Giants shocked to learn Colts cut ex-teammate Daniel Jones

John Harbaugh's eyes nearly popped out of his head upon learning that the Colts cut quarterback Daniel Jones.

Harbaugh wasn't the Giants head coach when they stunningly cut Jones in November 2024, so they don't have the personal relationship that Jones maintains with others in East Rutherford. But the Colts are next week's opponent, so the Giants' behind-the-scene staffers in charge of advance scouting were just thrown a major curveball.

The Colts plan to start first-round draft bust Anthony Richardson, which means an offense that could be more option-based in store on Nov. 29 to hide Richardson's passing inaccuracy.

Daniel Jones has been released by the Colts. Getty Images

"[It changes] probably pretty dramatically. I mean, wow," Harbaugh admitted. "I don't want to get ahead of ourselves here, and you just hit me with that. But definitely the offense will be the offense that they run, but it'll be centered around the quarterback for sure, and they're probably different styles to some degree, so we'll have to look at that when the time comes. We have people doing that this week. They'll have to make a left turn, obviously."

Unlike when the Giants demoted Jones from starter to third-stringer to protect themselves against an injury clause in his contract, Jones did not ask for his release from the Colts, head coach Shane Steichen said.

In the Giants locker room, news of Jones' release brought back memories. What does it say to teammates when you release the quarterback who started the first 11 games?

"That the writing is on the wall for the season and you have to get through the next six games the best you can play," right tackle Jermaine Eluemunor told The Post. "D.J. is a great guy but he's in such a predicament. He went there last year and balled out so it's definitely not a 'him' thing. When you trade everyone like they did, it's hard."

John Harbaugh, head coach of the Giants. Getty Images

Giants backup offensive lineman Josh Sills was with the Colts last season and to start 2026. He saw the same hard-working Jones who was beloved by Giants teammates.

"He helped me in any and every way imaginable," Sills told The Post. "He went above and beyond to make sure everyone had what they needed to achieve their goals. It's a team game. It takes 11 guys."

Next week was billed as the Jones Revenge Game. Instead, Jones didn't even make it through the first year of the two-year, $88 million contract that he signed after a torn Achilles.

Anthony Richardson will now start at quarterback for the Colts. Getty Images

Jones might be able to help the Patriots or Ravens in their playoff chase. Or he could help the Eagles not be an embarrassment the rest of the season. Or use his ties to Broncos offensive coordinator Davis Webb to become the backup for a Super Bowl contender.

Any team that claims him off waivers will inherit $2.1 million in salary this season and a $10.5 million injury guarantee for 2027.

Returning to the Giants or reuniting with Brian Daboll in Tennessee are extremely unlikely possibilities.

"I wish the best for him and I know he's going to make the most of his next opportunity," left tackle Andrew Thomas said. "But I'm focused on what we have going on and we're not looking at Indy right now. We're focused on this week and Jacksonville."

#2903. The bull market has a new problem. The 'smart money' is selling

Companies have shifted from buying back shares to issuing them, adding to the supply that the four-year-old bull market must absorb.

"No matter how you measure it, U.S. corporations have changed their behavior over the past two years, switching from repurchasing in 2024 to issuing today," Acadian Asset Management portfolio manager Owen Lamont wrote in a letter to investors this month. "The question you should be asking is: Why is the smart money selling?"

As of late last week, net new issuance of shares equaled 3.1% of total market capitalization, according to a recent Acadian analysis. While net issuance remains below the peaks during the tech bubble and the covid bubble, the analysis shows that the era of repurchases has ended and an era of equity issuance has begun.

And more supply came to the market this week with Anthropic's blockbuster IPO. The offering drew strong enthusiasm initially with the frontier AI laboratory selling shares in its IPO at $83 each, above the expected range and raising $81 billion. But the stock is falling on Friday and taking the broader stock market with it.

Some investors for years have cited the shrinking stock market as a reason why share prices can keep increasing. As companies repurchased stock, it shrunk the number of shares available for trading and so it helped boost prices. There was more money chasing fewer shares. Not to mention, it had the effect of boosting earnings per share by lowering the denominator in that ratio. Now that trend has reversed with increased supply acting as a potential headwind on the bull market.

A 2000 paper by Malcolm Baker and Jeffrey Wurgler found that when companies issued more equity over debt, low market returns followed, suggesting that managers timed the market when issuing securities. The Baker-Wurgler equity share, which measures equity issuance as a share of total stock and bond issuance, peaked at about 18% in 2021. It has risen in recent months but remains below that peak, Lamont wrote.

The Federal Reserve data show a similar trend in the year-over-year change in trailing 12-month gross stock and bond issuance. Debt gross issuance rose first in 2020, followed by equity issuance in 2021. More recently, debt issuance began climbing in 2024, followed by a surge in equity issuance in 2026.

Recent deals show companies tapping equity markets in different ways.

Oracle filed to sell $20 billion in stock through an at-the-market program and issued another $10 billion in common shares in November. In October, Meta priced a $25 billion common-stock offering and $12.5 billion in mandatory convertible preferred shares. Alphabet announced an $84.8 billion equity raise in June to help fund its AI infrastructure buildout. SpaceX raised $85.7 billion in its June IPO after underwriters exercised their overallotment.

"Before 2026, firms were net repurchasers of equity, consistent with the idea that they viewed their shares as undervalued. Today, for whatever reason, firms are issuing equity on net," Lamont wrote.

The investor said he cannot rule out that we are in an AI bubble now.

#2901. Tokenization platform Securitize gains 10% postmarket on Q3 results, move into asset-backed finance

Tokenization platform Securitize climbed about 10% postmarket after reporting third quarter growth alongside increased operating expenses, and announcing an expansion into asset-backed finance through a strategic acquisition.

Miami-based Securitize, which made its public market debut in July, operates in the relatively new corner of the crypto equity universe involved in institutional tokenization infrastructure. Tokenized assets on the platform grew 9% in the third quarter, to $4.8 billion, from the same period a year ago. Aggregate transaction volume rose 20% from a year ago, to $4.3 billion. Revenue of $11.5 million was up 28% from last year.

The growth came as bullish sentiment around tokenization gained traction throughout the third quarter, ahead of the Security and Exchange Commission formalizing the long awaited Innovation Exemption on Sept. 17, which created a temporary pathway for trading of certain tokenized stocks and strengthened the regulatory outlook.

Profitability is still meaningfully below year-ago levels, due in part to costs associated with going public over the summer, as well as the scaling up of the tokenization business at an active time for the entire sector, Securitize CEO Carlos Domingo told CNBC in an exclusive interview.

Securitize reinforced "certain areas of the company like finance, compliance, legal [and] cybersecurity," which increased corporate costs "compared to a year ago when we were privately held," Domingo said. "We're in in a very good position. The adjusted EBITDA … is improving significantly from previous quarters."

Securitize reported an adjusted EBITDA loss of $2 million, compared with positive adjusted EBITDA of $3.6 million a year ago, better than a $5.5 million loss in the second quarter. The company posted a 183% increase in operating costs and expenses.

Also on Monday, the Securitize board authorized a $50 million share buyback and announced the acquisition of River One, an institutional asset-backed financing platform developing technology to connect stablecoin capital with traditional lending markets. Terms were not disclosed.

Securitize sees an opportunity to modernize the $9 trillion market for asset backed finance by using stablecoins to finance real world assets, generate yield and tokenize investment positions and credit products, Domingo said.The acquisition also marks an expansion beyond Securitize's core niche markets at a time when tokenization's competitive landscape is growing, with traditional asset managers like BlackRock and Franklin Templeton, and established financial market infrastructure providers like DTCC and Broadridge, entering the industry.

"We have been very active in tokenized funds, treasuries [and] yield … And we've been obviously recently very active in tokenized stocks, so this adds another piece of the puzzle in the emerging tokenization space for us to capture revenue growth."

#2897. What's ahead for AI financing after Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang departs

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's departure hasn't produced a broad shock to the credit market, but it is putting the company's role as a financier of artificial intelligence infrastructure under scrutiny.

Huang, who stepped down Thursday amid an espionage and adultery scandal, built Nvidia into an AI powerhouse in the 33 years since he co-founded the company. Chief financial officer Colette Kress has been named interim CEO as the company searches for a permanent chief executive.

Nvidia not only supplies its graphics processing units to hyperscalers and tech giants, it also provides financing to other companies to help the industry expand. That could all change.

"Although we expect the company to fulfill all of its previously announced commitments, we believe that going forward, it may be more difficult for the company to negotiate new multi-party investments among ecosystem partners at the same scale it has done up to this point," Goldman Sachs analyst James Schneider said in a note Thursday.

The news of Huang's resignation comes after investors were already concerned about the massive amount of debt being taken on to fund AI infrastructure.

Tech bond yields have risen and spreads have widened on credit default swaps, most notably in lower-rated names. Credit default swaps are contracts in which investors pay for protection in case the borrower can't repay its debt. Wider spreads indicate investors view the bonds as riskier.

On Friday, the yield on Nvidia's 4.5% bond due in 2031 sat at 5.717%, nearly 5% higher week to date. Bond yields move inversely to prices.

Tech stocks slumped Friday with the Nasdaq 100 closing down 1.2% as markets continued to digest the Nvidia news. Yields on hyperscaler bonds ticked slightly higher on Friday, signaling the broader concern about AI debt lingers.

It's the smaller AI companies that could ultimately see an impact if Nvidia makes any changes to its AI financing. The company had $36 billion in off-balance-sheet AI cloud agreements, or backstops, for neoclouds, according to its last quarterly financial filing in July.

Neoclouds CoreWeave, Nebius and IREN are all in Nvidia's portfolio. Their shares were down sharply Thursday.

CoreWeave, which has become the poster child for AI debt fears, saw its bond yields retreat after its quarterly earnings on Tuesday eased investor concerns. The yield on its 9.75% bond due in 2031 was 14.79% on Friday after climbing to 16.7% last week. The spread on its five-year CDS also dropped more than 17% week to date, although it remained above 1,000 — which is generally considered a sign of distress.

It remains unclear how Nvidia's support for those companies may change, said CreditSights analyst Andy Li.

"We don't know what the ultimate strategy for Nvidia is going to be coming out the other side," he said.

"However, I think directionally, just given the fact that Jensen was so crucial in selling the Nvidia story and being a very aggressive deal maker, it's likely that a lot of this creative deal-making and circular financing and equity and credit support is going to moderate," he added.

CoreWeave CEO Michael Intrator told CNBC Wednesday, before Huang's departure, that Nvidia "is not one person."

"There are thousands of people over there that interface with thousands of people at my company every day. They integrate on the technology. They integrate on the physical infrastructure. They integrate on the networking," he said in an interview with "Squawk on the Street." "They have been a great partner for us for years. They will continue to be a great partner for us."

Bob Lang, founder and chief strategist at Explosive Options, doesn't see any big changes in terms of Nvidia's relevance or its ability to finance for now. As CFO, Kress was involved in the financing deals, and the search for a permanent CEO is going to take some time, he noted.

The real issue is just how much money the company will want to lend for AI financing, rather than, say using its hundreds of billions in free cash flow for other purposes, like share buybacks, capex, or acquisitions.

"Big companies like Nvidia and Meta, they're holding all the cards," he said. "When they decide that the game is over, that's it — it's going to be curtains for some of these smaller companies."

— CNBC'S Kif Leswing and Isabel O'Brien contributed reporting.

#2896. Raiders Week 11: Fernando Mendoza’s arm, legs key against stingy Broncos

With just 186 total points allowed in nine games, the Denver Broncos boast the ninth-best defensive unit in that regard, which will make it tough sledding for the Las Vegas Raiders this Sunday.

The Silver & Black Express rolls into Mile High with a new conductor in prized rookie quarterback Fernando Mendoza a road divisional matchup against the first-place Broncos (tied with the Kansas City Chiefs at 7-2 overall). The No. 1 overall pick in the 2026 NFL Draft, Mendoza made his first career start against the stifling Seattle Seahawks defense and Las Vegas fell 21-13 in Week 10.

Another formidable defense awaits Mendoza in Week 11 as Vance Joseph's Denver squad comes off a bye week after a wire-to-wire Week 9 26-21 win at the Carolina Panthers two Sunday's ago.

To hear the 23-year-old budding franchise quarterback tell it, however, he wouldn't have it any other way as he preps for his second-career NFL start this Sunday in Denver.

"I believe getting thrown in the fire is the best way to learn, and the Seahawks and the Broncos both have considerably elite defenses," Mendoza said when asked what it's like getting thrown into the fire against two quality NFL defenses. "I really believe every NFL defense is elite because when you're playing at this level, the margin for error is so small, and all the defensive coordinators are geniuses.

"So, I'm honored and blessed to go against a great defensive coordinator in Coach Robbie (Raiders defensive coordinator Rob Leonard) every single day, especially in OTAs and fall camp and training camp. So, I believe that's helped prepare me, an now I'm excited to embark on this journey with my teammates."

How Mendoza settles during his second start against an aggressive Broncos defense bears watching. The Seahawks produced a blueprint of how to and how not to defend the Indiana product last week. When protected with a clean (and sometimes noisy) pocket, Mendoza was a smooth operator going through progressions and using his accurate and strong arm to deliver the football to Raiders pass catchers.

In the first half, Mendoza went 11-of-13 for 90 yards including his first-career touchdown run. When Seattle saw enough of that, head coach and defensive play caller Mike Macdonald went to his bag of tricks of blitz, simulated pressure, and exotic alignments to take advantage of the neophyte signal caller and Mendoza went 6-of-16 for 53 yards in the second half while absorbing six sacks and 12 hits.

Expect Joseph to stick to a similar script and bring the pain on the 6-foot-5 and 225-pound Raiders quarterback. Especially considering the Broncos have 84 quarterback hits on the season alongside 26 sacks. Zach Allen is an ultra-productive defensive lineman who plays both inside as a tackle and outside as an edge rusher and paces Denver with 4.5 sacks and 23 quarterback hits. Edge rusher Nik Bonitto is right there with him with 4.5 sacks and 11 QB hits while defensive tackle Malcolm Roach is disruptive with four sacks and 13 quarterback hits.

Heat-seeking inside linebacker Alex Singleton is a quality clean-up man and is tied for the league lead in tackles at 107 (Raiders inside linebacker Nakobe Dean is who he is tied with) and also has two sacks and six quarterback hits on his stat sheet.

All of this is presented to note Mendoza's accurate arm and fresh legs are integral to Las Vegas' aspirations to walk out of Denver with an AFC West win. That dual-threat ability is essential to counter the Joseph's defense that is every bit as creative and chaotic as Macdonald's group. All it takes is a few successful scrambles to make a defense bite — Seattle did this — and with Mendoza lauded for his cerebral ways and ability to not make the same mistake again, watching the film from last Sunday's loss will showcase just that.

The second-quarter 22-yard dart Mendoza zipped to wide receiver Cody White is an example of this. Mendoza felt pressure, began to scramble but kept his eyes downfield, and his ability to thrown on the arm and velocity on the fast ball were on full display.

"We have a great strength and conditioning staff here, and performance staff as a whole, whether it's the training staff, nutrition, strength. There's so many different aspects of it, so I'm blessed to be a part of it," Mendoza began when as he answered a question about his mobility being an underrated aspect of his game, "I believe I've gotten faster since being here, and I'm grateful for that because in the NFL, those players are fast."

Those fleet feet will come in handy to escape the exotic blitzes Joseph is expected to deploy. Especially with Las Vegas ground game grinding to a halt consistently over the course of the 2026 season, much to the chagrin of Raiders rookie head coach and offensive play caller Klint Kubiak. Mendoza's ability to run helps provide an alternative run game and scrambling for hidden yards and first downs can frustrate the opposing defense.

Kubiak should again go play-action heavy with designed rollouts and bootlegs to vary the launch point of the ball and give his talented rookie cleaner passing lane and run-pass options. Mendoza has the arm talent to push the ball downfield and by getting the ball into the hands of his pass catchers quickly or downfield, Las Vegas can take advantage of Denver's penchant to whiff on tackles as the team has a high 16.2 percent missed tackle rate. Of course, it'd help to have a healthy Brock Bowers available as the tight end is the Raiders' de facto WR1.

Mendoza is keen on balancing staying in the pocket and winning with this arm while also knowing when to run and gain positive yards against Denver.

"There were a lot of moments in the game that we reviewed after in the film, whether it's Coach Kubiak, Coach (Andrew) Janocko or Coach (Mike) Sullivan, they made comments on whatever the situation was, whether it was some inefficiency or a play that in the future we want to change. So, I'm taking those day by day and trying to improve on them," Mendoza said regarding staying in the pocket longer to deliver a throw instead of taking off as quickly as he did in his first start.

While the Broncos boast the ninth-best red zone defense — teams have visited the space between the 20-yard line and end zone 29 times for only 15 touchdowns for a 51.7 percent conversion rate — they are susceptible to getting yards piled upon them allowing 3,195 yards for 27th most amongst the 32 teams.

That right there is a textbook example of a bend-but-not-break defense.

Sunday's Mile High tilt marks the 134th time the Raiders and Broncos go toe-to-toe. The Silver & Black lead the all-time series 73-58-2 (72-5702 in regular season play, 1-1 in the playoffs) but are riding a four-game losing skid against the Horsemen. The last time Las Vegas beat Denver was in January of 2024, a 27-14 victory inside Allegiant Stadium. Since that tilt, the Broncos have won the next four meetings 34-18, 29-19, 10-7, and 24-17. The pair of longtime AFC West foes will meet again in Week 15 in Las Vegas.

"We were in a zone, it was an extended play, and expecting a quick throw and it became something — when extended plays happen, we always talk about the second play, and plaster, and those types of things. Anytime something doesn't go well I wish I would have called something different."

—Las Vegas Raiders defensive coordinator Rob Leonard on Seattle Seahawks wideout Jaxon Smith-Njigba's 71-yard touchdown on a broken play

#2895. A massive bearish trade just happened in the chip stocks

So much for buying the dip.As the S&P 500 entered correction territory and the Nasdaq-100 flirted with bear-market territory, down 19% from highs as of midday Friday, the single biggest options trade of the day was a near $100-million bet that the VanEck Semiconductor ETF (SMH) has a long way more to fall.Around 12:30 E.T., someone spent $93 million buying 20,000 380-strike puts expiring Sep. 17, 2027, according to trading data reviewed by SpotGamma and Volland, options-analytics platforms that both show the trade as a likely opening position, with volume exceeding existing open interest in the contract. The trade bets that the index will fall below $380, a nearly 8% decline from its current level. The SMH is off 40% from its recent all-time highs.

"That trade stands out big-time," wrote Jason DeLorenzo, founder and owner of Volland. "It is either a hedge or speculative, but either way it isn't a vote of confidence."The puts were bought for $46.50 a piece, an arguably expensive choice for a downside bet with volatility in the ETF trading at 70.

"You're paying a metric ton of Vega for that thing," added SpotGamma's Brent Kochuba, referring to the contract's sensitivity to changes in volatility of the underlying chip ETF. "Maybe they were a forced buyer, or maybe they're actually selling this, which can still be the case even though the price shows it happening closer to the ask."

The semiconductor index headed for a 9% drop this week alone.

#2893. Pistons vs Raptors Discussion: Game Time, TV, Odds, and More

The Toronto Raptors are scuffling in the first leg of the NBA season, but there is certainly a lot of talent there. Yes, Kawhi Leonard is absent with news of managing knee pain and swelling that surely puts a shiver up the spine of every Toronto fan. But they also still have Scottie Barnes, and he is delivering the young star goods as advertised. He is averaging 23.8 points, 7.4 rebounds, and 4.7 assists a night. On certain nights, he looks like the young superstar that represents his clearly attainable ceiling. On other nights, he and his team's offense struggle and can't seem to get unstuck from the mud. He's doing a lot of things, but he isn't doing it as efficiently or consistently as you'd like.

The real problems with the Raptors, though, lie elsewhere. Nobody can seemingly hit a three. RJ Barrett is getting to the line five times a game and hitting just 60% from the charity stripe. Collin Murray-Boyles has regressed, and Jakob Poeltl's game has fallen off a cliff. It feels like if you solve Scottie, it helps you solve Barrett and Immanuel Quickley, and therefore, you solve the Raptors. Toronto has dropped six straight, and will be without the services of Barrett tonight.

Watching an Ausar Thompson and Scottie Barnes matchup could be electric. Barnes doesn't have the defensive chops Ausar does, and Ausar doesn't have the offensive versatility, but both could be liable to pull off a triple-double tonight. Cade Cunningham is also expected back tonight after sitting out Detroit's win against the New Orleans Pelicans. Duncan Robinson and Ron Holland are out, and doubtful, respectively.
