The Bears are a mismatch against the Saints, and Vegas thinks the Bears will cruise in this one, positioning them as a double-digit favorite. How will the Bears players do in this one? Here are 3 props we like this week. As always, odds courtesy of FanDuel.

Monangai is back, and I think he's going to be a huge boost to the running game. I think the Bears have their most efficient game on the ground that they've had in some time, and I think Monangai will get plenty of opportunities upon his return. I think Monangai will be the one to get the bulk of the 4th-quarter carries, and that should launch him over his total for the game.

If I'm accurate in that assessment, how am I not predicting Caleb to go over his passing total? Caleb has gone over that number every game this season except the monsoon against the Vikings. #2891. 3 thoughts as the Mavericks face another winnable matchup at home against the Los Angeles Clippers

The Dallas Mavericks (8-7) remain an interesting case study in the topsy-turvy NBA ahead of Saturday's clash with the Los Angeles Clippers (4-10), and their own penchant for wild swings in performance level are only part of that story.

They've got significant injuries holding them back in some ways, but that comes against the backdrop of players like Zaccharie Risacher and Caleb Martin, who are enjoying various levels of resurgence this year. Kyrie Irving is back and generally playing well, but how back is he really, and how committed are the Mavericks to seeing Irving finish the season with the team? Naji Marshall is playing the role in terms of minutes of a guy who just got extended for three years after setting a career-high scoring mark, but he's averaging just 10.9 points and 2.8 rebounds per game in November, so what gives there? Marshall seems to simply be the lesser evil right now in the mid-off between himself and Max Christie.

The stew is pungent and rich, and it should not be overlooked that, despite the mounting question marks, an 8-7 record is at least one step ahead of schedule as we near the season's quarter pole. The best part about that record is the chance to make it 9-7 against another vulnerable opponent at American Airlines Center on Saturday.

The Clip Show this year is a Darius Garland-Brook Lopez-Rui Hachimura production, with a little Keaton Wagler and Derrick Jones Jr. sprinkled in thus far. The 38-year-old Lopez, in particular, averaging 15.4 points on better than 55% shooting from the floor, is an inspiration for all of us who are past our prime. He's scored 23 or more in three of the Clippers' last four games and 20 or more in five of the last seven. That includes games against the San Antonio Spurs (23) and the Oklahoma City Thunder (26). He' shooting lights out from 3-point range — nearly 45% to start the year.

And Lopez isn't the only Clipper doing work from long range this season. LA is second in the NBA in team 3-point percentage, at 39.2%. Hachimura and Jones Jr. are also hitting about two per game from deep on surprisingly solid shooting clips. Jones' 50% mark on 3.7 attempts per game thus far this year will likely regress, but he's been feeling it from out there to say the least. Max Strus, Kriss Dunn, Gradey Dick and Wagler are all shooters as well.

But yet, they're 4-10. They don't rebound well, they turn the ball over the second most (17.6 per game) in the NBA and they rank 25th in defensive rating (117.2). A tale as old as the peach-basket net, really. Irving and Cooper Flagg should be able to have their way with this bunch, as they did on Wednesday against the flailing Golden State Warriors.

Mavericks first-year head coach Dusty May was asked on Friday if the team is close to having center Dereck Lively II back in action. He gave a very Clintonesque answer, questioning the technical definition of the word "close," which is never a good sign. Yes, Bill, er, Dusty, the whole question does center around what the definition of the word "is" is.

"I asked the same question this morning & close is a term that was used by D-Live," May said, according to the Dallas Morning News. "And when you look at it from the magnitude that he's been out for a while. He's doing a lot more now, but close, I'm not sure what close is. I would have to defer to our medical staff on that. He's in great spirits. He's moving well. I don't know what the exact ramp-up and build-up is, but he's progressing."

"I'm not sure what close is," is doing a lot of heavy lifting in that answer. Close is close. Can you put a number on the number of days or weeks away the player is? No? Ok, then it's not close. It's far.

The whole saga serves to weaken the premise that the Mavs' frontcourt was a definite strength coming into the season. The forwards, led by Flagg and a mildly resurgent PJ Washington, are playing well, but the center position remains a revolving door of things thrown against a wall. The ancient Lopez may find himself in a comfortable spot against the Mavs on Saturday as Dallas looks for a stopgap, but the rest of the team can compensate in other matchups.

The Mavericks have done well against 3-point shooters to start the season, with opponents shooting just 33.4% from distance against them through 15 games. That's the fifth-best mark in the league, and before you make the "variance" claim, just know, that includes a game at the Celtics in which Boston made 23-of-55 (42%) and at the Timberwolves in which Minnesota canned 15-of-32 (47%).

There appears to be something to the Mavs' ability to close out on shooters. If Dallas can limit the Clippers' production from deep, the Mavericks have the ability to make quick work of a Los Angeles side that seems to lack identity above all else.

The Mavericks and the Clippers tip off from American Airlines Center shortly after 7 p.m. on Saturday. Catch the game locally on KFAA Channel 29 or on a sister station throughout the team's regional viewership area. The stream will be on MavsTV or NBA League pass where available. The radio broadcast will be on KRLD 105.3 The Fan.

#2890. Luka Doncic scores 25, Lakers barely hold off Kings 106-104 on LaVine's miss at the buzzer

LOS ANGELES (AP) — Luka Doncic had 25 points and eight assists, Quentin Grimes hit a tiebreaking 3-pointer with 57 seconds to play, and the Los Angeles Lakers rallied in the fourth quarter for their 10th win in 11 games, 106-104 over the Sacramento Kings on Friday night.

Walker Kessler scored 15 points and Matisse Thybulle had 14 for the Lakers, who struggled at home against another rebuilding opponent two nights after their nine-game winning streak ended with a loss to the Utah Jazz.

The Lakers hung on for their fifth straight home victory over Sacramento when Zach LaVine missed a long 3-pointer at the buzzer.

De'Andre Hunter scored a season-high 30 points and hit five 3-pointers for the Kings, who dropped to 1-14 on the season — the worst 15-game start in team history — with their eighth straight loss despite taking a lead into the fourth quarter.

Sacramento led by 11 in the third quarter and took a small advantage into the fourth, but the Lakers built a 101-93 lead with 4 1/2 minutes to play. The Kings responded with eight straight points to tie it on Domantas Sabonis' free throws with 2:32 left.

After Grimes made his only 3-pointer of the night, Doncic made a turnover with 23 seconds left. Kessler then bricked two free throws to give the Kings one last chance, but LaVine pulled up and clanged his final shot off the rim.

LaVine scored 19 points. Sabonis had 19 points and 16 rebounds for Sacramento, and Darius Acuff Jr. had 18 points and 12 assists.

The Lakers improved to 2-1 in NBA Cup play. Sacramento is 0-3.

Austin Reaves missed his fourth straight game with a bruised heel. The Lakers' second-leading scorer has already missed five of their first 17 games due to injury after missing 31 games last season.

Kessler's first 3-pointer in a Lakers uniform put LA ahead early in the fourth.

Precious Achiuwa sat out for Sacramento with a leg injury.

#2889. Nvidia stock plunges 14%, wraps up longest weekly losing streak since 2008 financial crisis

The last time Wall Street was this down on Nvidia the chipmaker's flagship product was the GeForce graphics processor for high-end PC video games, and markets were reeling from the global financial crisis.

Nvidia shares tumbled 14% on Friday, sliding for a seventh straight day as investors grew increasingly concerned about life after co-founder Jensen Huang. The stock closed lower for a seventh consecutive week, the longest such stretch since 2008.

Shares of Nvidia are down 46% over the period, pushing the company's market cap down by about $2.6 trillion. The stock is now trading at roughly 8.4 times forward earnings, while Advanced Micro Devices is valued at 29 times profit for the next 12 months.

On Friday, Reuters reported that the Securities and Exchange Commission is exploring potential accounting issues at Nvidia, pushing the stock down further.

Once the most valuable company in the world, Nvidia now has a market cap of $3 trillion, putting it fourth behind Apple, Microsoft and Alphabet.

Nvidia is reeling from the removal of Huang, who resigned on Nov. 12, after multiple reported affairs, including one with a Chinese businesswoman who was allegedly a spy.

Harvey Jones, Nvidia's newly appointed board chair, said earlier this week that Huang had resigned after "disclosure of personal behavior inconsistent with the company's code of conduct, standards and cultural norms."

But the chipmaker's losing streak started before Nvidia was thrown into crisis as investors worried over the sustainability of the AI buildout, particularly after the midterm elections, and emerging competition in AI chips from Google and AMD.

Even a better-than-expected earnings report, which included an increase to fiscal 2028 guidance, didn't stem the selling. Interim CEO Colette Kress, the company's longtime CFO, projected revenue growth next fiscal year of 75%, and said that customers aren't going anywhere.

On Tuesday's earnings call with analysts, Kress signed off by saying, "we've got this." In a company meeting the next day, executives told staff that it was moving its yearly review process, which can come with more stock, from March to December.

For investors, one major question is whether Nvidia can continue to innovate and sustain its commanding lead in the AI chip market without its visionary leader. Almost the entire company now is built around the AI trade, a phenomenon that began taking hold just four years ago with the launch of OpenAI's ChatGPT.

Before that, Nvidia's primary business was selling graphics cards used for video games. In mid-2008, the last time the stock was this deep into a weekly losing streak, Nvidia was generating less than $1 billion per quarter. Now it pulls in more than $1 billion per day. At the time, the entire market was in freefall due to the worst financial crisis since the Great Depression.

#2888. Cowboys changing things up in search of consistency vs. Titans

Both the Dallas Cowboys, and their closest division rival this season, the New York Giants, host opponents from the AFC South this weekend. If the Cowboys were even living up to half of the expectations of this season, this Week 11 schedule would look like a golden opportunity to separate from the Giants, as they face the 7-2 Jacksonville Jaguars while the Cowboys have the 1-8 Tennessee Titans.

Instead, the Cowboys already blew an opportunity to win in the same week the Giants lost against the San Francisco 49ers. Even if the Giants do lose as home underdogs to the Jaguars, the Cowboys have invited enough added drama this week with a change of defensive play-callers to make the Titans feel like another handful of an opponent.

This is especially true considering how close both other games against AFC South foes have been this year, both in the win column, but neither convincing. Getting a convincing win is something Cowboys fans simply do not have faith in their team doing at all right now, and not something the Titans have given away easily despite struggling under the new coaching of Robert Saleh and offensive coordinator Brian Daboll.

Hope for the playoffs will still feel thin even with a win Dallas is expected to get against these Titans. The Cowboys have made their bed with this roster and coaching staff and will have to withstand however many embarrassing losses can come from it, with the Titans having the potential to be the worst of 2026.

It is time for our weekly look at how the Cowboys avoid this loss and go into Thanksgiving with a chance at a winning streak again, or what could lead to the latest AT&T Stadium embarrassment after all.

Every other week for the Cowboys passing attack seems to feel like a CeeDee Lamb feature game right now, with Lamb having a big say in the win over the Colts in week nine, but it was George Pickens' best game of the season with three touchdowns in week ten agaisnt the 49ers.

The fact we are still waiting into late November for a game where both Lamb and Pickens are established in a game plan and get going at the same time is a horrible sign for play-caller Brian Schottenheimer. Beating bad AFC teams is one thing he's proven capable of though, and if now isn't the time to see both Lamb and Pickens unleashed downfield, it may never come.

The Titans defense is effectively tied with the Las Vegas Raiders for worst in the league in yards per passing attempt allowed. All three of their cornerbacks that led the team in snaps last week against the Jaguars are new to the team this season – Cor'Dale Flott, Alontae Taylor, and Joshua Williams.

Tennessee is also the fourth worst team at getting to the quarterback this season with 14 sacks, just one more than the 49ers team the Cowboys offense scored 40 points against last week. The Cowboys defense is more of an unknown than ever before coming into this game after already underperforming all season, so the adage that Dallas can win this game by having Dak Prescott simply outplay Cam Ward by a big margin is not a reliable one.

That said, another huge Prescott game against this opponent where he's able to effectively get the ball deep to both of his top receivers should be enough to get in the win column. The Cowboys have beaten Robert Saleh defenses consistently before, and will have to do so again to keep faint NFC East hopes alive this Sunday.

The football gods always seem to work in mysterious ways, in the sense they will test the very thing a team is struggling with in the blink of an eye. The Cowboys reasoning for having the veteran coach Mike Pettine take over defensive play-calling from rookie Christian Parker has more layers than an onion. One of the more sound reasons though is that the team is heavily invested financially in their front seven, where Quinnen Williams has looked more and more like a cornerstone player to build around each week in his first full season with the team.

Pettine will likely look to run the defense with a front-centric mindset, focusing on this front seven first and then the backend, where as Parker was the opposite. For a coach that wasn't here for any offseason activity at all, and only been in the building since October, his first test of this new approach comes against a challenging opponent.

With last year's first overall pick Cam Ward at quarterback, the Titans rank in the 98th percentile in their utilization of the quick passing game this season. They have yet another dual-threat, home run hitting running back in former Cowboy Tony Pollard. Ward's best trait has always been his ability to throw the ball out of a moving pocket, and the Titans are figuring out in real time how to protect him and also target the middle of the field at a high rate to open up the sophomore passer's game.

The Titans lone win this season came in week six against the Colts, but this is what Ward's passing chart looked like:

For comparison, the Titans didn't beat the Jaguars last week, but they put up numbers offensively and played well, and this is how Ward threw the ball to do so:

If Mike Pettine is going to make a difference for the Cowboys after they just allowed a franchise record for yards given up in a home game, to a 49ers team that exposed them in the middle of the field, with a mobile QB as well, doing so against the Titans would actually be a solid start.

With the ball coming out so quickly from Ward's hands, the Cowboys likely can't rely on the pass rush element they got against the 49ers with five sacks either. Pass rush will have to come from sound coverage in the short to intermediate part of the field, where the Titans will look to stress Shemar James, Caleb Downs, and even Rashan Gary who got up field without contain on a number of big 49ers plays last week.

If the Cowboys cannot handle these things, and also struggle to get multiple bodies to Pollard in the run game and allow him to get around the edge or push the pile, it could be yet another long afternoon for Brian Schottenheimer's team against an inferior opponent.

#2887. Is a bottom in for the Nasdaq-100? Charts to watch

(See the video above for Frank's complete chart breakdown.)

Is the Nasdaq-100's recent bounce a sign that the bottom is in? We're going to look at several charts to help answer that question.

First, let's remember how we got here. In September, the NDX broke out from a bullish cup-and-handle pattern, producing a 33,200-upside target. That breakout quickly failed, however, and gave way to a bearish pattern that broke down aggressively two weeks ago.

The decline brought the index relatively close to its 26,350 downside target before the current rebound began.

Zooming out, it's telling to see where the bounce originated. Three important forms of support converged in the 25,000-26,000 area:

This confluence of support helped encourage buying after a roughly 15% decline.

For perspective, we also see what larger drawdowns of 20%, 30%, 40%, and 50% would look like … a lot worse.

The following chart tracks NDX drawdowns from 52-week highs going back to just after the financial crisis. Only a handful of declines exceeded 20%, and three produced relatively quick reversals: late 2018, the Covid lows in 2020 and April 2025.

Historically, buying into declines of approximately 15%–25% has generally paid off, even when additional downside occurred first (yellow highlights).

The chart also highlights previous declines of 10%–16% in green, putting the current pullback into perspective.

The major exception was 2022, when the NDX eventually declined nearly 36% over approximately 10 months ... a much worse outcome, which many investors are concerned could happen again now.

That raises the key question: Are we experiencing another routine correction, or the early stages of a larger bear market?

One important clue will be how far this bounce progresses.

Any rally that fails to reclaim the previous highs could establish a significant lower high. Additional lower highs would provide stronger evidence of a developing, longer downtrend.

We saw this in March 2022, when that first sharp rebound effort failed to reach the previous high before a much more severe decline followed.

The bottom line: The current bounce is encouraging, particularly given where it originated and knowing how well buying prior 10-20% drawdowns has paid off.

From here, it's all about the follow through.  We don't need to see new highs right away, but any rally from here that's met with another wave of strong selling could reignite downside momentum.

Frank Cappelleri is the founder and president of CappThesis, an independent research firm that helps active investors through time-tested chart and statistical analysis. Prior to starting CappThesis, he spent 25 years on Wall Street as an equity sales trader, technical analyst, research sales specialist and desk strategist.

#2885. Why Darian DeVries 'would love' to continue Indiana-Kentucky basketball rivalry

INDIANAPOLIS — Indiana basketball coach Darian DeVries doesn't want the Kentucky series to go back into hibernation.

The schools are midway through a four-year scheduling pact, but DeVries said after Friday's night's 90-76 win over the Wildcats at Lucas Oil Stadium that there should be no end date to the border rivalry that used to be an annual fixture for college hoops.

"I would love to keep it going," DeVries said. "I think it's great for college basketball. It's fun games for everybody."

In 2011, former Indiana forward Christian Watford, who attended Friday night's game, helped send the series into an extended hiatus by sinking a buzzer-beater 3-pointer to upset No. 1 Kentucky.

Former Wildcats coach John Calipari resisted scheduling a home-and-home series against the Hoosiers for years.

UK didn't play IU outside of a pair of meetings in the NCAA tournament (2012 and 2016) until the series resumed in 2025. The Hoosiers' win on Friday was the first game between the programs in the state of Indiana since Watford's heroics.

#2884. Daniel Jones has been released by the Colts. Should the Patriots go after him?

You heard about a Friday news dump, but have you heard about a Friday QB dump? That's precisely what the Indianapolis Colts did when they released their starting quarterback, Daniel Jones, earlier today.

Jones, 29, was in his second season with the franchise and just eight months removed from signing a two-year, $88 million contract extension. However, he and the Colts as a whole have struggled mightily this year, leading to some sweeping changes within the organization. First, they fired the GM who signed him to that deal, Chris Ballard, and now they let go of Jones himself.

His release comes with the Colts at 3-8 and Jones having completed 205 of 351 pass attempts (58.4%) for 1,974 yards with seven touchdowns and 13 interceptions. A year removed from tearing his Achilles tendon, he is ranked 30th among 31 qualifying quarterbacks in EPA per play this season (-0.139) and 31st in both net yards per attempt (4.8) and completion percentage above expectation (-6.1).

Long story short, he has been dreadful. And yet, the New England Patriots might just keep a close eye on him.

The Patriots, after all, saw their own starting QB, Drake Maye, suffer a fractured fibula during last Sunday's loss versus the Detroit Lions. While it remains to be seen how long he will be out, Maye missing most if not all of his team's seven final regular season games this year would not come as a surprise.

The question now becomes whether or not the Patriots should pursue Jones as a replacement. Let's make the argument in favor of and against such a move.

Before making this argument, it needs to be prefaced with this: everything in this section only applies to Jones going through the waiver wire unclaimed and making it to the open market. According to Jason Fitzgerald of Over the Cap, a team would need $6.35 million in salary cap space to claim Jones — financial flexibility the Patriots do not have at the moment. If he passes waivers, he could be signed to a deal worth as little as $505,554.

With that out of the way, let's take a look at why brining Jones in would make sense for the Patriots. It all starts with one question: would he be an upgrade over Tommy DeVito for however long Drake Maye will be sidelined?

His play this season so far suggests that that might not be the case. However, a change in environment might help Jones regain some of the confidence he had in his first year with the Colts, when he played some of the best football of his career and finished the season sixth in the league in EPA per play (0.201). If the Patriots can get a fraction of that, they should be happy with Jones as a better alternative than DeVito.

In addition, Jones also has some familiarity with the Patriots scheme. While he never played directly under offensive coordinator Josh McDaniels, he did spend the better part of three seasons under former New England assistant Brian Daboll. The offense Daboll ran with the Giants has similarities to McDaniels' in terms of terminology, quarterback responsibility and play designs, meaning that the lead-up time for Jones to get ready would be comparatively small.

Inserting Jones would likely also not come with significant philosophical changes. In essence, after all, he is a similar player type as Maye is: somebody capable of making plays with his arm as well as his legs, as evidenced by his career rushing average of 6.1 yards per carry.

While his highs have not been close to Maye's, he has shown that he can be an adequate NFL starting quarterback and somebody able to elevate the talent around him. That obviously was not the case in Indianapolis this season, but if a change of scenery and move to a better-run team can get him close to that, it would be precisely what the Patriots need right now if there are any doubts about DeVito.

And even if not, what do the Patriots have to lose? They are 5-5 and fighting for their playoff lives, and signing Jones would not automatically disqualify them from starting DeVito. At the very least, he would provide veteran depth better suited to challenge the post-Maye hierarchy than sixth-round rookie Behren Morton.

On Friday, Jones became the first quarterback in the NFL in more than three decades to get released less than 24 hours after starting a game. That plus the fact the Colts are willing to cut their losses despite the financial investment they made in him just this offseason should tell you all you need to know.

While there might be a snowballing effect to Jones' struggles and a fresh start could indeed help him regain the form he previously showed, there is no denying he has played some of the worst football in the NFL this year. The numbers mentioned above are an illustration of that.

Some of it had to do with the surroundings and a Colts team that a) lacking quality across the board, and b) is in a free fall since October. In addition, his injury situation also seems to have played a part in it: 12 months after his torn Achilles, he still does not look like he is close to 100% and fails to consistently to step into throws, maneuver the pocket and pick up yards on the ground, averaging just 3.8 yards per carry.

On top of it, Jones has been a streaky player throughout his career. While there have been stretches of play where he lived up to his former first-round status, the lows have been underwhelming, including this season.

Given all of that, expecting a quick turnaround in New England would be naive, particularly considering that the Patriots are missing several offensive pieces because of injury. But even if they didn't, Jones would not have any familiarity with them: despite some experience in a McDaniels-style system, he would have to build chemistry with his receiving corps, offensive line and coaching staff on the fly.

Tommy DeVito, meanwhile, has a two-year head start in that regard. Speaking of DeVito, he was signed to a contract extension in the offseason to serve as QB2 and has, by all accounts, been a model citizen deserving of his shot. Obviously, the job should go to the best option regardless of contract situation. That said, all things considered — including what a change might mean for the locker room — DeVito still has a strong case to be just that.

Even though they are still in a competitive position on paper, the Patriots are fighting for their playoff lives with Drake Maye sidelined for the foreseeable future. Their margin for error, meanwhile, is, to quote safety Kevin Byard, "basically at zero."

Would adding Jones increase it and allow New England to play winning football during Maye's absence? Ideally, that is not a question they would have to ask; Tommy DeVito stepping in and leading the offense at an adequate level would be the best-case scenario. Of course, as the Lions game showed, there is a realistic chance the lights are too bright for him.

The problem is, they also might be for Jones. So far this season, after all, he has not been good and not been able to hold up to the pressure of being the guy at quarterback in Indianapolis. He wouldn't quite be that in New England, sure, but the eyes of one of the NFL's biggest markets would still be on him.

Would that prevent the Patriots from signing him? Probably not. Still, it is one of the factors to consider alongside the arguments outlined above. And putting all of those together, it is clear the decision whether or not to sign Jones as a potential stand-in for Maye is quite nuanced. At the end of the day, Mike Vrabel, Eliot Wolf and Ryan Cowden — the latter of whom spent time with Jones in New York — will have to see their way through it.

And once they do, they very well might find that the potential disruption caused by adding Jones might not be worth the theoretical gains.

What do you think about Daniel Jones? Should the Patriots pursue him, or steer clear of him? Please head down to the comment section to share your thoughts, or jump into the following discussion thread set up by community member Gonzucla in the Feed.

#2882. A trading strategy to ride out the Nvidia uncertainty and generate income in the process

Jensen Huang's abrupt resignation has sent shockwaves through the tech sector, dragging Nvidia's (NVDA) stock down roughly 30% since Oct. 6. The market despises uncertainty, and losing a visionary founder, particularly one without a well-telegraphed succession plan, makes it worse.

However, for opportunistic investors, this panic has made the stock cheaper (obviously) while options have become significantly more expensive. Trading in the $160s, Nvidia's valuation has taken a big haircut, yet its underlying business fundamentals remain largely intact. While Huang was the architect of Nvidia's AI dominance, the company's moat — superior GPUs, the CUDA software ecosystem, and networking expertise — does not walk out the door with him. The company's structural advantage in the global artificial intelligence rollout persists.

Buying good companies at a discount is investing 101, but the current market dynamics may do something else for those who use options.  Huang's departure guarantees that Nvidia will be operating under a cloud of transition for the foreseeable future. Executing a permanent CEO search while navigating global regulatory scrutiny takes time. Consequently, Nvidia's implied volatility (IV)—the market's pricing of expected future price swings — has spiked to highs not seen since the tariff tantrum in the first half of 2025, and this elevated IV is highly likely to persist for several months as the leadership drama slowly unfolds.

This prolonged period of high implied volatility can be a goldmine for option sellers, specifically those executing options overlays such as covered call strategies. When you buy NVDA shares at these depressed levels and simultaneously sell out-of-the-money call options against your position, the premiums you collect are directly tied to IV. Because fear and transition-related uncertainty have dramatically inflated these option prices, the income generated from selling covered calls is now significantly higher than usual.

This lowers the risk profile of holding the stock considerably over time. Consistently collecting these inflated premiums systematically lowers your overall cost basis in the underlying shares. If the stock trades sideways or recovers slowly during the CEO search, you are generating an outsized cash yield on a stagnant asset. If the stock experiences further downside turbulence, the heavy premium income acts as a substantial downside buffer, meaningfully reducing the strategy's overall risk over time.

By deploying a persistent covered call strategy, investors can leverage the exact fear driving the stock down to generate robust, risk-reducing income while maintaining exposure to the undeniable future of AI.

The Utah Jazz released an injury update on Jusuf Nurkic today. It looks like Nurkic was re-evaluated, is making progress, and will be re-evaluated again in one week.

#2880. Colts' mismanagement continues with release of QB Daniel Jones

The Colts arguably mishandled the Jonathan Taylor situation, when it comes to the possibility of trading him. As to the possibility of trading Daniel Jones, there’s no argument to be made.

He has played two games since the deadline came and went, and now he’s been cut. They should have seen it coming.

They cut him to avoid an injury that would have triggered a $10.51 million guarantee in 2027. A trade would have avoided that guarantee. And they would have gotten something.

Now? They get nothing. They’ll pay him $50 million for 11 games and have three wins to show for it.

The Vikings were interested, and they were talking to the Colts. While Minnesota wanted Kirk Cousins, the Colts could have lowered the price to a point that the Vikings couldn’t have refused.

Then again, the Vikings may have used the possibility of pivoting to Jones to get the Raiders down to a third-round pick for Cousins.

Regardless, the Colts blew it. It’s no surprise. They have no General Manager. Which makes it hard to generally manage the decisions that come with the opportunities to trade players.

Our guess is that the trades of guard Quenton Nelson and defensive tackle DeForest Buckner created the perception that the Colts were having a fire sale. Which resulted in lower offers for players like Taylor and Jones.

In the end, they traded neither. Now, Jones is gone. Taylor possibly will ask to be traded after the season, or they’ll decide to do it again on their own. Chances are that, when they do, they’ll get less than they could have gotten if they’d traded him at the deadline.

#2879. Why Darian DeVries 'would love' to continue Indiana-Kentucky basketball rivalry

INDIANAPOLIS — Indiana basketball coach Darian DeVries doesn't want the Kentucky series to go back into hibernation.

The schools are midway through a four-year scheduling pact, but DeVries said after Friday's night's 90-76 win over the Wildcats at Lucas Oil Stadium that there should be no end date to the border rivalry that used to be an annual fixture for college hoops.

"I would love to keep it going," DeVries said. "I think it's great for college basketball. It's fun games for everybody."

In 2011, former Indiana forward Christian Watford, who attended Friday night's game, helped send the series into an extended hiatus by sinking a buzzer-beater 3-pointer to upset No. 1 Kentucky.

Former Wildcats coach John Calipari resisted scheduling a home-and-home series against the Hoosiers for years.

UK didn't play IU outside of a pair of meetings in the NCAA tournament (2012 and 2016) until the series resumed in 2025. The Hoosiers' win on Friday was the first game between the programs in the state of Indiana since Watford's heroics.

Kentucky will host next year's matchup at Rupp Arena before the teams return to Assembly Hall in 2028. DeVries wants to reward the passion he witnessed from the 23,078 fans who attended Friday night's game by keeping the series alive.

"The fanbases, the proximity, to have two historic programs like this to continue to play in the non-conference, yeah, I would love to continue it," DeVries said.

#2878. Berkshire breaks out to record high with fourth-quarter rally reaching 19%

(This is the Warren Buffett Watch newsletter, news and analysis on all things Warren Buffett and Berkshire Hathaway. You can sign up here to receive it every Friday evening in your inbox.)

Berkshire Hathaway's red-hot shares are ending a strong week with four consecutive fresh record high closes.

Since the rally began with the start of the fourth quarter on October 1, the Class A shares have closed at record highs 11 times as they soared 19.1%.

The much more widely held B shares closed at new highs just 10 times but are also up 19.1% from their near-term low.

The gains are in sharp contrast to the S&P 500's 1.2% dip as investors appear to be seeing Berkshire as a relatively safe haven amid worries about AI stocks and spending.

Both Berkshire classes were up 3.9% on the week as the S&P advanced by 2.5%, its best week since early August.

Berkshire's B shares are now 7.5 percentage points ahead of the benchmark for the year so far.

After erasing their post-Buffett CEO announcement losses three weeks ago, Berkshire shares are now up almost 10% since last year's shareholders meeting.

They've gained around 29% since last August's lows and are up around 18% year-to-date.

Berkshire Hathaway is due to disclose its quarterly equity portfolio snapshot on Monday when it files its Q3 13F with the SEC.

We won't know for sure until then, but numbers in the company's third quarter 10-Q, released last Saturday, indicate Berkshire continued to reduce its Bank of America stake.

It would be the ninth consecutive quarter of selling.

As of June 30, Berkshire reported holding 483.4 million BAC shares valued now at $24.7 billion, it's fifth largest equity position.

On June 30, 2024, before the selling began, Berkshire owned just over 1 billion shares valued at $41.1 billion.

Since then, the number of shares in the portfolio has decreased by 53.2%, not including any Q3 sales, while the stock price has increased by 28.3%.

If there had been no reductions, the position would be worth $52.7 billion.

The belief Berkshire sold more BAC shares in the third quarter is based on a $372 million reduction in the cost basis for equities categorized by the company as "banks, insurance, and finance."

The 10-Q also reports $2.2 billion of pretax gains from equity sales.

Barron's notes Bank of America and American Express are the only financial stocks in the portfolio that have a cost basis low enough to generate a gain like that. Amex is one of the four stocks new CEO Greg Abel indicated would be held for "decades" to come in his February letter to shareholders.

In any case, Berkshire's almost 23% stake in Amex would have required it to quickly disclose any sales.

Based on the numbers in the 10-Q, the warrants Berkshire received as part of a 2011 $5 billion investment in the bank that it used in 2017 to buy 700 million shares at $7.14 each, and BAC's average stock price in the quarter, Barron's estimates 30 to 40 million shares may have been sold.

That would be roughly in line with the 30.2 million shares sold in the second quarter.

The case isn't as good, but Barron's thinks Berkshire may have bought more Alphabet shares in the period. That's based on a $2.3 billion increase in the cost basis for the category that includes Google's parent.

Overall, it looks like it will be a relatively quiet quarter with $2.4 billion in equity purchases and $2.8 billion in sales.

In the first quarter, Berkshire bought $15.9 billion and sold $24.1 billion of equities. In the second quarter, it purchased $23.5 billion and sold $3.7 billion.

All those numbers exclude the company's buybacks of its own stock.

In a term sheet filed early Friday, Berkshire Hathaway says it is selling more than 182 billion yen ($1.1 billion) worth of bonds with maturities between 2029 and 2056 and annual interest rates between 2.6% and 4.6%.

Bloomberg points out the rates, compared to benchmarks, are higher than its previous sale this year.

It quotes a fund manager as saying market sentiment "has become more sensitive," forcing issuers to make their offerings more attractive.

In April, Berkshire sold 272 billion yen ($1.7 billion) of bonds.

Berkshire uses the proceeds from the yen-denominated bonds to protect its ongoing investments in Japanese stocks from currency fluctuations.

Warren Buffett recommends Benjamin Graham's book, "The Intelligent Investor," and outlines what he considers Graham's three most important ideas.

WARREN BUFFETT: On investment books, if you're asking about that, I would recommend the first two books that Phil Fisher wrote back around 1960, "Common Sense [Stocks] and Uncommon Profits" and the second one ["Paths to Wealth Through Common Stocks"]. They're very good books.

You know, I obviously recommend, first and foremost, [Benjamin Graham's] "The Intelligent Investor," with chapters eight and 20 are the ones that you really should read.

Two of the — well, all of the important ideas in investing, really, are in that book, because there's only about three ideas. And those — two of them are emphasized in those two chapters...

One is that — to think of yourself — to think of investing as owning a business and not buying something that wiggles around in price.

And the second one is your attitude, which ties in with that, the attitude toward the market, that's covered in chapter eight. And if you have the proper attitude toward market movements, it's an enormous help in securities.

And the final chapter is on the margin of safety, which means, don't try and drive a 9,800-pound truck over a bridge that says it's, you know, "Capacity: 10,000 pounds." But go down the road a little bit and find one that says, "Capacity: 15,000 pounds."

Excluding Rail Cash and Subtracting T-Bills Payable: $352.3 billion (Down 1.9% from June 30)

#2876. Las Vegas Raiders-Denver Broncos FEED Topic: Kolton Miller, DJ Glaze vs. Nik Bonitto, Jonathon Cooper

The Seattle Seahawks managed to get six sacks on Fernando Mendoza in his starting debut last week, and pressure on the quarterback will be another major key to the game for the Las Vegas Raiders' offense in Week 11. The Denver Broncos present the same problem but in a different way than Seattle, one that will place a greater emphasis on the Raiders' offensive line, especially tackles Kolton Miller and DJ Glaze.

Led by head coach Mike Macdonald, the Seahawks defense uses a creative scheme to help manufacture pressure and create confusion for opposing quarterbacks. To a certain extent, they're able to work around or neutralize the offensive line by attacking running backs in pass protection or forcing quarterbacks to account for free rushers by throwing hot. Last week was a perfect example, as top cornerback Devon Witherspoon led the team with two of the six sacks.

Meanwhile, Broncos defensive coordinator Vance Joseph isn't afraid to blitz, but that's to help create one-on-one opportunities for their defensive linemen, especially edge rushers Nik Bonitto and Jonathon Cooper.

Bonitto is coming off back-to-back Pro Bowl seasons, where he registered 27.5 sacks and 133 pressures, per Pro Football Focus, in 2024 and 2025 combined. This season, he's recorded 4.5 sacks in nine games and ranks tied for 19th among edges with 30 pressures.

Meanwhile, Cooper notched 18.5 sacks and 112 pressures in the two previous seasons to give the Broncos a quality No. 2 pass-rusher on the edge. In 2026, he's only participated in three games due to off-field issues in the offseason and has just 10 pressures and 0.5 sacks on 77 pass-rush snaps. So, Cooper's numbers are down as a consequence of his actions, but his track record on the field speaks for itself.

On the other side of the trenches, Miller has an impressive resume in pass protection but has been regressing this year. With 32 pressures on 379 opportunities, per PFF, his 8.4 percent pressure rate is the highest it's been since the 2018 first-round pick's rookie year (11.0 percent). But Glaze is trending in the opposite direction, going from 8.3 percent and 8.0 percent pressure rates in his first two seasons, respectively, to 4.0 percent (15 pressures on 379 opportunities) this year.

With the Raiders shifting from Kirk Cousins to Mendoza last week and seeing their playoff hopes rapidly decline over the last roughly two months, we've reached the point in the season where it's time to start looking at building for the future. Part of that is making sure the rookie quarterback can stay upright enough to make some plays and build confidence heading into next year. The other part is evaluating the team's needs before the offseason gets going.

It would be remiss to talk about the Broncos' pass rush and not mention Zach Allen, who currently leads all defensive tackles with 38 pressures and has 4.5 sacks. However, it feels like a foregone conclusion that Las Vegas will have to address its guard situation this spring. Meanwhile, offensive tackle is still up in the air, and this weekend will be a good litmus test for Miller and Glaze.

Is the veteran left tackle past his prime, where it's time to find a new blind side protector? Is the third-year pro's growth legitimate? How they fare against one of the NFL's best edge-rush duos over the last few years, Bonitto and Cooper, on Sunday will go a long way toward answering those questions.

So, for this week's game day discussion on The FEED, we'll be keeping tabs on how well Miller and Glaze can protect the edges to keep Mendoza clean in Denver. The game kicks off at 1:25 p.m. PST and will air on CBS (check your local listings). I look forward to talkin' some ball with y'all!

#2875. Game Preview #17: Wolves vs Grizzlies Thread

Remember when there was a "budding rivalry" between the Minnesota Timberwolves and Memphis Grizzlies? A drama filled first round playoff matchup between future faces of the league, Anthony Edwards and Ja Morant. Karl-Anthony Towns Sr. and Tee Morant became the cutest "Dads of the NBA" bromance. Dillon Brooks, Mike Conley, Kyle Anderson…just so many storylines.

Now these two teams couldn't appear to be any further away from that. The Grizzlies have zero players remaining on their 2022 roster. The Wolves roster just Anthony Edwards and Jaden McDaniels from their respective squad. The connection these two franchises share is more about who gets to join the Eastern Conference in a few years. In the meantime, contrary to what many thought, Memphis is standing right above Minnesota in the Western Conference standings!

Who: Minnesota Timberwolves (11-5) vs. Memphis Grizzlies (12-4)

When: Saturday, November 21 at 7:00 PM CT

If you haven't been paying attention, the Memphis Grizzlies have been one of the feel-good stories of the NBA so far. They completely, and rightfully, ditched their previous oft-injured core of Ja Morant, Jaren Jackson Jr., and Desmond Bane. Many pundits believed that Memphis would be among the worst teams in the league as they rebuild from the ground floor this year (not me, as I was wise beyond comprehension).

Instead, they were shot out of a cannon to begin the season, starting 6-0. No, I'm not talking about a 6-0 scoring run. I'm talking about a 6-0 team record. They follow Tommy Ice/T.I.'s (head coach Tuomas Iisalo) lead, employing a strategy where he spreads minutes across a 10 to 12-man rotation. 11 different players average over 15 minutes per game. They're led by early season MVP candidate Ty Jerome (missed previous four games) and 2026 third overall draft pick, Cameron Boozer.

They fell back to Earth by losing four of their next five games, but have already bounced back to win five straight (against admittedly relatively easy competition). When you zoom out further, the only "good teams" they've beaten are the Trail Blazers and Cavaliers. They've had the easiest strength of schedule by a considerable margin.

So are they for real? More real than most thought.

Equal opportunity to all. That might be on the Grizzlies' whiteboard before every game. 11 different players average over 5.0 field goal attempts per game, compared to just eight for Minnesota. Nine different players average 9.0+ points per game, compared to just seven for Minnesota.

You cannot just guard one player to stop Memphis.

For Minnesota, this means their league-best defense will need to be on a string. Everyone is dangerous and they have to be treated as such. As a team, the Grizzlies make the third most amount of triples per game (15.6) and drain them at a league-best rate (39.6%) – most of these being open attempts. They are third in assists per game. They will spread things around and make themselves unpredictable.

Don't be surprised if a Cam Spencer or Javon Small pop off for 16 points. It's very possible Jerami Grant has a MPE (medium player explosion) and suddenly scores 24 points out of nowhere. Where did Taylor Hendricks come from, and why is he so buff and athletic now? Memphis also has no shortage of quality role players like Cedric Coward and Jaylen Wells, who will muck the game up on both ends of the court.

What has happened to LaMelo Ball's offense? (Did you see what I did? I removed the "O" from his name above. Like he's playing so bad offensively he doesn't have "O" in his name. Do you get it?) After I spent some time developing (more) creative headlines so that I could gush about his defense, it's been quite jarring to see his drop-off in what's supposed to be his main talent.

This month of November has been a seesaw of production so far. If you compare the numbers in the first five games of the month when the Wolves went 5-0, to his most recent four game stretch, it's eye-popping.

If you've been watching the games, you'll see that Ball is clearly trying to shoot his way through this slump. In fact, he's taking three more shots per game during this most recent stretch. Is this something more than just a rough patch? LaMelo had some tape around his left hand which he tugged at during throughout Thursday's game. There hasn't been any reports of ailments, at least not to my knowledge.

We're not at Rudy Gobert levels of offensive concern yet, but I'm keeping me eye on this.

When's the last time the Wolves had a blowout win? Over a week? Yeah, that's too long. It's time for Minnesota to bump up that point differential and pad some stats. LaMelo Ball finally breaks out of his slump and busts up the Grizzlies for a cool 33 points thanks to eight three-pointers. Rudy Gobert gives Cam Boozer his "Welcome the NBA" moment by dunking all over his head off an alley-oop.

Who says you can't tailgate an IPO?Traders are preparing for this week's blockbuster Anthropic IPO in different ways, including buying options on one publicly-traded fund that invests in private assets.The Destiny Tech100 (DXYZ) is a publicly-traded, closed-end fund that invests in equity, equity-linked instruments, or shares of private funds and venture companies to gain exposure to private companies. #2871. Shane Steichen: Daniel Jones didn't ask to be released

The Colts abruptly released quarterback Daniel Jones on Friday. When coach Shane Steichen spoke to reporters on Friday, he was asked whether Jones asked to be cut.

“No, this was a decision that was just made for both, you know, the best interests of both parties,” Steichen said, via James Boyd of The Athletic.

Steichen also said that, as expected, Anthony Richardson will become the starter.

So instead of squatting on Jones and not letting him play, Jones gets a chance to land with a contender as a backup. Likely on the practice squad, which would give him the ability to jump to an active roster if/when a starter gets injured.

#2870. The Colts cut QB Daniel Jones. Should the Cardinals make a claim?

The Arizona Cardinals are one of the league's worst teams this year. Another bad team, the Indianapolis Colts just released their starting quarterback, Daniel Jones.

Should the Cardinals make a waiver wire claim? Before Jones was released, he had trade value. The waiver wire is situated where the worst team has the first dibs, then the second worst, and so on.

RELATED: CARDINALS DRAFT PICKS HAVE FAILED TO MAKE AN IMPACT

This means that the Cardinals are seventh in line. The Titans drafted Cam Ward last year, the Chargers have Justin Herbert, Miami spent big bucks on Malik Willis in last year's free agency period, and Geno Smith for the Jets is back into the fold.

The big question might be for Arizona is if they grabbed Jones off waivers, would he become the default starter or just another stallion in the QB stable? Would he play, or sit until Carson Beck becomes injured, frustrated, or incapable of assuming the starting role?

While with the Colts, Jones was under a two-year $88 million deal.

Titans: Ward is doing okay considering the offensive line is weak. The team has a capable backup in Mitch Trubisky.

Chargers: LA got their first win with Herbert still under center. His backup, Trey Lance, is on IR which means QB3 D.J. Uiagalelei is now Herbert's backup.

Dolphins: Willis is on IR with Kyle McCord starting and Brady Cook his backup.

Jets: Starter Geno Smith has just returned from IR. Rookie Cade Klubnik is his backup.

Eagles: We are all aware that starter Jalen Hurts is on IR and gone for the year. In his place is Joe Milton who isn't exactly tearing up the league. Tanner McKee and Cole Payton are backups.

With the Colts picking at #6, they are out. The Titans look good along with the Jets. But the remainder could use Jones. So could Arizona.

Jones was taken with the sixth overall selection in the 2019 NFL Draft by the New York Football Giants after a standout career at Duke. He was supposed to be the future after Eli Manning retired. In 2022, Jones made NFL history by becoming the first player ever with 300+ passing yards, 2+ touchdown passes, and 70+ rushing yards in a playoff game.

The next year, Jones inked a four-year $160 million extension, but had a knee injury in Week 9. Late in 2024, he was benched for undrafted Tommy DeVito who set the New York area on fire as an Italian-American quarterback who was winning games and his downhome attitude. In late November, the Giants shocked everyone by releasing Jones.

He spent one season with the Minnesota Vikings before hooking on with the Colts in 2025 who signed him to a one-year $14 million deal and competed with Anthony Richardson for the starting role. Jones tore his Achilles tendon late in the season and landed on IR.

This season, he was named the starter for Week 1. Jones started all 11 games for a listless offensive unit which has been selling off their key players. Their offensive line is absolutely porous. This year, Jones hasn't had any time to throw and has paltry production such as 144 (twice) yards, 108 (twice), 143, and 166 in games. He has thrown seven touchdowns against 13 interceptions.

For his eight-year career, Jones' stats: 94 games with 93 starts, 1,903 completions on 2,976 attempts for 19,657 yards, converted 979 first downs, 96 TDs, 68 picks, a 63.9% completion ratio, sacked 257 times, an 84.1 QB rating, and a 35-57-1 starting win-loss record. He is a very good runner with 478 carries for 2,441 yards with another 21 scores.

Thursday night, the Colts lost to the then-3-6-0 Houston Texans 26-3 in a totally lopsided contest. Indy had 38 rushing yards while Jones went 12 for 27 for 144 yards, sacked twice and had a single pick with zero TDs.

The Colts' offense is ranked #30 in the league. They have allowed the eighth most sacks (28) and thrown the most interceptions with 13. Despite losing one of the best offensive guards in the league by trading away LG Quenton Nelson to the Seattle Seahawks before the trade deadline, the Indianapolis offensive line is ranked #17.

By claiming Jones, it could mean the Cardinals would have a capable quarterback for their remaining games this season who would compete with Gardner Minshew and Carson Beck in training camp next year.

Then again, what would be the cost to bring him in? None of the four quarterbacks on the roster are costing the franchise hardly anything.

#2867. Nvidia has lost $2 trillion in value. Here's what options traders expect from earnings

Jensen Huang's forced exit from Nvidia shocked Wall Street and Washington, wiped $2 trillion off its market value, and raised serious questions about national security and regulation of the AI complex. Traders, for the moment, must content themselves with the comparatively mundane business of Tuesday's earnings, and options traders are expecting big moves.

With volatility in the stock near the highest since April 2025, options traders are now pricing a 7% reaction in the stock after its report, the biggest implied move going into an Nvidia report since February 2025, and well above the median move of 3.2% over the last eight quarters.

Implied volatility reached 68 Monday, compared to under 40 in early October, according to ThinkOrSwim data. Nvidia vol reached a multi-year high above 92 in the aftermath of President Trump's tariff Liberation day in April '25. In other words, the company that for much of the AI boom was the most reliable winner in the market, is now more volatile than all but 35 companies in the S&P 500.

"Longer term, I see significant downside risk unless Nvidia can attract an exceptional leader, someone like Tim Cook or AMD's Lisa Su," Nigam Arora, founder of the Arora report, said in a text ahead of the company's after-market report on Tuesday.

Since hitting all-time highs in October, Nvidia shares have fallen 35%, shedding $2 trillion in market capitalization along the way. Including Nvidia, there are only five companies in the S&P 500 worth more than $2 trillion. However, there is evidence that bulls have been holding out hope while bears snatch up profits: the ratio of open puts to call contracts has actually widened towards calls, according to Barchart data. The ratio is currently 0.87, down from a one-year high of 1.02 two weeks ago and the lowest since the first week of October.

The most popular contract by volume on Monday was the 155-strike put expiring at the bell Monday, but the most popular trade for later expiry was the 160-strike call expiring Thursday, and the second-most-common purchase was the 190-strike call expiring Friday, SpotGamma data show.

One catch is that most call contracts traded Monday looked like neutral to bearish positions opened by traders selling calls. Of the 5 million options traded on Monday, 3 million were calls, but more were likely opened by sellers than buyers. Of the $2.3 billion in options premium traded on Nvidia Monday, $1.2 billion was tied to puts, SpotGamma data show.

With bears taking profits in puts the past month, there's another potential snag on open interest. There are very few open put contracts at or below the $157 level at which the stock closed Monday. That suggests any bad surprise would find many traders with little downside protection.

By the same token, there's notable open interest in call contracts from the 175-strike and above, suggesting profit-taking on a bounce could limit Nvidia's recovery past levels it breached over the past week.

Either way, Tuesday's earnings could provide fireworks.

#2861. Antonelli fastest but penalty leaves Verstappen on pole

Mercedes' Kimi Antonelli was fastest in qualifying for the Las Vegas Grand Prix but a grid penalty will hand pole position to Red Bull's Max Verstappen.

Antonelli, running at the back of the pack of cars in qualifying and maximising his tow, beat Verstappen by 0.258 seconds.

But the new world champion has a five-place grid penalty for using more than permitted number of engine components and will start sixth.

The Italian's team-mate George Russell was just 0.073secs behind Verstappen and will start alongside the Dutchman on the front row.

McLaren's Lando Norris qualified fourth, and will start third, ahead of Red Bull's Isack Hadjar.

Both Ferraris were eliminated in the second session and qualified 11th and 12th with Charles Leclerc ahead of Lewis Hamilton.

Each will be promoted a place by a 10-place penalty for McLaren's Oscar Piastri, who qualified seventh.

Racing Bulls' rookie Arvid Lindblad, sixth fastest, is the last driver to be promoted as a result of Antonelli's penalty.

Racing Bulls' Liam Lawson will start seventh with Alpine's Pierre Gasly eighth and Franco Colapinto ninth.

Drivers limited to 27 laps for tyres in Qatar

Antonelli's final run did not start auspiciously as he and Russell became tangled up with the Alpines and Racing Bulls as they sought the best track position to optimise the slipstream effect.

Antonelli ended up the last driver to run and made it to the line with only one second to spare before the chequered flag, one second behind Lindblad.

Normally, being so close to the car in front would be a compromise but in Las Vegas it magnified the tow effect and Antonelli leapt to the front. "That was all the tow," he said over the radio after being told he was on pole.

"Starting the lap, I was only one second behind the car in front and I thought I was screwed," he said. "But the tow is so powerful here."

Verstappen, who has won two of the three Las Vegas Grands Prix, said: "I have been struggling with some weird issues that were also in the car in Brazil and just trying to get my head around how to deal with that.

"In qualifying we managed to make it work. Had a solid qualifying. My first lap in qualifying was pretty much as good as it could be with the tow, so to be on the front row is good for us.

"It was solid in the corners, and you have a bit of dirty air, but it is insane how much you gain on the straights."

Russell had looked in the running for the pole, but was happy with his session despite missing out.

"Overall it's been a very solid session and a solid day," he said.

"I wanted to be on pole but I am probably more pleased with the performance than in Mexico when I was on the front row.

"Kimi did a great job and he probably thought it wasn't going to work out so well being so close but with the tow it worked out."

#2859. AMD dives on short seller report of alleged China exposure

Shares of chipmaker Advanced Micro Devices close down 7.72% to $489.19 on Thursday after investment firm Hunterbrook Media issued a report alleging that the company "has its own China problem."

Hunterbrook disclosed that it had taken a short position in AMD.

The report claims that "after the U.S. blacklisted Chinese server maker IEIT Systems, the company kept getting newer AMD firmware, which enables AMD chips to run in servers."

CNBC did not independently verify this information.

Hunterbrook also said that new export rules in Washington could "tighten restrictions on AMD's foreign business," alleging that AMD "bills over 20% of its revenue to customers in China and Hong Kong," and "depends on a Chinese partner to finish most of its chips."

The allegations come amid investigations of rival chipmaker Nvidia over its sales to companies in Asia with ties to China. AMD has been viewed as a beneficiary of the uncertainty surrounding Nvidia.

"The claims misrepresent AMD's business practices and relationships," an AMD spokesperson said in a statement. "AMD complies with applicable US export control laws and regulations and maintains rigorous processes to ensure compliance across our global operations. We remain focused on serving our customers and upholding the highest standards of integrity."

Lisa Su, CEO of AMD, told investors on the company's recent earnings call that China is an "important" market and that the semiconductor giant wants to be fully compliant with U.S. export controls.

AMD continued to slide after hours, trading down 0.45% to $487 shortly after 4 p.m.

#2857. Moritz Wagner’s first-ever chance to face his brother Franz is nothing short of ‘awkward’

Brooklyn's Moritz Wagner has been in the NBA for nine years and alive for 20 more, and he had never, ever played against his brother, Franz, in a game that counted.

That is, until Friday night. The Wagner siblings faced off when the Nets hosted Franz's Orlando Magic, and they squared off against each other for the first time.

"It's awkward, I'm not gonna lie. I don't really know how to compartmentalize it, really deal with it, and try to focus on the game plan and see what happens emotionally," Moritz said beforehand. "It's a dilemma because you want him to do well. At the same time, you game plan for him, you prepare to limit him. So, it's an awkward feeling. I've never been in this position.

Brooklyn Nets center Moritz Wagner (13) warms up before an NBA basketball game against the Orlando Magic at Barclays Center in Brooklyn, NY, Nov. 20, 2026. Robert Sabo for NY Post

"It's weird. It's definitely a new role I've got to grow into. But it's also fun, and we talked about that [Thursday]. It's an absolute privilege, and for our family it's really cool because we've established each other as independent NBA players on two different sides and get to play against each other in this league and have 20 people here watching us. So it's very special, and it's gonna be an emotional night for our family."

Moritz played for Michigan and was a 2018 first-round pick by Orlando, where he'd spent his entire career until signing with the Nets this summer.

Franz, four years his junior, had followed in his footsteps and starred for the Wolverines before being picked eighth overall by the Magic in 2021.

Orlando Magic's Franz Wagner handles the ball during an NBA Cup game against the Brooklyn Nets at Barclays Center in Brooklyn, N.Y., Friday, Nov. 20, 2026. NBAE via Getty Images

"My whole NBA career I played with him. The first time in my life playing against him," Franz said. "But as weird as it's going to be, it's a blessing to share the court with your family.Not a lot of people get to do that, so that's how I look at it."

The younger Wagner is breaking out this season with Paolo Banchero injured, entering the meeting averaging 24.4 points, 8.5 assists and 6.9 rebounds.

And it was 30.7 points, 8.5 assists and 7.7 boards over his prior six games.

"We've got a new staff, a new system and a lot of clarity on roles and what's expected of everyone. That helps everyone with their rhythm," Franz said.

"He's playing incredible," Moritz said. "New coach, new system, found a way to be a playmaker and scorer at the same time, dominating the pace, the paint. Obviously with an enormous usage rate as well, which you've got to give him credit for the physicality he plays with. One thing that's really underrated about Franz is his defensive ability. There aren't many players that dominate on both ends of the floor like he does. … It's a joy to watch him as a big brother, regardless of my situation."

Young fan Dylan wears a custom jersey featuring Orlando Magic forward Franz Wagner and Brooklyn Nets center Moritz Wagner in Brooklyn, N.Y., Nov. 20, 2026. Robert Sabo for NY Post

That situation is serving as backup center and veteran voice on a struggling Nets squad.

Many have suggested that Brooklyn could target Franz if Orlando breaks him and Banchero up. But for the moment, the Nets have to figure out their own woes internally, and Franz was the enemy Friday.

"I grew up with him. When he's mad, when I see that look in his eyes, it takes me back to when we were 9 and 5 and playing against each other. I don't think I know a human better than him. So on an emotional level, just knowing what he goes through with every step, it's weird to be the opponent," Moritz said.

"Obviously, [he's] a big part of my development as a person and as a player," Franz said. "So it should be fun out there."

Orlando Magic forward Franz Wagner (22) warms up before a game against the Brooklyn Nets on Friday, Nov. 20, 2026, at Barclays Center in Brooklyn, N.Y. Robert Sabo for NY Post

The Wagner family was on hand to watch Friday. They even had specialized jerseys made with both of the brothers' names and team colors, and their mother Beate is especially nervous.

"We made these corny split jerseys," Moritz said. "I know my mom's going to go through it [Friday]. I know that. … That's going to be really difficult for her. Definitely a new spot for her. … But she's going to figure that out, and it's also a really good problem to have, obviously.."

#2854. Anthropic was supposed to save the AI trade. What went wrong?

Anthropic's historic IPO — the largest in U.S. history — delivered a big win for the company and its investors. But it failed at another task: reviving animal spirits in the AI trade.

The Nasdaq Composite closed down 2.25% amid a broader market pullback with the S&P 500 declining 2% to 7,175.

Investors had been looking forward to the Anthropic IPO as a "clearing event" — reviving the tech sector after months of choppy trading, the Nvidia espionage scandal, and growing concerns that AI chip sales to Asia are likely to slump as Washington cracks down on potential violations of export controls.

Some of those worries resurfaced Thursday as a short seller, Hunterbrook Media, alleged in a report that firmware sold by chipmaker AMD had turned up in U.S.-blacklisted companies. AMD stock closed down more than 7%. The chipmaker told CNBC it complies with U.S. law.

Adding to the worries, the House Foreign Affairs Committee released documents showing that a subagency of the Commerce Department approved the sale of more than 879,000 AI chips to Chinese companies. That also raised the prospect of an Asia sales slowdown under stricter export controls.

Many investors and analysts continue to express confidence in the AI trade, noting that Anthropic's successful market debut — the stock closed up 17% from its IPO price and raised more than $81 billion — bodes well for capital spending across tech.

But investors acknowledged that this is a high-tension moment and said it's time to be more strategic about the trade.

Dan Niles, founder of Niles Investment Management, described himself as a "circumspect bull," making the comparison to market conditions in 2022 when the big indices tanked in the back half of the year.

"You could have still made money that year. You just needed to have the right combination of longs and shorts," he said.

Part of the challenge, Niles said, has been a price war between the two cutting-edge frontier labs, Anthropic and OpenAI. That has posed a threat to the overall revenue and profit potential of AI and the capex going into it. Fierce competition on price is producing collective losses between the two top companies, he notes.

"The combined revenue growth for OpenAI plus Anthropic went from growing 75% sequentially in Q2 to 48% in Q3," he told CNBC. "The combined losses went from $7.9 billion to about $8.4 billion from Q2 to Q3."

While uncertainty is high now, some resolution may be on the horizon if a greater degree of commercial and regulatory clarity emerges over the next few months.

Trading experts also told CNBC that the tech sector's troubles Thursday were a more serious matter than Anthropic simply siphoning capital from other parts of the market.

"There's a reset of AI in general," said JJ Kinahan, senior vice president at Cboe Global Markets.

Investors for years have profited in tech by buying the dip, he said, but "there's been an attitude change that [you] can't buy things blindly and they'll all go up." Nvidia's ongoing declines have shaken confidence in the buy-the-dip strategy, he said.

Anthropic's next few trading days will be a key gauge of the market's strength. Friday will be a "wild card," Kinahan said. It could be a "final flush out" for the market, or a bounce that leaves the market in "no man's land" while volatility remains elevated.

A better gauge may be Monday, when options on Anthropic start trading, providing insight into whether retail investors will back the company in force — providing more of a vote of confidence in the AI trade.

"If people think we're going up [on Anthropic] they'll buy stock, but retail particularly will just buy calls," he said. "There's a bigger test coming Monday."

For now, investors continue to sell AI-related stocks.

Neoclouds, the generally highly indebted AI-dedicated computing providers at the outer edges of the AI trade, were down substantially Thursday, with CoreWeave closing down more than 6%.

Hyperscalers such as Alphabet and Amazon.com, viewed as relatively defensive AI bets, also finished lower.

One bright spot: Cybersecurity stocks continued to rally, with Palo Alto Networks and CrowdStrike both closing up. AI is finding security holes across software infrastructure, creating a scramble to patch the vulnerabilities before hackers can exploit them.

— CNBC's Daren Fonda contributed reporting.

#2853. JJ Redick Gives Two Reasons For Replacing Jake LaRavia With Ziaire Williams On Lakers’ Starting Lineup

The Los Angeles Lakers play the Sacramento Kings tonight at the Crypto.Com Arena. Head coach JJ Redick once again replaced Jake LaRavia with Ziaire Williams in the Lakers' starting lineup.

Ahead of tonight's game, JJ Redick spoke to the media and explained the decision that initially puzzled many Lakers fans when they made it in their 97-109 loss to the Jazz, which ended their nine-game winning streak.

"I felt like, with the group they were going to start, again, we talked to Ziaire and Jake when we were in Milwaukee last week about both of those guys having some flexibility."

"I think Ziaire was in a really good rhythm, and he's had some good games since he missed some time. We were trying to get him back into a rhythm. He hasn't really had one over the last three games or so. So, some of it was about that, and some of it was about the matchup."

"Those guys are both easy, low-maintenance winners. They're not going to be affected by that. I think the message from day one of training camp has always been that every game is going to be a little different. It's going to present itself differently, and the challenges are going to be different. You have to play the game in front of you, and the guys are bought into that," Redick explained.

Before tonight, Jake LaRavia averaged 5.8 points, 4.1 rebounds, and 2.2 assists while shooting 47.1% from the field and 46.7% from beyond the arc so far this season.

Ziaire Williams averaged 9.5 points, 2.4 rebounds, and 1.8 assists while shooting 56.4% from the field and 44.7% from beyond the arc.

It seemed like Jake LaRavia had cemented his role in the Lakers' starting lineup in 15 games (11 starts). But Ziaire Williams clearly stepped up and made a promising case for a starter role (six starts in 13 games played).

The Lakers began the season with three prominently fixed starters whenever they are healthy: Luka Doncic, Austin Reaves, and Walker Kessler. As a result, the wings were always a position to contend for and earn as the season progressed.

Given that both power forwards are willing to do whatever it takes to win, this is a good problem for the Lakers: everyone plays so well that they don't know who to start or who can come off the bench and make an impact.

They seem likely to use this position rotationally throughout the season, based on the matchups they expect. It will be interesting to see who the Lakers' coaching staff decides to start as the playoffs approach later in the season.

But down the stretch, it remains to be seen who earns the regular starting power-forward role, as role clarity helps players mold their tendencies accordingly.
