#2799. James Harden erupts for 51 to pass Kobe Bryant on 50-point games list

James Harden turned back the clock Friday, Nov. 20, reminding NBA opponents and fans why he is one of the best scorers in league history.

The 37-year-old scored 51 points Friday to lead the Cavaliers to a 128-119 win over the Indiana Pacers in an NBA Cup East Group B game at Rocket Arena in Cleveland.

Harden's 51 points were the most he's scored in a game this year and came two days shy of the one-year anniversary of his last 50-point game, when, as a member of the Los Angeles Clippers, he shelled out 55 in a win against the Charlotte Hornets on Nov. 22, 2025.

But his scoring output Friday night represented a more meaningful milestone: Harden notched his 26th 50-point game of his career, passing Kobe Bryant for third all time in NBA history. Michael Jordan ranks second with 31 such games, while the all-time leader, Wilt Chamberlain, seems likely to retain that record for a very long time having crossed the 50-point threshold an astounding 118 times.

Only three other players have scored at least 50 points this season, and all three are MVP candidates. Los Angeles Lakers star Luka Dončić has passed the 50-point mark twice, tallying 53 against the Portland Trail Blazers on Nov. 5 and 52 against the Utah Jazz on Oct. 25. San Antonio Spurs star Victor Wembanyama dropped 52 against the Dallas Mavericks on Nov. 4, while Detroit Pistons guard Cade Cunningham scored 52 in a double overtime win against the Washington Wizards on Nov. 12.

Harden ranks ninth in NBA history and 10th in NBA-ABA history for most career points with 29,633 in 1,237 games played. Next on the career list is Hall of Famer Julius Erving with 30,026 career points in the NBA and ABA. Philadelphia 76ers star LeBron James holds the record for most points in league history with 43,628 (through Nov. 20).

#2798. At 7-2, Have the Bears Met or Exceeded Your Preseason Expectations?

The Chicago Bears have completed what Ben Johnson would refer to as "2 quarters" of the 2026 NFL season. Despite early adversity, they've managed to come away with only two losses so far – a mark that would be more impressive if they weren't still sitting in 3rd place in their division. Still, the halfway point of the season presents us with a great opportunity to look back at the record predictions of a few of our WCG writers and evaluate just how close we each were to predicting this level of performance from the Bears. It also gives us a chance to look at our predictions for the remaining games and how confident we may now be feeling in those predictions.

First, here's the Bears' remaining schedule:

Week 11 – Nov. 22 vs. New Orleans Saints, noon on FOXWeek 12 – Thanksgiving Day at Detroit Lions, noon on CBSWeek 13 – Dec. 6 vs. Jacksonville Jaguars, noon on FOXWeek 14 – Dec. 13 at Miami Dolphins, noon on CBSWeek 15 – Dec. 19, Saturday at Buffalo Bills, 7:20 p.m. on CBSWeek 16 – Christmas Day, Friday, Dec. 25 – vs. Green Bay Packers, noon on NetflixWeek 17 – Jan. 3 vs. Detroit Lions, 3:25 p.m. on FOXWeek 18 – Jan. 10 at Minnesota Vikings, TBD

Donald Gooch: I had the Bears at 12-5 on the season, so if you project out their record over the next eight games based on the first nine, the expected record is between 11-6 and 12-5, so the Bears are on pace for what I expected before the season. TJ Starman: My original prediction was for the Bears to end up 11-6. That said, I still expect 3 more losses in the next 8 games, which would put my adjusted prediction at 12-5.

Josh Sunderbruch: I had the Bears at 10-6-1 and just barely making the playoffs in May, and I had guessed 6-3 at the bye, so they've beaten my expectations by one win so far. I can see them going 4-4 down the stretch, for 11-6, but I'd love for it to be 5-3 (for 12-5). #2793. Nicolas Cage on his John Madden misfire: "I'm not Frank Caliendo"

This whole thing is antithetical to my identity.

That’s the one line that resonates most strongly in the new Madden movie. Because it’s exactly what John Madden would say if he saw it.

It’s bad. It’s very bad. Anyone who says it’s good has no taste and/or an agenda that involves the strategic application of lips to asses.

The biggest problem is Nicolas Cage as John Madden. Anyone who vividly remembers the unique charisma of John Madden knows instantly that nothing about Cage’s portrayal reflects the true nature, spirit, and demeanor of one of the most important and impactful figures in the entire history of the sport of football.

Cage, who seems to realize that his portrayal missed not only the bull’s-eye but also the entire wall on which the dart board was mounted, has defended the outcome by peering down his nose at the sports world.

“He was not on my radar,” Cage said of Madden, via Kirsten Chuba of the Hollywood Reporter. “I was growing up watching black-and-white Orson Welles movies and Fellini movies and Bruce Lee. I wasn’t interested in team sports whatsoever.”

“I’m someone that likes to stay out of his comfort zone and take a risk, I said yes, because that would be a transformation,” Cage explained regarding the decision to attempt to play the towering, iconic figure. “It would be something where I might learn a few things, taking on this character who is really nothing like me.”

The audience has learned that Cage’s interpretation is nothing like Madden.

“That’s what acting’s about,” Cage said. “I’m not an impressionist, I’m not Frank Caliendo. I lean more towards being a surrealist, meaning I like to get a concept of a personality and then dig deep and see what I can locate emotionally with my imagination and my psyche and filter it through the cadence and the appearance, within reason, of John Madden. I’m trying to give you an evocation, more or less, or an interpretation.”

The problem was it wasn’t even “within reason.” It was without reason. And the entire film was beyond unreasonable.

And I like Nicolas Cage. He was brilliant in Moonstruck. He won an Oscar for Leaving Las Vegas. And The Family Man remains a highly underrated holiday film.

Still, to quote another holiday film, “They can’t all be winners.” Cage’s role in Madden as John Madden is not.

I only met John Madden once. I watched him on TV for decades. I’ve heard plenty of stories about him from people who worked with him at NBC. And I’m confident that he would be livid about this film.

“That’s not me,” he’d say. “Not even close.”

Or, perhaps, “This whole thing is antithetical to my identity.”

#2792. Amari Jones and Gabriela Fundora Give Fans the Goods Friday

Undefeated middleweight Amari Jones says he brought out the dog Friday night in the Golden Boy Boxing main event Friday in Indio, California.

Undisputed women's flyweight champion Gabriela Fundora scored two knockdowns but settled for a dominating decision over challenger Jasmina Zapotoczna of England.

Coachella Valley-based boxers sent their fans home happy with satisfying knockout wins.

DeAundre Pettus had avoided being stopped as a professional boxer, but his luck ran out when "The Reaper" Amari Jones came for him on Friday night at the Fantasy Springs Resort and Casino.

Jones of Oakland (18-0, 16 KOs) got it done in eight rounds against a game Pettus of South Carolina (14-5, 7 KOs).

Jones is currently the IBF mandatory challenger to champion Aaron McKenna of Ireland. McKenna's mandatory isn't due anytime soon, so Jones didn't want to sit around. He said he was glad to face an opponent who could give him some good rounds in Pettus.

"I've been waiting for a guy like that to give me some rounds. He was athletic, a little crafty, a little less athletic than I thought. He put together fast, twitchy movements and that's always tricky no matter how experienced a guy is. It just didn't work," said Jones.

Jones was patient, knowing his power punches were more relentless and skilled than anything Pettus had seen before. He had earned his reputation as a tough fighter, and he made Jones earn the win

The end came from a check left hook a minute into the eighth round. Pettus went down hard. Referee Thomas Taylor gave him a hard look, and decided the night was over. Pettus protested but it was the right decision.

A vicious check left hook was the end for DeAundre Pettus. Photo: Golden Boy Boxing

"I showed a little bit more dog. You all know I come forward and get a guy a little more technical. Today I dug a little more deeper and dug it out more than I normally do," said Jones.

Jones revealed that he had worked as a sparring partner for Canelo Álvarez in preparation for his fight with Christian Mbilli, and picked up a few moves, including the technique Álvarez uses to batter his opponents to the arms. "I was definitely going to use it in my next fight."

Jones promised The Reaper is here to stay, and sent a message out to the world champion."Aaron McKenna, where you at, Leprechaun Man? I'm coming for the pot of gold. You've got it, and I'm going to get it."

Gabriela Fundora came up just short of stopping Jasmina Zapotoczna on Friday, but there was more than enough action to send fans home happy. Photo: Golden Boy Boxing

It was the co-main event, but everyone in Indio considered their hometown champion Gabriela "Sweet Poison" Fundora (19-0, 10 KOs) the star of the show. The undisputed WBA, WBC, WBO and IBF flyweight world champion and The Ring magazine champion didn't disappoint them.

While Fundora couldn't deliver a knockout, she scored two knockdowns and gave fans ten rounds of action against former European flyweight champion Jasmina Zapotoczna of England (10-2). Scores were 100-88, 99-91, and 98-92.

Fundora kept up a furious pace from start to finish. If she had three-minute rounds to work with, she would have followed up her knockdowns with enough time to make them stick. But she wasn't too disappointed.

"She's a mover. She had a game plan, and her first time was not too shoddy … I was just trying to get the knockout in. But my father said, 'Hey, you can't knock everybody out.' So, it's fine," said Fundora.

Fundora kept Zapotoczna in front of her, sizing her up. Fundora suffered an accidental headbutt in the first few seconds of the fight. It likely made the southpaw fighter more cautious about making things worse.

But it wasn't long before the uppercuts, body shots, and hooks landed. The fans cheered every thudding punch. Fundora said she hit the openings, but with Zapotoczna on the move, it was a challenge. "She was running around the ring, so she was trying to survive. That's cool, but hey, this is boxing."

Gabriela Fundora punishes her opponents and fans love her for it. Photo: Golden Boy Boxing

Fundora scored knockdowns in both the seventh and eighth rounds on combinations of body shots and hooks. It was surprising that Zapotczna's corner allowed her to come out for the ninth round, but she finished the fight on her feet.

"The bell won this time. But it was an exciting fight. I got to showcase a couple different things, and it was just Sweet Poison time."

Asked about her future, Fundora didn't name anyone. Instead, she made the case that she is the fighter fans come to see and the opponent hardly matters.

"Whoever steps into the ring, they're coming to see Fundora, just like Michael Jackson, Prince, Oscar De La Hoya. They want to see both people. So. I want to be one of those names that they're just seeing Fundora. Doesn't matter who's in the ring." It's true in the Coachella Valley without a doubt.

Joel Iriarte had his opponent Darvin Galeano hurt and using the ropes to hold him up. He didn't last much longer. Photo: Golden Boy Boxing

Joel Iriarte of Bakersfield, California (12-0, 11 KOs) was a man on a mission against Darvin Galeano of Panama (15-5, 9 KOs). He overwhelmed Galeano with his offense. By the second round, Galeano was losing the will to continue, his legs failing under him.

As Iriarte pinned him against the ropes, Galeano sat on them, letting them hold him up. The sharp-eyed referee, Michael Margardo, properly called it a knockdown, and after giving the count, Galeano decided his night was over at 2:31 of Round 2.

"The first jab, literally, we see him broken in his spirit," said Iriarte. He said he'd like to return to the ring quickly on January 23 as part of the Arnold Barboza Jr. vs Oscar Duarte undercard in Anaheim.

Cayden Griffiths says his bread and butter is his body work, and he's right. Photo: Golden Boy Boxing

Cayden Griffiths of Indio (9-0, 8 KOs) added a knockout win to his resume at super lightweight against Jean Rivera Pacheco of Puerto Rico (10-3, 1 KO). Rivera decided early to stay on the move after taking a few shots. Griffiths had to follow Rivera and later said he was frustrated by it. He got to Rivera in round six with a perfect left hook to the liver, and the fight was over at 1:59 of round six.

"I know my body shots are my bread and butter. I knew once I landed clean, he wasn't going to be able to take it," said Griffiths, but he was hard on himself for not being able to pin Rivera down more quickly, and said he's got some things to work on in the gym. "The end result is all that really matters, and we got him out of there." Griffiths is only 20 years old, so he's got time to work on his craft.

Ruslan Abdullaev is ready for tougher opposition. Photo: Golden Boy Boxing

Undefeated Uzbek Olympian Ruslan Abdullaev (6-0, 4 KOs) was never threatened by Juan Meza Angulo of Mexico (9-4, 6 KOs), but credit Mesa for hanging in tough while taking a hiding, peppered with snapping jabs, followed by relentless body punching and hooks.

Abdullaev's punishment finally took its toll as referee Ray Corona waived off the fight after Meza went down from a 1-2 combination of hooks at 2:40 of round six. Abdullaev picks up the WBA Continental Gold super lightweight belt.

Middleweight Amir "The Cashman" Anderson of Syracuse, New York (9-0, 7 KOs) drilled Daniel Buciuc of Romania (16-4, 6 KOs) for two hard rounds, scoring a knockdown at the end of two. We could say Buciuc was saved by the bell, but not really, as Anderson came straight at him and dropped him again with a vicious left hook to the body for a third-round knockout at 23 seconds of the round.

Lightweight Leonardo "Bazooka" Sanchez of Cathedral City (12-0, 10 KOs) had an impressive number of local supporters in the seats. They only got to see him for a round, but he packed plenty of action into those few minutes and lived up to his moniker. He went right at Victor "El Tornado" Morales of Vancouver, BC (20-2-1, 10 KOs), first with body shots for his first knockdown, right and left hooks for the second, ending the fight at 2:48 of the round.

Making his Golden Boy debut, Sebastian "Lil Beast" Mitry of Phoenix (8-0, 6 KOs) put in six solid rounds against Anthony Alvarado of Salem, Oregon (9-2, 2 KOs) in his middleweight bout. Scores were 60-54. 60-54, and 59-55. Mitry is based at the Diaz Brothers gym in Indio where he's been impressing the trainers as a sparring partner. He fought his first seven fights in Mexico.

Undefeated super lightweight Javier Meza of Amarillo, Texas (7-0, 4 KOs) scored his first knockdown over Jonathan Figueroa of Hartford, Connecticut (8-3, 7 KOs) in the third round, then made it stick with a body-shot knockout in the fourth.

Opening the night, welterweight David Lopez of Las Vegas (10-0, 7 KOs) made it quick with a first-round knockout over Alan Crisosto of Guadalajara (12-8, 8 KOs).

#2790. Gritty road trip was enough to give Knicks their mojo back

The Knicks left for their four-game road trip a bit unsettled, with questions circling them due to a rocky start to the season. After going 3-1 on the trip and beating potential playoff teams in the Raptors (twice) and Timberwolves, they return as a side rounding back into form and a better team than when they left.

"I feel like we're getting into a good rhythm offensively, defensively," Josh Hart said after the team's 102-99 win over the Timberwolves on Thursday night in Minneapolis. "I think you'll see some of the defensive rotations were on point. Obviously, we can be better. But we're continuing to learn, gain experience. Every year is different, every team is different. So I think we're doing that, and I think we're getting better.

"Whenever you win in the league, you continue to build confidence."

It is notable that they won in different ways: through free-flowing offensive showings; through grittier defensive efforts; and on Thursday, a combination of both. None of the three wins were blowouts — each game was on the line in crunch time.

New York Knicks center Karl-Anthony Towns looks on after an NBA basketball game against the Minnesota Timberwolves in Minneapolis, Friday, Nov. 20, 2026. AP Photo/Ellen Schmidt

"It shows that we're able to win in different ways," Hart said. "And that's what you have to do in the league. Some days, you're gonna shoot the cover off the ball and you can win by 10, 15, 20. Other games, it might be a possession game and offensively it's not really clicking, not knocking down shots, but you're getting stops defensively and you're able to pull out stops like that. I think it shows that this team can win games in different fashions. And that's the most important thing in the league."

A road trip like this can be beneficial, particularly early in the year as a new group gels. Despite all the continuity the Knicks have, they have been adamant that chemistry has to be reestablished.

They begin a five-game homestand Saturday, in which they'll host the surprisingly strong Trail Blazers, get a rematch with the Pacers (who could have back injured Tyrese Haliburton), play the Cavaliers for the third time already this season, see Giannis Antetokounmpo and the Heat for the first time and square off with the Hawks, who have started this season well after the Knicks eliminated them from the first round of the playoffs.

"For us to have a great road trip like this and win in so many different ways, it [says] a lot about our team," Karl-Anthony Towns said. "It's great when you can find a way to win. At the end of the day, it's hard to win in the NBA. I've learned that. It's hard to win. And you just have to find a way to win every single night. For us on this road trip to continue to find ways to win, it speaks to this team, and it's the most important thing when you play."

The Knicks' injury-free run came to an end.

New York Knicks forward OG Anunoby dunks during the first half of an NBA basketball game against the Minnesota Timberwolves, Thursday, Nov. 19, 2026, in Minneapolis. AP Photo/Ellen Schmidt

Jordan Clarkson (right plantar fasciitis) is out for Saturday's game against the Trail Blazers at Madison Square Garden. OG Anunoby (left calf tightness) is listed as questionable.

They might also get a break as Blazers star Deni Avdija is listed as questionable with a left foot contusion. He has missed the Blazers' last two games. Ja Morant remains out with a hamstring injury.

#2789. Braelon Allen making case for more touches in Jets’ crowded backfield

Braelon Allen averaged less than six carries per game as a rookie. Last season, the Jets' backup running back was limited to only four games due to a knee injury.

But the 134th player — and 11th running back — selected in the 2024 draft was ready when opportunity arrived.

While Breece Hall was sidelined by a quad injury, Allen ran for 504 yards (5.4 yards per carry) in his first five career starts, opening this month with a 147-yard effort that made him the first Jets running back in 11 years (Chris Ivory) to rush for 100 yards in consecutive games.

Entering Week 11, the 22-year-old is one of only eight running backs in the NFL averaging at least five yards per carry.

New York Jets running back Braelon Allen (0) looks on before a game against the Buffalo Bills in East Rutherford, New Jersey, Sunday, Nov. 15, 2026. IMAGN IMAGES via Reuters Connect

"I know what I can be in this league," Allen told The Post on Friday. "You needed the platform to show what you're capable of. I always knew when that time came I was gonna take advantage.

"Fortunately, but unfortunately, I came to a place where they had a really good running back already."

Once again, touches have become hard to find for one of the Jets' most dynamic players.

In returning to a backup role over the past two games, Allen has rushed for 73 yards on 16 carries (three receptions, 27 yards), while Hall had 26 rushes for 102 rushing yards (seven receptions, 93 yards). This season, Hall has 265 rushing yards (3.4 yards per carry) and one touchdown in five games, while Allen has 638 rushing yards and three touchdowns in 10 games.

Still, head coach Aaron Glenn reiterated Friday that Hall — who signed a three-year, $43.5 million deal in the offseason — remains the team's top option in the backfield.

New York Jets running back Breece Hall (20) runs past Cleveland Browns linebacker Carson Schwesinger (49) in East Rutherford, N.J., Nov. 9, 2025. USA TODAY Network via Reuters Connect

"Obviously, Breece and Garrett [Wilson] are guys that we really try to target for the most part, and then we make sure the rest of the guys get their touches when they come," Glenn said. "I think, obviously, Breece will end up getting a tick more [touches] because, again, he's our starting running back, but we want to make sure that Braelon gets his touches."

There has been a greater effort made to include the backup in the past two weeks than there was in the Jets' first three games, when Hall received 51 carries and Allen got 19.

But there is no clear solution to finding the right balance.

"We should talk about it because that's what people want to know," offensive coordinator Frank Reich said this week. "I think that's a great problem to have. For me, it's terrible because I can't win. I know it's never gonna be enough. I'm not worried about that to the outside. I'm worried about that when I walk in the room with the guys.

"I'm not worried about the fantasy stuff and all that stuff. I'm worried about those guys in the locker room and I want them to know — and I know they do — how much I believe in each one of them and how much I want to give each one of them the ball, but there's only so many touches in a game.

"They have a lot of respect for each other. I wish we could get it to them both more, but that's just the way it's gonna be."

#2785. Bud Dupree requests release from Chargers, who haven't granted it

As the season continues to unfold — and as the trade deadline fades into the rear-view mirror — unhappy players have only one option: To request a release.

Via Jeremy Fowler of ESPN, Chargers linebacker Bud Dupree has done that. The Chargers, however, have not granted the request.

Dupree, per Fowler, also requested his release before the season.

It’s unknown whether the Chargers tried to trade him. His playing time has systematically dropped this year. He had no defensive snaps in two straight games before he was a healthy scratch in the Week 10 Monday night game against the Ravens.

That move came three days after Dupree had some pointed comments for the front office.

“Don’t ask hard questions to us; ask hard questions to them,” Dupree said. “Ask the hard questions. Everybody pull they cameras out and go upstairs and ask questions to them. Everybody. Walk upstairs and ask them.”

Dupree is under contract, so there’s nothing he can do to force his way out. Still, the situation underscores the one-way nature of these deals.

The team can cut the player whenever it wants. The player can’t get out unless the team is willing to let him go.

#2784. Player grades: SGA's 40 points lead Thunder to 126-120 win over Pelicans

OKLAHOMA CITY — Racing down the floor, Shai Gilgeous-Alexander had his eyes set on the rim. He trucked through Jeremiah Fears to bank in the and-one layup. The two-time MVP walked the baseline and stared at the crowd before he finished the dagger three-point play with 34 seconds left.

The Oklahoma City Thunder survived a thriller in a 126-120 win over the New Orleans Pelicans. This was part of the 2026 NBA Cup West Group B.

In a sport that features plenty of runs, this was one even more roller-coast-esque than usual. The Thunder completely blitzed the Pelicans to get started with a quick 11-0 lead a little over two minutes in. And then New Orleans immediately responded with a 17-4 run. Saddiq Bey buried an outside jumper to cap it off.

The Thunder were in a 36-33 deficit after the first quarter. Once again, it was another NBA opponent that completely cooked OKC from the perimeter. This time, it was by one of the worst outside-shooting teams in the league. The second quarter was much the same story.

As the Thunder fell behind by double-digit points, they couldn't muster up much offense. Pair that with the Pelicans getting whatever they wanted, it was a recipe for bad vibes. Zion Williamson bully-balled his way to the cup. He looked like his vintage self. Jeremiah Fears' speed helped him dissect OKC's defense. The latter only had 23 points in the second frame. It was in a 64-56 deficit at halftime.

Yeesh. The Thunder looked lifeless for most of the first half. For as demoralizing as things have gotten with them, multiply that by 10 for the Pelicans. To finish a putrid first half, Gilgeous-Alexander had a scary-looking fall on a drive to the basket. His shoulder collided with the floor.

To start the second half, the Thunder fell into an even deeper hole. Herbert Jones' back-to-back corner 3-pointers were soul-crushers. That put OKC in a 91-76 deficit. The possibility of dropping another gimme stared them straight in the face once again. They salvaged things with a 14-2 run to end the third frame.

At that point, the Thunder scored 36 points in the third quarter. The offense finally started to play like an upper-half team. Jared McCain and Bennett Stirtz caught on fire. Aday Mara was a godsend as he played like an old-school seven-footer. They were in a 98-92 deficit with a dozen minutes to go. Another clutch-time scenario was on the verge of materializing.

Eventually, McCain erupted the OKC crowd with a game-tying outside jumper. Knotted up at 102 points with nine minutes left, the Thunder played rejuvenated in comparison to their first-half selves. Mara injected some youthful energy as a stop-and-board sequence had him thumping his chest in excitement. When Gilgeous-Alexander checked in, they actually had a three-point lead with eight minutes left — a great spot to be at.

Struggling in the clutch in recent outings, Gilgeous-Alexander finally came through. He drilled a stepback 3-pointer on Williamson's grill. After the latter had a couple of successful downhill drives to the rim, Mara's weak-side defense came up with a big-time stop.

Gilgeous-Alexander closed this out with an and-one driving layup. Meanwhile, the Pelicans' hot outside shooting fizzled out at the worst time possible. The Thunder ran away with this game with 34 points in the final frame. They live to see another day as the alternative of two straight home losses to similar-level teams would've continued uncomfortable conversations about this zombified group.

The Thunder shot 53% from the field and went 14-of-36 (38.9%) from 3. They shot 14-of-20 on free throws. They had 26 assists on 49 baskets. Four Thunder players scored double-digit points.

Gilgeous-Alexander led the way with 40 points and eight assists. Mara had a monstrous 22-point and 16-rebound double-double. McCain dropped 18 points and three rebounds. Stirtz helped with 18 points and two assists.

Meanwhile, the Pelicans shot 45% from the field and went 17-of-39 (43.6%) from 3. They shot 19-of-24 on free throws. Thet had 23 assists on 42 baskets. Six Pelicans players scored double-digit points.

Willamson had a throwback night with 22 points, seven assists and six rebounds. Fears also shone with 22 points, six rebounds and five assists. Bey surprised with 21 points and six rebounds. Trey Murphy III had 17 points and four rebounds in limited action. Derik Queen had 11 points and seven rebounds. Jones finished with 14 points and three assists.

Well, you'll get wins any way you can get them in this Post-D.C.C. (Dub, Chet, Caso) era. The Thunder can't get too picky on how they play out. At this point, it's just about raw survival. Is the defense still concerning? Yes. Is this form of winning a sustainable formula? Probably not. But you can worry about those problems another day. For now, Gilgeous-Alexander and friends did just enough to squeeze by the Pelicans.

That's what these two months are about. And hey — if Mara and Stirtz continue on their current trajectories — maybe this stretch of ugly basketball will be remembered fondly as their respective breakouts. The Thunder badly need an injection of youth and fresh faces. This is one of the few ripple effects of OKC's apocalyptic injury wave that happened a month ago.

Dancing with Williamson, Gilgeous-Alexander had that killer look in his eye. He wanted to add to his clutch-moments catalog — which has seldom seen clips added recently. He finally did so. Going with the patented stepback 3-pointer, he drilled the jumper despite the fellow All-Star player's textbook contest.

Gilgeous-Alexander finished with 40 points on 14-of-24 shooting, eight assists and three rebounds. He shot 4-of-7 from 3 and went 8-of-11 on free throws. He also had one block.

The one-man show continues. Gilgeous-Alexander has fully embraced the task required to keep the Thunder alive. The two-time MVP has dropped video-game scoring numbers after video-game scoring numbers. This time, he complemented the meaty box-score stat line with a win and fourth-quarter highlights. It was a textbook super-efficient 40-ball that one of the greatest players has normalized throughout the decade.

Back to the formula for Gilgeous-Alexander. After adventuring out to the perimeter to start the season, he's returned to his within-the-perimeter bag. You can't outdo the Big Mac sauce at McDonald's. He completely dissected and destroyed New Orleans' nonexistent defense. It's funny. It has a ton of defense-first names on paper. But for whatever reason, it can't actually gel on the floor.

Gilgeous-Alexander exposed that hooping oxymoron. He drilled a bucket of mid-range jumpers. His outside shot even fell for him. And the drives to the rim featured pure shot-making artistry. It was also another game where the free-throw attempts reached double digits. Everything is aligning for him to have another super-efficient 30-plus point campaign for a historic fifth consecutive year.

As the rest of the Thunder needed a half to get going, Gilgeous-Alexander kept them at bay. He had 19 points in the first half. Even when everybody else discovered a second-half groove, his scoring onslaught continued. He finally played closer with nine points in the final frame. The Pelicans didn't have a counterpunch as several role players took turns jacking up outside shots.

Been discussed ad nauseam, but this is what the Thunder will need out of Gilgeous-Alexander if it wants a puncher's chance most nights. That is, until Chet Holmgren returns. Against a disoriented Pelicans, having a 40-burger felt like a given. He's back to having those on a regular basis. The NBA superstar has done his fair share of carrying jobs this year. This was the latest example.

Winning the easy jump ball over Fears, Mara rolled to the rim. Kenrich Williams played a quick game of catch. The 21-year-old's trust in his veteran teammate's savviness paid off. He had a wide-open dunk to give the Thunder a high-leverage bucket in the final 90 seconds.

Mara finished with 22 points on 11-of-15 shooting, 16 rebounds and two assists. He also had two blocks.

With each passing game, Mara has seen his NBA confidence balloon. Alongside that, his play as well. This was the peak of the first month of his rookie year. He notched a double-double that has never happened before in Thunder history. The 7-foot-3 giant was a one-man dunk machine as nobody on the Pelicans' frontcourt dared to meet him at the rim.

The Thunder didn't take long to realize how easy it was going to be to slice up the Pelicans' paint defense. Isaiah Hartenstein connected on Mara for a couple of easy dunks to get things going. The latter had a dozen points in the first half. It wasn't just all spoon-fed stuff either, though. He showcased his old-school-esque footwork in the post. Each time he scored, his confidence oozed through the TV screen.

Patient in the post, Mara has mastered the art of the pump-fake and pass-fake. He didn't even need to depend on his raw strength and size to get the best of his defenders. Looking like prime Al Jefferson, the 21-year-old was a scientist in the paint. It was all finesse as he feathered in a few layups.

On the boards, Mara was a monster. On both ends. Remember when that was a concern in the NBA draft process? What makes this even more impressive is that the Pelicans are filled with plenty of talented frontcourt players. As the Thunder try to stitch together a decent NBA offense, his second-chance looks and putbacks have proven to be a lifeblood.

I mean, Mara has completely salvaged OKC's year. I don't think it's an overreaction to say that. Peaks like tonight write for themselves. But he's low-key had a steady floor game. There's a reason why the Thunder have settled on the Mara-Hartenstein frontcourt look for their starting lineup in this stretch. It's been a minute since OKC has seen fresh faces help at this level. Incredibly timely for it to happen right now.

Getting a one-on-one look with Williamson, Stirtz went to work. He shuffled his feet toward the baseline. The 23-year-old boasted his self-creation talent as he drilled a mid-range jumper. Also knocking down his catch-and-shoot looks, this was a sneak peek of his best-case scenario.

Stirtz finished with 18 points on 7-of-10 shooting, two assists and two rebounds. He shot 4-of-6 from 3. He also had one steal.

Since the Post-D.C.C. era started, the Thunder desperately needed complementary scorers. Stirtz had fit the bill on paper. But this was one of the early times that he materialized that over to the court. Considering how short his leash had been this year, OKC decided to let him get creative and gain confidence as a bucket-getter.

The result? Stirtz's best game of the year. He had a couple of straight-line drives to the rim. But most of his buckets happened in the jumper-variety. The outside shot fell for him. He drained his first high-leverage outside shot of his career in the final four minutes of this contest to put some distance on the scoreboard.

Like Mara, Stirtz has been a contributor from the jump — albeit on rockier waters and in a smaller role. But the Thunder are hoping he can eventually enter a flow state as a scorer. Right now, he's still adjusting to the NBA. To the point that OKC hinted at wanting him to play much more within the flow instead of overthinking things. This tape should be their top example of why they believe that's for the best for him.

Pump-faking from the outside, McCain took Bey to the cup. A step-through move helped him get his first bucket of a banked layup. That was enough to turn into a microwave scorer. He swished a deep catch-and-shoot outside jumper soon after. Then did the same a couple of minutes later to open the second half.

McCain finished with 18 points on 7-of-14 shooting and three rebounds. He shot 3-of-9 from 3 and went 1-of-2 on free throws. He also had one steal and one block.

The most Jekyll-and-Hyde player on the Thunder had the most Jekyll-and-Hyde performance. First half? Zero points. Scoreless. Second half? A full 18. As OKC scored in a flurry, he aided with his sizzling shot-making. He had a dozen points in the third quarter. That type of scoring run is what makes him such a fan favorite and tantalizing prospect.

The outside jumper finally fell. Most importantly, though, McCain put up outside shot attempts. That has surprisingly been a struggle this year. Too often, he's settled for mid-range looks. But if he's going to thrive in the NBA, he needs to take most of his attempts from deep. And this game is why as he turned it around with a surging second half.

Sure, McCain's scoreless halves happen too often. His highs and lows are too volatile and badly need to center his consistency. But he salvaged this outing as he helped the Thunder get a comeback win. Until help arrives, they're going to continue to rely on his hot-or-cold scoring game. That's really one of the few options they have sans Gilgeous-Alexander.

This article originally appeared on OKC Thunder Wire: Player grades: SGA's 40 points lead Thunder to 126-120 win over Pelicans

#2783. If this is the end, it’s a disappointing one

If Puka Nacua has played his final game as a member of the Los Angeles Rams, it's a disappointing end for fans.

Nacua elected to have what seems to be season-ending surgery to repair his knee injury in a contract year. The Rams will likely regret not extending his contract before the year started, as his lack of long-term security likely factored into the business decision. If Nacua had future assurances, he might have put off surgery in order to make a comeback towards the late regular season into the playoffs.

And to make matters worse, Nacua suffered said injury on a meaningless play. Sean McVay called a receiver run to salt away an insurmountable lead against the Washington Commanders. It didn't make sense at the time. It felt even worse once the injury news broke. Somehow, it seems even more nonsensical now.

Nacua's production through his first four NFL seasons—when healthy—rivals that of former top draft picks in Odell Beckham, Justin Jefferson, and Ja'Marr Chase. The key difference: Nacua was the Rams' third draft pick in the fifth round back in 2023. He went on to record the most yards by a rookie receiver and he's second to only Jefferson through three seasons.

This is the kind of found money that should set a franchise up for years. Instead, the Rams might have wasted their opportunity.

Despite what looks to be a large amount of available salary cap space heading into next offseason, Los Angeles has a handful of difficult decisions to make—Nacua included. They will also decide whether Kobie Turner, Byron Young, Steve Avila, Kevin Dotson, Coleman Shelton, Warren McClendon, Colby Parkinson, and others over the next couple of years. That money is reserved for taking care of their own. If they don't, the cupboard will be left so bare that the Rams risk falling back into irrelevance.

And separately there is the issue of how the Rams have used Nacua.

The team has failed to properly stack the receiving corps around their star pass catcher. Often times over the last few seasons it has seemed like their passing game was simply Nacua or bust. As a result, he's handled a historically large workload. That has taken a toll on his body especially with the physical nature in which he approaches the game.

That could be the reason why the Rams are hesitant to invest in Nacua long-term, which would be another sign that they failed to protect the luxury of their 2023 draft day steal.

If this is the end for Nacua with the Rams, it's an incredibly discouraging way to separate. The team has turned a historic advantage into a few years of great production with little to show for it thereafter.
